--- Page 1 ---
<meta>The following text is a translation of a Latin ecclesiastical legal document, likely from the registers of Pope Gregory the Great (c. 590–604 AD). It continues a discussion regarding the wrongful deposition of Bishop Stephen of Malta and the legal procedures required for his reinstatement.</meta>

### Folio CXXXV.

If the mentioned bishops have freely confessed that they acted out of fear of the judge and did such things, the time of their penance must be shortened for them, and the manner of penance should be tempered. If, however, he who usurped his place has perhaps departed from this light <note>A common Latin metaphor for death: *de hac luce migrauit*</note> and another has been ordained: because the fault seems lighter—not as if the office succeeded to this survivor, but to the deceased—the office of the episcopacy shall be forbidden to him in that same place; however, if he should be elected to another church that is vacant of a priest, he may be a bishop. Nevertheless, he is never to return in any way to the Church of Malta <note>original: *milicitanam*, referring to the Diocese of Malta</note>. 

Furthermore, whatever the aforementioned bishop is proven to have spent or suffered as a loss through violence and persecution, let the Glorious Count <note>original: *comicolus*, a variant of *comitiolus*, referring to a high-ranking official or "comes"</note> be condemned to restore to that same bishop, upon his oath. But if it is perhaps claimed that the matter was conducted differently than the petition of the aforementioned bishop contains, it must be investigated with subtlety; and once the truth is known with the fear of God, judgment must be made as the order of justice shall persuade.

Because, therefore, Bishop Stephen has claimed that certain things were forged against him out of hatred and that he was accused on false charges, and that nothing was conducted in an orderly fashion—it must be determined if the accusers and witnesses were different people, or the nature of the case, and if it was worthy of exile or deposition <term>deposition|The formal removal of a clergyman from his office and status</term>; or if testimony was spoken against him under oath in his presence, or conducted in writing; or if he himself had the opportunity of responding and defending himself. 

But also concerning the persons of those accusing and testifying, it must be subtly inquired into: of what rank and of what reputation they are; or lest they be destitute, or perhaps had some enmities against the aforementioned bishop. And whether they gave testimony from hearsay, or certainly testified that they knew it specifically; and if it was indicated in writing, it must be recited with the parties present.

But if by chance these things were not solemnly performed, nor was the cause proven to be worthy of exile or deposition, he shall be recalled to his church by all means. Those, however, who condemned him against the fear of God and the statutes of the canons <term>canons|Ecclesiastical laws or rules established by church councils</term>, being excommunicated, are to be sent into a monastery to perform penance for six months; provided, of course, that if the danger of death should happen to be imminent for any of them, the blessing of the viaticum <term>viaticum|The Eucharist given to a person who is dying, literally "provisions for the journey"</term> shall not be denied to him. 

He himself, however, who rashly coveted his place while he was still living, being deprived of the priesthood, shall be repelled from all ecclesiastical ministry, and shall be handed over to the same most beloved brother and our fellow bishop, so that he may either send him to us or keep him in custody with himself. For the bishops who presumed to ordain him, or perversely provided their consent to his ordination, being deprived of communion, shall be assigned to a monastery for five months to perform penance.

<summary>This page outlines the legal ruling and disciplinary measures following the wrongful deposition of Bishop Stephen of Malta, detailing the requirements for a fair trial and the penance for those who illegally usurped his see.</summary>
<keywords>Bishop Stephen, Church of Malta, canon law, deposition, penance, viaticum, ecclesiastical justice, monastery</keywords>

## Instruction in Measurement with the Compass and Straight-
## edge, in lines, planes, and solid bodies <note>original: "gantzen corporen," referring to three-dimensional geometric solids</note>,
## compiled by Albrecht Dürer,
## and for the use of all those who love art,
## brought into print with the corresponding illustrations
## in the year
## 1525.

--- Page 6 ---
<meta>This page is the formal dedication of Albrecht Dürer’s treatise on geometry. It follows the title page, which established the book's purpose as a manual for measurement. This dedication is addressed to his lifelong friend, the humanist scholar Willibald Pirckheimer.</meta>

<margin>WP <note>The monogram of the dedicatee, Willibald Pirckheimer.</note></margin>
<margin>AD <note>The famous monogram of the artist, Albrecht Dürer.</note></margin>

# To a particularly dear lord and friend, Lord
## Willibald Pirckheimer, I, Albrecht Dürer, wish health and salvation.

Favorable lord and friend, until now in our German lands, many talented young men have been set to the art of painting <note>original: "mallerey"</note> who have been taught without any foundation and solely through daily practice. They have thus grown up in ignorance like a wild, unpruned tree. Although some of them have attained a free hand through steady practice, so that they produce their works with power, they do so thoughtlessly and only according to their own liking.

However, when sensible painters and true artists have seen such unconsidered work, they have—and not without reason—laughed at the blindness of these people; for to a true understanding, nothing is more unpleasant to see than falsehood in a painting, regardless of whether it was painted with great diligence. The only reason that such painters took pleasure in their errors was that they had not learned the art of measurement <note>original: "künst der messung," referring here to geometry and perspective</note>, without which no one can be or become a proper craftsman. This was the fault of their masters, who themselves did not know such an art.

Because this is the true foundation of all painting, I have resolved to provide a beginning for all art-loving youth and to give them a reason to take up the compass and straightedge. Through this, they may recognize and see before their eyes the true truth, so that they may not only become eager for the arts, but also arrive at a proper and greater understanding.

This is despite the fact that now, in our times, the art of painting is being greatly despised by some who say it serves idolatry <note>original: "Abgötterey"</note>. Yet every Christian human is as little drawn to a superstition by a painting or image as a pious man is drawn to a murder because he carries a weapon at his side. He would truly have to be an senseless person who would want to worship a painting, wood, or stone. Therefore, painting brings more improvement than offense, provided it is honorable, artistic, and well-made.

In what honor and dignity this art was held among the Greeks and Romans is sufficiently shown in the ancient books. Though it was subsequently lost and hidden for over a thousand years, it was only brought back to light within the last two hundred years by the Italians <note>original: "Walhen," a term Dürer uses for Italians, who had begun the Renaissance revival of classical art and perspective</note>. For arts are very easily lost, but only with difficulty and over a long time are they rediscovered.

Therefore, I hope that no sensible person will criticize this undertaking and instruction of mine, as it is done with good intentions and for the benefit of all who love art—and not only for painters, but also for goldsmiths, sculptors, stonemasons, carpenters, and all those who use measurement. No one is forced to use this teaching of mine; but I well know that whoever undertakes it will not only grasp a fundamental beginning from it, but through daily use will reach a greater understanding, seeking further and discovering far more than I am now showing.

Since I know, favorable lord and friend, that you are a lover of all the arts, I have dedicated this little book to you out of special affection and friendly will. It is not because I thought I was showing you something great or excellent, but so that you might understand and measure my inclined and good will toward you. Even if I cannot be particularly useful to you with my works, my spirit is nonetheless always ready to repay your favor and the love you bear for me with equal return.

<summary>In this dedication to Willibald Pirckheimer, Dürer argues that German art lacks the theoretical foundation of geometry found in Italian and Classical traditions. He defends the practice of art against Reformation-era charges of idolatry, asserting that geometry is an essential tool for all craftsmen.</summary>
<keywords>Albrecht Dürer, Willibald Pirckheimer, Geometry, Art Theory, German Renaissance, Iconoclasm, Perspective, Education, Craftsmanship</keywords>

--- Page 7 ---
<meta>Albrecht Dürer continues his introduction to "The Manual of Measurement." Having dedicated the work to Willibald Pirckheimer in the previous section, he now addresses the reader directly to define the most basic elements of geometry—the point and the line—following the classical traditions of Euclid but adapted for the practical needs of artists and craftsmen.</meta>

<margin>A ij <note>This is a "signature mark," used by early printers to help bookbinders assemble the pages in the correct order.</note></margin>

# The most ingenious Euclid / has composed the foundation
## of geometry <note>original: "Geometria"</note>. Those who understand him well / will have no
## need of these things written hereafter / for they are written
## only for the young and for those who have no one else
## to faithfully instruct them.

To begin with / it is necessary that those who wish to teach the young / to measure / should know / what the foundation is from which one measures / and how the measuring is done / whether it be a new thought / or a pre-made thing. Three kinds of things are to be measured / First=
ly / a length / which has neither width nor thickness / Thereafter a length which has width / Third=
ly / a length / which has both width and thickness. The beginning and end of all these things are point=
s / But a point is such a thing / that has neither Size, Length, Width, nor Thickness / And yet it is a
beginning and end / of all physical things / that can be made / or that we can imagine in our minds
/ Just as the highly learned / in this art well know / and therefore a point fills
no space / for it is indivisible / and yet it may be placed in all ends
or places / through our senses or thoughts / For I may in my mind cast a point high into the air / or drop it into the deeps / where I cannot reach with my body. But so that the young may be understanding in practical
work / I will present the point to them as a picture with a dot / from a pen /
And write the word "point" next to it / so that the point is signified. point / . When now th=
is point / is drawn from its first beginning / to another end / it is called a Line / and
this Line is a length / without any thickness and width / and may be drawn as long as one wishes . This
Line I will draw here opposite with the pen as a straight stroke / and write the name "Line"
upon it / <image-desc>A horizontal straight line labeled "Line" above and "1" below</image-desc> So that the invisible line / may be understood in the mind through the straight stroke / For in such a way must the inner=
most understanding be shown in the outward work / Therefore I will draw all things / that I describe in this little book
/ alongside the text / so that my presentation / may provide the young with an image before their eyes
/ and they may grasp it all the better. Now it is to be noted / that lines may be drawn in many ways
/ and especially there are three kinds of lines / from which much can be made / Firstly there is a straight
Line / Secondly the Circular Line / thereafter there is yet a crooked Line / which may be drawn by hand / or
from point to point / as some arts show / through which many
variations come / But this crooked line / I know not how better to name / than a Serpentine Line <note>original: "Schlangen Lini," literally "snake line," referring to a free-form or S-curve.</note> /
because it may be drawn here and there / as one wishes / For a clear understanding of this / I have
drawn them here below and written their names upon each.

-> A straight Line / &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; A circular Line / &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; A Serpentine Line / <-

<image-desc>Geometric diagrams showing three types of lines: a straight vertical line, a circle with a central point, and a large "S"-shaped serpentine line. Between the first and second figure is a small "z"-shaped mark.</image-desc>

<vocab>Euclid, Geometry, point, Line, Circular Line, Serpentine Line</vocab>

<summary>Dürer introduces the fundamental elements of geometry, defining the point as a dimensionless entity and the line as the movement of a point, while categorizing lines into straight, circular, and serpentine types for the student.</summary>

--- Page 8 ---
<meta>Albrecht Dürer continues his geometric primer. On the previous page, he defined the mathematical point. Here, he expands on the nature of the line—the second dimension—and introduces the concept of the plane, while providing practical German terminology for classical Latin geometric concepts.</meta>

It is to be noted that these three aforementioned lines can be drawn short or long. And if it were possible to achieve, and time did not hinder it, the straight line could be drawn out forever, or at least be imagined as such. It is also to be used in three ways: upright <note>vertical</note>, across <note>horizontal</note>, and diagonally <note>original: "vber ort"</note>.

But the circular line <note>original: "zirkellini"</note> may be used in whole or in part, and it cannot be drawn longer than from the start back to where it began; if it should proceed further, it simply returns to the previous orbit. This circular line may be made large or small. However, if one makes it rise or fall, it becomes a **snake line** <note>original: "Schlangen lini"; here Dürer refers to a spiral or helix</note>. Now, the snake line is infinitely changeable, from which one may create wonderful things, whether in length, width, height, or depth. And as it is certain that one can make many strange things with just one line—of which those who do not reflect upon it know nothing, and which is little mentioned here—one can well imagine what might be accomplished with two, three, or many lines. This is especially true when the three different kinds of lines are used together with all their variations. For there are many lines to be made that cannot be drawn without the help of other lines.

It is also necessary to know what **parallel lines** are, so called in Latin <note>original: "Paralell lini"</note>, which I will call "pair lines" <note>original: "parr Lini"</note> in our German. These are such lines that always run at an equal distance from one another, whether they are drawn straight, snakelike, or in a circular fashion. One should also know that where two lines do not run equally with each other, they will eventually meet and form an acute angle. 

Therefore, two **perpendiculars** <note>original: "Perpendicular"</note> next to each other—which are two **plumb lines** <note>original: "bleyschnür"; weighted cords used by builders to find a vertical line</note> hanging side-by-side—do not yield two perfectly upright pair lines <note>parallels</note>. For they run together at the center point of the earth and form an acute angle. All lines do this if they do not run equally with one another: either they eventually meet, or they run steadily further apart, as mentioned before. However, the reason that upright vertical lines are used as "pair lines" <note>parallels</note> is that they would have to travel as far as the center of the earth to meet; therefore, to our senses and our sight, the convergence is and remains unnoticeable. I have drawn these types of pair and level lines here below: straight, snakelike, circular, and horizontal.

->Straight pair lines / Circular pair lines / Snakelike pair lines / Horizontal lines<-

<image-desc>A horizontal series of four geometric diagrams. From left to right: 1. Two vertical parallel straight lines. 2. Two concentric semi-circular lines with the number 3 between them. 3. Two parallel undulating S-shaped lines. 4. Two converging vertical lines meeting at a small circle at the bottom (representing the center of the earth), with various letters and a central mark.</image-desc>

Now that one has understood a little of what lines are and their differences, I will speak further of that length which also has a width, which always accompanies it, whether it be drawn straight or curved. This is called a **plane** <note>original: "Planum" in Latin</note>, but in German I know no other name for it than **a flat surface** <note>original: "Ebne"</note>, although there are many variations within it, as follows hereafter. A plane could be imagined that has no end on any side; however, we will not deal with that here. We speak only of those that have a beginning and an end, and which can be enclosed by lines so that they take on a shape. There are many kinds of these, some of which I will show here. The first plane is entirely even, such that it is neither high nor low nor curved. Secondly, there is...

<summary>Dürer defines parallel lines (which he calls "pair lines"), spirals, and the concept of a geometric plane. He famously notes that plumb lines are not truly parallel because they converge at the Earth's center, though they appear parallel to the human eye.</summary>
<keywords>parallel lines, pair lines, snake line, spiral, plumb lines, center of the earth, plane, geometry, Albrecht Dürer</keywords>

--- Page 9 ---
<meta>Albrecht Dürer continues his geometric instruction, moving from the one-dimensional line (discussed on the previous page) to the two-dimensional plane and finally the three-dimensional solid body.</meta>

<sig>A iij</sig>

...a rounded plane, like a half-sphere. Thirdly, there is a hollow plane, like a round cauldron. Fourthly, there is a bulging plane <note>original: "beülette ebnen"</note>, high in some places and low in others. There are also planes like a broad ring, curved inward and outward in various ways; for all these things should and may be used for practical benefit in one's work. Where this is not done, one racks one’s brain in vain. First, I will enclose the first true plane within a proper square. To do this, I draw a horizontal line, **a. b.**; I then move this line straight downwards as far as it is long, and thus a squared plane is created. But a round, flat plane I make in this way: I draw a straight line, **a. b.**, and hold it still at point **a**. But with the end **b**, I travel around from the beginning until I come back to the start. Thus, the end **b** describes a round segment, and the point **a** remains a center point. From this point **a**, it is everywhere equidistant to all ends of the round plane. I have drawn these below.

A squared plane, whole sheet | A round plane | A spherical plane | A hollow plane | A curved plane
->A bulging plane<-

<image-desc>A horizontal row of six diagrams illustrating different geometric planes. From left to right: a rectangle labeled with 'a' and 'b' at the corners; a circle with a radius line from center 'a' to circumference 'b'; a convex shaded hemisphere; a concave shaded bowl shape; a curved rectangular plane; and an irregular, undulating or "bumpy" plane. A numeral '4' is placed between the circle and the hemisphere.</image-desc>

SO, now that the length and breadth—which is the plane—have been discussed a little to explain what they are, I will now say what length and breadth together with thickness are: these are the <term>Bodies</term> <note>original: "Corpora"</note>. From these, I will demonstrate and teach how several may be made. First, take the previously made square plane **a. b. b. a.** and move it straight upwards as high as it is wide; thus a proper squared **cube** <note>original: "würffel"</note> is made from it, with equal sides, equal planes, and equal angles. After that, take the previously made round plane and draw a line straight out from the center point **a** to the edge of the circle; there place a **c**, so that **c. a. b.** is a straight line. On one side outside the round plane place a **d**, and on the other side an **e**. But let **c. a. b.** be the axis upon which this plane must be turned, from **d** to **e**. Thus, this round circuit describes a completely round **sphere** <note>original: "kugel"</note>, which from the outside is everywhere equidistant to its center point **a**. Yet, in the turning, the axis must remain stationary at points **c** and **b**. Thus you have two perfect Bodies. But there is no body more perfect, being more equal in all parts, than a sphere. I have drawn these two Bodies here.

->A squared Body or cube<- ->A round ball or sphere<-

<image-desc>Two diagrams illustrating three-dimensional geometric solids. On the left is a cube in perspective, with its vertices and edges labeled with 'a' and 'b'. On the right is a sphere shown with a vertical axis labeled 'c' at the top, 'a' in the center, and 'b' at the bottom, with points 'd' and 'e' marking the horizontal extrema. A numeral '5' is placed between the two figures.</image-desc>

<summary>This page defines various types of planes (convex, concave, and irregular) and demonstrates how to progress from two-dimensional surfaces to three-dimensional solids like the cube and the sphere.</summary>
<keywords>geometry, planes, surfaces, solids, cube, sphere, Albrecht Dürer, measurement</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>Continuing from the previous page, Albrecht Dürer shifts from defining types of planes to the practical application of geometry: drawing specific complex lines—specifically spirals—on those planes.</meta>

SINCE it has now been shown what a line, a breadth or plane, and a <term>solid body</term> <note>original: "Corpus"</note>—that is, a physical object—are, one must also know that such things, whether they be large or small, may be measured through art; for measurement reaches both the far and the near. Now I will first begin at the front again and will draw several measured lines which are useful for various works. It is well known that from a single line many forms can be drawn and shown in a drafting.

But first, I want to draw a <term>spiral line</term> <note>original: "schnecken lini," literally "snail-line"</note> with the <term>compass</term> <note>original: "zirckel"</note> on a plane; for the <term>plane</term> <note>original: "planus"</note> must constantly be used, whether for the sake of the line or the body. I draft this spiral line thus: I make an upright line, let it be **a.** at the top and **b.** at the bottom. I divide it with three points, **c., d., e.**, into four equal spaces. Then I divide **d. e.** with a point **f.** into two equal spaces. Then I place a **g.** on the right side of the line and an **h.** on the left. Then I take a compass and place one foot on point **d.** and the other on point **a.** and draw toward side **h.** down to point **b.** Then I take the compass and place one foot on point **f.** and the other on point **c.** and draw toward side **g.** until <meta>crossed out word</meta> <s>below</s> point **b.** Then I take the compass again, place one foot on point **d.** and draw toward side **h.** with the other foot from point **c.** to point **e.** Then I place the compass with one foot on point **f.** and the other on point **d.** and draw from there toward side **g.** to point **e.** Then I place the compass on line **a. b.** with one foot halfway between **d.** and **f.**, and place the other foot on point **d.** and draw from there toward side **h.** to point **f.** Thus this line is finished; and it is useful for many things, and among others, it is useful for a <term>volute</term> <note>original: "horn eissen," likely referring to the scroll-work or "horns" of a capital</note> on a <term>capital</term> <note>original: "capitel"</note>. And to understand this better, I have drafted two straight horizontal lines here below, from the two points **a.** and **c.**, and drawn back from the spiral line.

->This spiral line is drawn with the compass.<-

<image-desc>A geometric diagram illustrating the construction of a spiral (Schneckenlini). A central vertical line is labeled with 'a' at the top and 'B' at the bottom, containing several small construction points. A spiral line expands from the center, created from connected circular arcs. A larger enclosing circle contains the spiral, with numbers 1 through 6 marked on the left side and various construction letters (c, d, e, f, g, h) placed near the central axis.</image-desc>

NOW I will draw another spiral line in another way, which is useful in many things and very beneficial; much is learned from it as well. It can also be used in place of the previous line. I take its beginning from the <term>center point</term> <note>original: "mittel punckten"</note>; from there its length goes into the distance as far as one wishes. Yet the space between the overlapping lines always remains equally wide, except in the first revolution. But I make this spiral line thus: I set a point **a.** and draw a circle around it as far as I want the spiral line to run. And I divide this circular line with 12 points into 12 equal spaces. Then I draw a straight line upward from the <term>center</term> <note>original: "Centro"</note> **a.** to the circular line, the end of which is **b.** At this same point I place 12, and I begin to count the divisions of the points on the circular line toward the left hand: 1, 2, 3, etc., until I come back around to 12. But the straight line **a. b.** I divide with 23 points into 24 equal spaces and begin counting at **a.**: 1, 2, 3, etc. Then I take a straight <term>ruler</term> <note>original: "richtscheyt"</note> and mark the points of the aforementioned line **a. b.** upon it and label them with their numbers; and I place the end with the lower number **a.** on the center **a.**, and the end **b.** on the circular line at point 1; and where the ruler then points with its point 1...

<summary>Albrecht Dürer explains the practical geometry of drawing spirals (Schneckenlinien), providing step-by-step instructions for two different types: one constructed from circular arcs (used for architectural capitals) and another with equal spacing between its coils.</summary>
<keywords>spiral, geometry, compass, architectural capital, volute, measurement, drafting, Archimedean spiral, Albrecht Dürer</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>Continuing the instructions for drawing a spiral from the previous page, Albrecht Dürer explains how to map the specific points of the curve by using a rotating straightedge and a numbered circle as a guide.</meta>

There I also place a point **1**. Thus I continue all the way around to all the numbers in the <term>circle</term> <note>original: "zirckelryß"</note> and always keep the <term>straightedge</term> <note>original: "richtscheyt"</note> fixed in the **Center a.**; thus the points of the straightedge will indicate all the points of the <term>spiral line</term> <note>original: "schneckenlini," literally "snail-line"</note> through the number where one should place them. Therefore, pay close attention to the numbers so you do not go astray. But when the line runs over itself twice, and there are now **12** numbers in the circle, but **23** on the rotating straightedge, take care that the numbers of the straightedge proceed in order; for to the number **1** comes **13**, to **2** comes **14**, **3** to **15**, **4** to **16**, **5** to **17**, **6** to **18**, **7** to **19**, **8** to **20**, **9** to **21**, **10** to **22**, and **11** to **23**. One may also draw this line many times over one another, whoever needs it; let him increase the numbers on the straightedge with the points, and leave the points in the circle unchanged. This spiral line is drafted nearby with all its figures. However, if one wishes to see and use this spiral line correctly, one must remove the circle and the dotted straightedge with all their numbers, by which the spiral line was made, and let only the spiral line with its points remain and stand <term>bare</term> <note>original: "ledig," meaning free of construction lines</note>. And how they should be drawn, I have thus drafted them twice hereafter as mentioned above. And in particular, I have added two straight lines to the bare spiral line and drawn them <term>horizontally</term> <note>original: "vberzwerch"</note> toward the left hand, at equal angles; the upper one from point **12** where the **b.** stands, but the lower from point **12** of the spiral line, so that one may see what difference it has compared to the first.

### The Spiral Line

<note>A geometrical diagram illustrating the construction of a spiral. A large circle is marked with points 1 through 12 around its circumference. A vertical line (the "straightedge") extending from the center "a" to the top point "12" is divided into 23 numbered intervals. A spiral curve originates at the center point "a" and winds outward, intersecting the rotating radius at points corresponding to the sequence 1 to 23, ending at point "b" at the top. The diagram shows the construction lines and the numerical mapping used to plot the curve.</note>

### The Bare Spiral Line

<note>A second version of the spiral diagram, shown "bare," meaning without the surrounding construction circle or the numbered ruler lines. The spiral curve itself is preserved, and from its endpoints at the top, two horizontal parallel lines extend to the left.</note>

<summary>Dürer describes a precise method for plotting a spiral by coordinating a rotating straightedge with a numbered circle, allowing for multiple revolutions. He provides both the construction diagram and the final "bare" curve to show how the geometry functions in practice.</summary>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for constructing geometric curves, specifically focusing on the "snail line" or spiral. It follows a page describing the initial setup of the circle and the division of the straightedge using equal segments. Here, the author introduces a method for creating a non-uniform spiral where the spacing between the lines changes as it curves.</meta>

Now I wish to change this previously made <term>spiral line</term> <note>original: "schneckenlini", literally "snail line"</note> once more using the dotted <term>straightedge</term> <note>original: "richtscheydt"</note> *a-b* from which the spiral is created. One must mark the dots differently than before; this is done using two kinds of lines—one curved and one straight—which are placed together so that one is measured by the other. Although they are unequal, they are made comparable through unequal parts. I do it thus: I draw an upright line as long as the straightedge I intend to use for the spiral; let the top be *b* and the bottom *a*. Then I draw a <term>transverse line</term> <note>original: "zwerch lini"</note> *c-d* so that the upright line stands upon it at point *a* at right angles. Next, I draw a straight <term>diagonal line</term> <note>original: "ortlini"</note> *d-b* and take a <term>compass</term> <note>original: "zirckel"</note>, placing one foot at point *d* and the other at point *a*, and draw a curve upward from there until it reaches the diagonal *d-b*. Where it touches, I place point *e*. 

Then I divide this curved line *a-e* with 23 points into 24 equal <term>fields</term> <note>original: "felt"; referring to the intervals between points</note> and draw straight lines from point *d* through every point on the curve *a-e* until they intersect the vertical line *a-b*. Wherever these lines cut through line *a-b*, I mark those points with numbers, beginning to count at the top below *b* with 1, 2, 3, 4, etc., down to *a*. From this, it becomes apparent how the fields between the points expand as they go upward and become narrower as they go down. I prick these points onto a straightedge that I will use during the work of rotating the spiral. Such a preparation I have drafted here below, and note especially that many things can be made from this technique which are not shown here. This spiral line does not run in parallel pairs over one another.

->The straightedge must be dotted according to this line *b-a* for the rotation of the spiral.<-

<image-desc>
A geometric diagram illustrating the division of a vertical line for constructing a spiral. A horizontal baseline contains points c, a, and d. A vertical line rises from point a to point b. A diagonal line connects points d and b. An arc is drawn from point a to point e on the diagonal line. Multiple lines radiate from point d through numbered points (1-24) on the arc, intersecting the vertical line ab at varying intervals. A stylized figure, resembling an "8" or infinity symbol, is positioned in the empty space between the vertical line and the diagonal.
</image-desc>

But if you wish to expand the fields even more on the outside of the spiral and make them narrower toward the inside, then lean the upright line *a-b* at the top at *b* toward point *c*. Then redraw the diagonal *d-b* accordingly; thus the compass arc *a-e* will become shorter. Afterward, divide everything anew as before, and you will find a great variation in the work; this is also partially shown in the drafting above. Now, when the modified line *a-b* with its points is pricked onto the straightedge, then draw a circular arc in which your straightedge touches the <term>center</term> <note>original: "Centrum"</note> with point *a* and the arc with point *b* at the top. Place the number 12 at the top at point *b* and proceed as was shown previously for the last spiral line. However, whereas before you moved outward from the center, now move from the outer arc inward. Therefore, place the sequence of points on the arc on the other side and begin counting 1, 2, 3, 4, etc. Thus you will see the difference compared to the previous line, as has been drafted twice here below according to the previous order, whereby you may see how one is more beautiful than the other.

## The altered spiral, plain <note>original: "ledig"; referring to the diagram stripped of its construction lines</note>

Furthermore, if you wish to place straight lines upon a spiral at all the numbered points, which follow one another in order, do it thus: take a <term>straightedge</term> <note>original: "richtscheynt"</note> and let one end of it touch the <term>center</term> <note>original: "centro"</note> *a*, and place the other part on point 12. From there, draw a straight line outward along the straightedge. In this manner, keep one part of the straightedge constantly still at the center *a*, but with the other part, move all the way around to all the points of the spiral—such as 1, 2, 3, 4, etc.—and draw straight lines outward until you come back around to the center *a*. This you see <term>drawn</term> <note>original: "geryssen"; often implying a precise draft or incision</note> below.

But to find the length of each straight line that is placed upon the **spiral** <note>original: "Schnecken", literally "snail"</note> in proper order, do as follows: Take a <term>compass</term> <note>original: "zirckel"</note>; set one foot in point 12 and the other in point 1, and from there draw a curve upward. Thereafter, set the foot of the compass in point 1 and the other in point 12; from there, also draw a curve upward. Where the two circular marks intersect one another, set a point **c**. Do the same between all the points of the numbers in the spiral line, such as between 1 and 2, and 2 and 3, etc., and mark the intersections of the circular curves one after another at the top, as **d, e, f, g**, etc., through the whole alphabet, as far as it reaches. Then, if you draw straight lines together—**c** to **d**, and **d** to **e**, and **f** to **g**, etc., through the entire revolution of all the letters—they will cut off for you the straight lines that are drawn from the points 1, 2, 3, 4, etc., and through all the other numbers. If, however, you wish to properly divide the **leaves** <note>original: "bletter"; referring to the petal-like shapes formed by the intersecting arcs</note> that have been created by the circular curves with a center line, then first draw straight lines from point **c**, and thereafter from points **d, e, f, g**, etc., toward the **center** <note>original: "Centro"</note> **a**, right up to the spiral line. Thus, these things come about in an orderly fashion, as you see drafted here below.

->How the straight lines on the spiral should be ordered by length.<-

<note>A geometric construction of a spiral (*Schneckenlinie*). The diagram shows a central point 'a' with radial lines numbered 1 through 17 extending outwards. Henceforth, I will make the spiral simple and lead it from the **circular line** <note>original: "Cirkelryß"</note> into the center **a**, and again place leaves upon it. But the straight lines that are drawn through the leaves stand differently than the previously presented lines. First, draw a circular curve from the center **a**, and mark it with numbers as before. But the upright line **a-b** with which you move around, divide that with 11 points into 12 equal fields. Then travel around again, as you were previously instructed, and mark the spiral line until it reaches the center **a**. Thus is this line made, and it is useful for many things; in particular, it is also to be used for a **bishop’s staff** <note>original: "Bischoffstab"; a ceremonial crosier, which traditionally ends in a decorative spiral</note>. To do this, one must proceed as follows: Draw a straight line downward from the circular curve out of point 6, and use the half-circle with the larger numbers together with the spiral line. But leave out the other half-circle with the smaller numbers. Thereafter, take a compass and set it with the...

## 14

Place one foot of the <term>compass</term> <note>original: *zirckel*</note> on the <term>circle-stroke</term> <note>original: *zirckelriß*; the construction line drawn with a compass.</note> at point 9, and with the other foot at point 7. And draw from there roundly outwards a round <term>circle-segment</term> <note>original: *zirckeltrum*; a portion of a circle's circumference.</note>. Then place one foot at point 7 and draw with the other foot out from point 9. And where the two curved lines meet, there place a point **c.** And draw a straight line from the circle-line at point 8 to point **c.** Do the same also between the two points 9 and 11, and place a **d** at the top where the curves meet.

Thereafter, set one foot of the compass on the circle-stroke at point 11, and the other foot on the <term>spiral</term> <note>original: *schneckenlini*; literally "snail-line," a common term for a volute or spiral in 16th-century geometry.</note> at point 1, and draw from there upwards. Then again place one foot at the aforementioned point 1 and the other at point 11, and draw from there until the curved lines meet; there place an **e.** Do the same thereafter in the same way on the spiral between points 1 and 2, and 3 and 5, and 5 and 7, and 7 and 9, and 9 and 11, and mark their closures at the top, one after another: **f. g. h. i. k.**

Thereafter, draw the lines for the leaves upon the spiral with straight strokes: **e. 1** <note>The transcription reads "e. 12," but based on the sequence of numbering and the diagram, this likely refers to point 1.</note>, **f. 2, g. 4, h. 6, i. 8, k. 10.** Thereafter, there remains a segment at the top between 11 and the **Center .a.**; draw that to the point **a** to complete the innermost curve.

<summary>This page provides geometric instructions for drafting two specific ornaments: the basic curve of a bishop's staff and a complex foliage boss constructed through a series of overlapping compass arcs.</summary>
<keywords>geometry, Albrecht Dürer, spiral, volute, bishop's staff, crozier, foliage boss, ornament, architectural drawing, compass construction</keywords>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>Following the previous page's discussion on decorative foliate spirals (like those on a bishop’s staff), Dürer here explains the mathematical principles for constructing spirals—both flat and projected into three-dimensional space for architectural use.</meta>

It is to be noted that the circle-line <note>original: *zirckelryß*; the circumference or path of the compass</note> through which a snail-line <note>original: *Schneckenlini*; Dürer’s term for a spiral</note> is drawn, may be divided into as many points and parts as one wishes. Indeed, the more parts into which it is divided, the more accurately it can be measured. In however many parts you divide the circle-line, you must always divide your straightedge <note>original: *richtscheyt*; a ruler or scale</note> into that same number of parts from which you wish to make a simple snail-line.

But if you wish to make it double, triple, or quadruple, etc., then multiply the points and numbers on your straightedge twofold, threefold, or fourfold, and then follow the rotation around again; thus you will find what you desire. However, if you worry that by multiplying the points on your straightedge and rotating around the circle-line, you might become confused by the unequal numbers that occur and run over one another, then do it this way: 

When you have divided the circle-line—I will set it here at 12 parts—and you then want your snail-line to run around twofold, threefold, or fourfold, then divide your straightedge into as many points as you like and set the figures <note>numbers</note> thereto: 1, 2, 3, etc., up to 12. After that, begin again to count 1, 2, 3, up to 12; then do it again, and yet again. And align your straightedge with the figures so that the numbers always correspond between the circle-line and the straightedge with which you rotate; in this way, you cannot go wrong. 

Now that the snail-line is drawn on a flat plane, I will teach how to draw it upward from the bottom. It is to be noted that when one wishes to make something, one should first establish its ground plan, whether it be a building or something else. For this reason, one cannot easily lead the snail-line upward unless it is first laid down in a ground plan on a flat plane. 

Therefore, first draw the ground plan of the plain snail-line made previously, together with its circle-line from which it was made, just as it stands before. But leave out all its leaves <note>referring to the decorative foliage from the previous page</note>. However, you must change the figures at the points in the snail-line; note it thus: when you have come around the circle-line from 1 to 12, and then you press inward with the points into the snail-line where the number 1 previously stood, change it there. From the point 12 of the circle-line, where you press into the first point of the snail-line (where 1 was before), set the number 13 at that same place, and so forth in number up to 23. 

Now that this ground plan is drawn out below, then draw an upright straight line up from point 6, through the center **a**, and through point 12, as high as you need it. And at its end, set an **a** at the top, for that same point **a** stands above the center **a**. Then intersect this upright line **a** at the bottom with a horizontal line **c d**; let that place be **b**. Divide this line **a b** with 23 points into 24 equal spaces. 

However, I wish to lengthen the spaces upward in an orderly fashion here, as indicated before. Therefore, I take the same way again for myself, only I reverse the two letters: I set the **a** at the top and the **b** at the bottom; likewise, I begin to count with the figures at the bottom going upward: 1, 2, 3, etc. Now, once this upright line, divided with its points and figures, stands in the middle of the ground plan, I then proceed with an upright line from point 1 of the ground plan upward through the horizontal line **c d**. Then I proceed from point 1 of the vertical line **a b** with a horizontal line toward the upright line that was drawn up from point 1 of the ground plan; where these two lines form a corner <note>intersect</note>, there I set a point 1. This is the first point that begins in the projected snail-line. 

I do this through all the numbers and points of the flattened ground plan and the upright line **a b** on both sides. Thus the snail-line is marked with points from the lowest point **b** upward to the point **a**. After that, I draw the snail-line from point to point. 

Likewise, if a snail-stair <note>original: *schnecken styeg*; a spiral staircase</note> is made through this line in a tower roof <note>original: *dürn dach*; likely a steeple or turret</note>, then the bottom step should be much longer than the top one. And they should be divided in order going up; and the more they come to a point, the higher the steps should become according to the things mentioned above. Just as I have first drawn the ground plan of the snail, and above it the projected snail with all the necessary straight lines. And afterward, I have also drawn the snail-line entirely on its own. 

This snail-line may be drawn closely together or allowed to stretch steeply into the height, depending on whether one makes the line **a b** short or long. This line is useful for many other things. I have also drawn the triangle **a b c** below—from which I lengthened the line **a b** in its parts at the top—through the circle-line **a e** with all its belonging parts. 

Likewise, the aforementioned snail-lines may also be drawn with corners <note>angularly</note> if one always leaves out a point or number in between. Note this in the projected snail: when you draw from point **b** to 2 a straight line, from 2 to 4, from 4 to 6, etc., and so forth all the way through to the end **a**.

<sig>B ij</sig>

->Here you see the<-
->spiral plainly<- <note>original: *Schnecken bloß*. Dürer uses the word "Schnecke" (snail) to describe both flat spirals and three-dimensional helices due to their resemblance to a snail's shell.</note>

<image-desc>A complex geometric construction for a spiral curve. On the left, a large triangle is defined by vertices 'a' at the top, 'b' at the bottom left, and 'c' at the bottom right. The vertical line segment a-b is divided into 23 intervals, numbered 1 through 23 from bottom to top. An arc labeled 'e' extends from point 'b' into the interior of the triangle. A series of lines radiate from vertex 'a' to intersect the arc 'e'. Within the triangle, the number 16 is printed. To the right of this construction, a serpentine or spiral-like line is drawn, composed of 23 connected segments, each numbered from 1 to 23 corresponding to the divisions on the triangle. Labels 'a' and 'b' are also placed at the endpoints of this spiral line.</image-desc>

From this <term>triangle</term> <note>original: *drey angel*; literally "three-angle."</note> **a.b.c.** the line **a.b.** is made, from which the <term>spiral-line</term> <note>original: *schnecke-lini*.</note> is divided. <note>This construction shows a method of "descriptive geometry" where the dimensions of a complex curve are derived from a simpler linear scale on a triangle.</note>

<summary>This page illustrates the use of a proportional triangle to subdivide a spiral into twenty-three precise segments. It provides the mathematical basis for the curves shown on the preceding pages, moving from conceptual shape to practical drafting measurement.</summary>

--- Page 20 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical geometric instructions for constructing complex curves, moving from flat spirals to three-dimensional helical curves used in architecture and engineering.</meta>

Furthermore, another <term>spiral line</term> <note>original: "schneckenlini," literally "snail-line." In this period, the term was used for both 2D spirals and 3D helices.</note> is made from a proper circular draft, which <term>stonemasons</term> <note>Dürer often addresses craftspeople directly, bridging the gap between practical trade knowledge and formal geometry.</note> also use for stairs; however, it is more properly called a <term>helical line</term> <note>original: "schrauffen lini," literally "screw-line."</note>. Yet, call it what you will, it is a useful line, and for that reason, I will also teach how to make it hereafter; and whoever wishes to search further may find much in it. 

First, draw a circular draft as previously shown from a center point **a.**; divide it with a vertical line through the center **a.** into two equal parts and set **12** at the top where the circle is touched and **6** at the bottom. Thereafter, continue from point **12** with a straight line upwards as far as you require; let its end be **a.** Next, cut this vertical line at the bottom near the base of the circle with a <term>cross-line</term> <note>original: "zwerchlini," meaning a horizontal line intersecting the vertical axis at a right angle.</note> **c. d.** at right angles; let its end be **b.** 

Then divide the base circle with points into **12** equal parts and add their numbers to them; start counting next to **12** with **1, 2, 3, etc.** until you come back to twelve; but after that, the numbers must continue to run and be placed one above the other as far as you require; thus you come to **13** upon one, **14** upon two, etc. In this way, one may draw and lead the numbers three, four, or five times, and as often as one wishes over one another, according to how high one wants to make a spiral. 

Now that this base is prepared, then mark the vertical line **a. b.** into as many parts as you want and add the figures to it, beginning to count upwards from the end of **b.** as **1, 2, 3, 4, etc.** Then, from point **1** of the base on the circle, draw a line upwards through the cross-line **c. d.**; then, from point **1** on the vertical line **a. b.**, draw a cross-line toward the vertical line that was drawn up from point **1** of the base. Where these then meet in a corner, set a point **1.** Do this through the entire number of the base and the vertical line **a. b.** through all points, even in the continuation of the numbers.

->Now that the spiral line is thus marked with points everywhere, then draw the spiral<-
->line in by hand, as I have drafted it hereafter. Furthermore,<-
->the spiral lines may also be drawn angularly from point to point.<-
->One may also make such a spiral double in<-
->its path. First, one makes the spindle in the spiral<-
->straight and solid; yet one may also make it hollow<-
->or twisted so that one can see through it from the top<-
->down to the base, just as<-
->the stonemasons should<-
->know how to do in<-
->their drafting<-
->and bring it into the work through the shifting of<-
->the base templates.<-
->Furthermore, from this aforementioned line, one makes<-
->screws of one, two, three, or four threads, etc.,<-
->with which one may lift and break such heavy and strong things<-
->that it is truly to be wondered at.<-

<meta>This page continues the geometric instructions for constructing spirals, moving from the method described previously to a new technique involving the projection of points from a quadrant onto a semicircle.</meta>

Furthermore, I wish to teach how to draw a simple <term>spiral line</term> <note>original: "schneckenlini"; a curve winding around a center, like a snail shell</note> by a different method than before. To do so, proceed as follows: Make a right <term>quadrant</term> <note>original: "firteil von eym zirckel"; a quarter-circle</note> a.b.c., and let /b/ be the <term>center</term> <note>original: "Centrum"</note>, with /a/ at the top and /c/ at the side. Next, divide this <term>circular arc</term> <note>original: "zirckcltrum"</note> with eleven points into twelve equal parts. Start the numbering at point c and draw vertical parallel lines down from all the points until they reach the <term>horizontal line</term> <note>original: "zwerch lini"</note> c.b. Mark these in the same way with numbers as the arc, starting the count at the point nearest to c. In this way, the straight line c.b. is divided proportionally according to the circular arc a.c. Now the first foundation is prepared.

Afterward, draw beneath it from a point c a semicircle, uniform with the upper quadrant, so that the straight line that divides the semicircle is <term>vertical</term> <note>original: "auffrecht"</note> with a at the top, b at the bottom, and the center c in the middle. Next, divide the semicircular line with eleven points into twelve equal fields, and start counting the numbers at the top by a. Then draw straight lines from all the numbered points into point c. Then draw <term>radial lines</term> <note>original: "stram lini" or "streim lini"; literally "streak lines" or rays</note> from the center c to all the numbered points.

Then take a <term>pair of compasses</term> <note>original: "zirckel"</note> and place one foot in the upper quadrant at the center b, and place the other foot on the horizontal line c.b. at point 1. Take this distance with the compasses and carry it down into the semicircle. Place one foot of the compasses in the center c and the other foot at the top under a on the vertical line a.b., and draw a curve from there out to the radial line 1-c. At this resulting point, also place the number one.

Then take the compasses again and place one foot in the quadrant at the center b, and the other on the horizontal line c.b. at point two. Take this distance again and carry it into the lower semicircle. Place one foot of the compasses in the center c, but place the other foot on the radial line 1-c, and draw a curve from there to the next radial line 2-c. At this point, also place the number two.

Continue in this manner between all the radial lines of the lower semicircle's drawing. Once you have taken all the previously indicated distances from the upper quadrant on the horizontal line c.b. and carried them down, marking the points created by the compass marks with their associated numbers along the radial lines, it will show you how to lead the path of the spiral line from the outside of the circular line—starting from point a—into the center c, from point to point, just as I have drawn here below.

Also, you may always place the foot of the compasses that moves around on the vertical line a-c-b and draw from there to its radial line; this indicates something special, as you can see in the drawing.

->18<-

<image-desc>A geometric diagram showing a quadrant (labeled a, b, c) with point 'b' as the center. The arc 'ac' is divided into segments numbered 1 through 11. Vertical lines drop from these points to the horizontal base line 'cb', which is also numbered 1 through 11.</image-desc>

<image-desc>A second geometric diagram showing a semicircle with center 'c'. Radial lines originate from 'c' to the outer edge, numbered 1 through 11. A spiral curve (schneckenlini) is constructed by connecting points on these radial lines, starting at point 'a' at the top and winding inwards toward center 'c'. The base of the vertical axis is labeled 'b'.</image-desc>

--- Page 23 ---
<meta>This page continues the geometric instructions for constructing spirals, following the previous method of using projected points. Here, the author introduces a method of inward-winding spirals (Archimedean spirals) by incrementally reducing the radius of a pair of compasses.</meta>

<page-type>text</page-type>
<sig>B iiij</sig>

Furthermore, I will make a **spiral line** <term>original: "schnecken lini"; literally "snail line," referring to a curve that winds around a central point</term> with the **compass** in another way, thus: First, set a **center** <term>original: "Centrum"</term> **a**; from there, draw a **circular outline** <note>original: "zirckelryß"</note> and divide it, as before, with 12 points into 12 equal fields. Draw straight lines from all the points into the center **a**, and add the numbers to them. Start at the very top and place **12**, then place the numbers 1, 2, 3, etc., around the points until you reach 12 again. 

Next, divide the line **12-a** with 35 points into 36 equal fields <note>By dividing the radius into 36 parts, the spiral can complete three full rotations of 12 sectors each (12 x 3 = 36)</note>, and begin counting the numbers from the top at point **12** downwards: 1, 2, 3, and so on. Then take a pair of compasses and place one foot in the center **a** and the other foot on the line **12-a** at the first point. From there, draw a curve toward the **radial line** <term>original: "streim lini"; literally a "streak line" or ray</term> **1-a**. Then let the one foot of the compass remain still in the center **a**, and move the other foot on the line **12-a** to the second point, **2**, and draw an arc with it between the two radial lines **1-a** and **2-a**. 

In this way, always move the foot of the compass on the line **12-a** by one step at a time, and draw circular arcs in order between all the radial lines until you have gone around for the third time. Thus, through this shifting, the compass becomes narrower and narrower until it nearly reaches the center **a**. Once this is done with the compass, then draw the spiral line from point to point; start at point 12 on the circular line and go around for the third time until you reach the center **a**. I have drawn this out here with all the necessary construction lines by which it is made, and then also plainly as it is drawn by itself, placed side by side.

->19<-

Furthermore, I wish to draw another spiral line. Draw a full circle from a center **a** and divide it with 6 points into 6 equal fields. Add the numbers so that **6** stands at the top, and draw radial lines from all points of the circular line into the center **a**. Then divide the line **6-a** with 7 points into 8 equal fields. Then do as before: take a pair of compasses and place one foot in the center **a** and the other at point 1 on the radial line **1-a**. Proceed in this way step by step through all the numbers, as you can see from the previous spiral line. I have also drawn this below, both with all the necessary supporting lines and on its own <note>original: "ledig"; meaning the "pure" or "empty" version of the shape without the construction markings</note>.

<summary>This page explains a practical method for drawing an Archimedean spiral by dividing a circle into sectors and incrementally shortening the radius of a compass for each segment. It provides instructions for spirals with different numbers of rotations and divisions.</summary>

--- Page 24 ---
<meta>This page continues from the previous discussion on constructing spirals, moving from the movement of the compass to the fundamental geometric principles required for such drafts, specifically focusing on how to divide lines and bisect curves.</meta>

<page-type>text</page-type>

It is necessary to know how to divide a **given line** <term>original: "fürgebene lini"</term>, whether it be long or short, into three equal parts. To do this: let the given line be **a-b**; set it at the bottom on a **horizontal line** <term>original: "zwerch lini"</term> **c-d**. Thereafter, take a **compass** <term>original: "zirckel"</term> and set it to a distance less than half the length of the upright line **a-b**. Place one foot of the compass on the horizontal line **c-d** next to the line **a-b**, and measure three lengths vertically upwards with it. Draw a straight line through these points so that it is parallel to **a-b**. Let this line be **e** at the top and **f** at the bottom. Mark the two middle points that create the three equal fields with the numbers **1** and **2**. 

Next, draw a straight **orthogonal line** <term>original: "ort lini"</term> from the end **e** down through the end **a**, and continue it until it reaches the horizontal line **c-d**. Mark the point where it touches as **g**. Then, from point **g**, draw two straight lines to the points **1** and **2**. Thus, the line **a-b** will be divided into three equal fields by the intersection of the lines just drawn.

Another method for dividing a line into three equal parts: make four horizontal **parallel lines** <term>original: "barlinien"</term>, marked with the numbers **1, 2, 3, 4**, at equal distances from one another. Then take your chosen line, with **a** at the top and **b** at the bottom, and lean it so that the end **a** touches the top horizontal line **1** and the end **b** touches the horizontal line **4**. The two middle horizontal lines, **2** and **3**, will then divide the line **a-b** into three equal parts, just as I have **sketched** <note>original: "aufgerießen"; refers to a technical geometric drawing</note> here below.

Also, before I proceed further, I will first teach how to divide a line exactly in the middle. It is done as follows: Let the given line be horizontal **a-b**. Now take a compass, place one foot on point **a** and the other foot on point **b**, and draw **arcs** <note>original: "zirckelz ryß"; literally "compass-marks" or segments of a circle</note> above and below as far as you need. Then, keeping one foot of the compass still on point **b**, draw with the other foot from point **a** a curve above and below. Where these two compass marks intersect, place a **c** at the top and a **d** at the bottom. Then draw a straight line **c-d**, which will cut **a-b** exactly in the middle; mark that point **e**. 

Furthermore, if someone wishes to place a straight line perpendicularly upon a **circular arc** <term>original: "zirckeltrum"</term>, let him do it in the same manner as shown above. Let the arc be **a-b** and the straight line be **c-d**. Likewise, if one needs to, one should place an upright line in a **concave arc** <note>original: "holl zirckeltrum"; literally a "hollow" or "sunken" segment of a circle</note> according to the same method mentioned above. Both of these ways I have sketched here below, and for the concave arc, I have placed an **f** where the upright line **c-d** intersects it.

<summary>This page explains fundamental geometric constructions for dividing a line into three equal parts using two different methods and provides instructions for bisecting a line or arc using a compass.</summary>
<keywords>geometry, line division, compass construction, parallel lines, perpendicular bisector, circular arcs, technical drawing</keywords>

--- Page 25 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the general geometric division of lines to the practical application of these techniques: constructing a perfect "egg-shaped" oval. It follows the previous page's instructions on dividing lines into equal segments, which is now applied to a horizontal axis to create the proportions of the egg.</meta>

Now I wish to teach how to make a line that would be equal to a **well-formed egg** <term>original: "wolgestaltē ey"</term>; to do this: make a straight **horizontal line** <term>original: "zwerch lini"</term>, the beginning of which shall be **a** and the end **b**. Divide it with 9 points into 10 equal **fields** <term>original: "felt"; referring to the intervals between the points</term>.

Thereafter, take a **compass** <term>original: "zirckel"</term>, set one foot in the middle of the line at point **5**, and the other foot at point **3**; then draw a full circular line passing through point **7**, all the way around above and below.

Afterward, set the compass with one foot at point **b** and the other foot at point **3**; from there, draw a curve downwards. Thereafter, set the compass with one foot at point **a** and the other at point **7**, and also draw downwards from there. Where these two circular arcs meet, place an **e**. 

Then draw a horizontal **parallel line** <term>original: "barlini"</term> below the first circular line, opposite the upper line **a-b**. Where this horizontal line is intersected by the long circular arcs, place a **c** in the point directly below point **3**, and a **d** below point **7**. 

Then draw an **upright line** <term>original: "auffrecht lini"</term> from point **5** down to the angle at **e**. Where it intersects the horizontal line **c-d**, place the number **10**. 

Afterward, divide the **circular arc** <term>original: "zirckeltrum"</term> between **3** and **10** with a point **f** in the middle. Then divide the other arc between **10** and **7** likewise with a point **g** in the middle. 

And then set the compass with one foot at point **f** and the other at point **d**, and draw a curve downwards through the vertical line **5-e**. Thereafter, set the compass with one foot on the other side at point **g** and the other at point **c**, and draw from there downwards. Where these two curved lines meet at the vertical line **5-e**, place an **h**. 

Afterward, divide the segment **h-10** with a point **i** into two equal fields. Set the compass with one foot at point **i** and the other on the circular line **c-h** at the furthest point you can reach, and draw from there in a circle until you reach the other arc **h-d**. 

Thus is this **egg-line** <term>original: "eyer lini"</term> made, as it is shown here below drawn with all the necessary construction lines, and also sketched plainly.

It will be necessary for one to know how to find the **center** <term>original: "Centrū oder mittel puncte"</term> of a **circular arc** <term>original: "zirckeltrum"; literally a "circle-fragment"</term>. This is done in the same way as previously shown, by placing an upright line upon a circular arc; do it as follows: let the given circular arc be **a-b**. Now take a **compass** <term>original: "zirckel"</term> and place one foot at point **a**, and place the other foot a little way along the circular arc, and draw a circular stroke **upwards and downwards** <term>original: "vberſich vnd onderſich"</term>, as far as you need. 

Afterward, take the compass and set one foot in the point that your curved stroke made on the circular arc, and with the other foot draw from point **a** rounds above and below; where these two circular strokes then **intersect** <term>original: "ſchlieſſen"; literally "close" or "lock" together</term> at the top and bottom, draw a straight line through them, and let it extend downwards as far as you require. Do the same on side **b**; thus the two straight lines will intersect each other below where the center is found; there, place a **c**. Thus the center for this circular arc is found, as I have **sketched** <term>original: "auffgerieſſen"</term> below.

<image-desc>A third diagram at the bottom of the page demonstrating the method for finding the center (c) of a circular arc (a-b). Arcs are drawn from points a and b to create two intersection points; a line drawn through these intersections points to the center c of the original arc.</image-desc>

<meta>This page continues the technical geometric instruction from the previous leaf. Having discussed how to find the center of an arc, the author now demonstrates how to circumscribe a circle around three points and how to precisely locate the point of tangency between a curve and a straight line—essential skills for the precise drafting of the ovoid shapes previously discussed.</meta>

It is useful to know, when three points are placed unevenly, how one may skillfully enclose them in a circle whenever necessary. Do it this way: Let the three points be **a**, **b**, and **c**. Connect them with two straight lines **a-b** and **b-c**. Then proceed just as shown in figure 21: find the midpoint of both lines **a-b** and **b-c**, and let the two straight lines—which divide the two given lines **a-b** and **b-c** each in the middle—extend downwards through each other. If needed, lengthen both straight dividing lines. Then take a **compass** <term>original: "zirckel"</term> and set one foot in point **d** and the other in point **a**, and draw a complete circle from there. Thus, the circle will touch the three points **a**, **b**, and **c**, just as I have drawn below.

<image-desc>A geometric diagram showing three points (a, b, c) on a plane. Two line segments connect a to b and b to c. Perpendicular bisectors are drawn for each segment, intersecting at a central point labeled d. A circle is drawn with its center at d, passing through all three points a, b, and c, illustrating the text's instructions on how to circumscribe a circle around three points.</image-desc>

One should also know how to find where a wide **arc** <term>original: "zirckelryß"; literally a circle-drawing</term> touches a straight line. Because the angles where the lines run so close together are narrow, one cannot easily see by eye where they actually meet. This can be accomplished by the following method: Take an **arc segment** <term>original: "zirckeltrum"; literally a "piece of a circle"</term> **a-b** and a straight line **c-d** that touches it. Now take a compass, set one foot at the end **c**, and with the other foot draw a small circular stroke toward the end **d**, both above and below. Where this stroke intersects the line **c-d**, place an **e**. Then, without moving the compass, set one foot in point **e** and draw with the other foot a circle through the end **c**. Where these two circular strokes meet at the top, place an **f**, and at the bottom, a **g**. Then draw a straight line from the two points **f-g**. Where this line intersects the **horizontal** <term>original: "zwerch"</term> line **c-d**, place an **h**. Where it intersects the arc segment **a-b**, place an **i**. Then, from point **i**, draw a **parallel line** <term>original: "barlini"</term> across to the upper horizontal line **c-d**. Where this line intersects the arc segment toward end **b**, place a **k**. Next, take the length **i-k** and lay it on the line **c-d**, with one end at point **h**, and mark its length toward **d** with a point **l**. Then connect **l** and **k** with a straight line; from this, an **oblong rectangle** <term>original: "ablang firung"</term> **h-l-k-i** will be formed. Now, if you want to find the center—the point where the curved line **a-b** touches the straight line **c-d**—take a compass, set one foot in point **l**, and with the other draw a circle through point **h**, above and below. Then reverse the compass, set one foot in point **h**, and draw with the other foot a circle through point **l**, above and below, as shown in figure 21 <note>This refers to the standard method of finding a perpendicular bisector</note>. Where these two circular strokes meet at the top, place an **m**, and at the bottom, an **n**. Then draw a straight line **m-n**. Where this line intersects the horizontal line **c-d**, place a point **o**. This is the exact place where the curved line **a-b** touches the straight line **c-d**, as you see drawn here below.

How is it that two lines, which form an **acute angle** <note>original: "spitzigen winckel"</note> at a single point, can be drawn closer and closer together forever and yet eternally never meet? To see a demonstration of this, understand it as follows: Let there be two **parallel lines** <term>original: "barlinien"; likely from the Latin "lineae parallelae"</term>, the upper labeled **a-b** and the lower **c-d**. These two lines should be extended outward from their ends **b** and **d** toward infinity, or at least be imagined as such. If you then **graduate** <term>original: "gradirst"; to mark with regular intervals or degrees</term> the upper parallel line **a-b** with points and numbers infinitely, and then draw a line from the lower line out of point **c** to the upper line **a-b** at point **1**, then this line **c-1** creates an acute angle at **c**. After that, continue to draw straight lines, one after another, from point **c** to the...

<image-desc>Geometric diagram showing four horizontal or slightly angled lines labeled a, b, c, and d. Lines a and b diverge from a point on the left. Vertical dashed lines connect points marked 1 through 7 across the top lines, demonstrating geometric convergence or projection.</image-desc>

An eternal line <term>original: "ewige lini"; an infinite curve</term> may be conceived, which constantly runs inward toward a center <note>original: "Centrum"</note> and also,
on the other part, runs outward across itself into the distance, and never reaches an end. This
line cannot be made by hand because of its infinite size and smallness. For
since it has no beginning and no end, it cannot be found; it exists almost solely in the intellect <note>original: "verstand"</note>. But I will show it below
with a beginning and an end, as much as is possible. I begin at a point **a** and draw this
line in a circular fashion inward, as if it were running to a center; and as often as it winds around itself, I
remove half of the distance between the lines. I do the same when I run outward from point **a** with the line:
as often as I pass over it, I add a half-part to the distance of the line. Thus this line
runs ever tighter inward and ever wider outward, and yet never reaches any end,
neither inward nor outward, as I have drawn here below to be understood.

<margin>27</margin>
<image-desc>Geometric diagram of a spiral winding outwards from a central point. The letter 'a' is placed at the top of the outer curve of the spiral.</image-desc>

Next, I want to teach how to make a useful line <note>Dürer shifts from theoretical paradoxes to practical drafting</note> that bends in a special way. First,
make a transverse line <term>original: "zwerch lini"; a horizontal axis</term> **c-d**, and divide it with 9 points into 10 equal spaces. Upon the middle
point **5**, place a vertical line at right angles; let its top be **a** and its bottom **b**. This line **a-b**
divide with 19 points into 20 equal spaces, and begin counting from the bottom upward: 1, 2, 3, etc. Then take a
straightedge <note>original: "richtscheyt"</note> and mark the length **b-d** upon it; mark the ends of this length with **e** and **f**. With this length, all the
points of the curved line that one wishes or should make here will be indicated. Next, take one part from **b-d**
and divide it into 3 equal fields. With one of those third-parts, lengthen the aforementioned part. Take a
compass <term>original: "zirckel"</term>, open it as wide as this lengthened part is, keep it fixed, and measure thus:
place one foot in point **d** and with the other foot draw a circular arc <note>original: "zirckeltrum"</note> upward. Then take

--- Page 30 ---
<meta>This page follows the discussion of infinite lines and spirals on page 26. Albrecht Dürer is now transitioning from theoretical infinite lines to the practical, step-by-step geometric construction of specific complex curves, which modern mathematicians recognize as conchoids.</meta>

## These are the two curved lines made through the previously described method.

Take the previous length **e-f** that you have marked <note>original: "gestochen"; literally 'pricked' or 'incised' into the tool with a compass or stylus</note> on your straightedge <term>straightedge: a ruler without markings used for drawing straight lines</term>, and place it with one end **e** on the vertical line **a-b** at point **1**, and lay the other end **f** upon the circular arc <term>original: "zirckeltrum"; an arc or portion of a circle</term> so that it touches it; at that spot, set a new point **1**. After that, lay your straightedge again with one end **e** on the line **a-b** at point **2**, and then set the compass with one foot in the newly formed point **1**, and with the other foot draw a round arc upward as before. Where the projected line <note>original: "vordacht lini"; the line intended or visualized through the construction</note> with the end **f** touches the curve, set a point **2**. Continue doing this through the whole sequence of numbers as high as you can reach. Afterward, draw this line in its curve from point to point through its numbers. I have subsequently drawn this line twice with a shorter line **e-g**; they come closer together at the top of the curve than at the bottom. This is plotted <note>original: "auffgerissen"; a technical term for a draft or geometric drawing</note> as follows:

<image-desc>
A geometric diagram demonstrating the construction of two curved lines (likely conchoids) using a vertical axis and projection points. The diagram features a vertical line labeled 'a' at the top and 'b' at the bottom. To the right of this axis, several curved lines are plotted, with one set labeled with numbers 1 through 19. A symbol consisting of two stylized 'z'-like flourishes appears next to the number 8 near the vertical axis. </image-desc>

This previously made line can be altered in another way, namely, by the following logic: First, the line **e-f**, which is shifted and used to measure this line, must be graduated <term>graduated: divided into regular steps or degrees</term> and marked with 16 points in 17 fields. Likewise, in every shift it must be laid down so that one of these parts is cut off. But such parts of the line **e-f** must not be of equal size; rather, the degrees should increase in size toward **e** and become smaller toward **f**. To find how to mark such an ending of the degrees on your straightedge, you must derive it from the previously described triangle <term>original: "dyangel"; an archaic form of 'triangle'</term> **a-b-c** through the circular arc **b-e** in the eighth figure. However, incline the line of the triangle **a-b** far from the circular arc **b-e** to the side. If the circular arc **b-e** is then divided with 16 points into 17 equal parts, and then from point **c** straight lines are drawn through the points in **b-e** until they reach line **a-b**, then the parts on **a-b** will be quite large toward **a**, but toward **b** [they will be small].

<summary>This page provides a technical guide for plotting complex curves by marking points with a straightedge and compass. Dürer also explains how to vary these curves by using a non-linear scale derived from a geometric triangle projection.</summary>

Whoever wishes to create this second curved line must use the first lines previously made—namely the vertical line **a-b**, the curve, and the scale <term>original: "ortlini"; here referring to a base line or measuring scale</term> **e-f**, as it was marked. Afterward, draw this newly formed curved line from point to point; thus you will see how it takes shape against the first curved line, as I have drawn here.
