You should perform such a division of this line using the previously mentioned triangle **a-b-c** from the eighth figure. However, the newly chosen short straight line **a-b** must be used in place of the circular arc <term>original: "zirckeltrum"; a curved section or arc of a circle</term> **b-c**. <meta>This page continues the geometric instructions from the previous page, shifting focus from architectural arches to the more advanced mathematical construction of conic sections, specifically the ellipse.</meta>

<image-desc>A geometric diagram at the top of the page illustrating the projection or construction of points for a conic section. A main horizontal line is labeled with points 'c' at the far left and 'e' at the far right. Between them, several points are marked with alphabetical labels: 'a', 'g', 'f', and 'd'. Below this line, there are two sets of numbered markers from 1 to 11, flanking two central points labeled 'g' and 'h'. The points are connected or related by a horizontal line that seems to represent a scale or an axis.</image-desc>

The ancients have indicated that one may make three kinds of cuts through a <term>cone</term><note>original: "kegel"</note>, which are distinct from one another and which do not have the same circular outline as the base of the cone. Otherwise, one might cut the cone apart through the middle [horizontally]; such a cut is shaped like the [base of the] cone, but this is not taken into account here. 

But the other three cuts each produce a unique line; I wish to teach how to draw those same lines. The scholars call the first cut <term>Ellipse</term><note>original: "Elipsis"</note>; it cuts the cone <term>obliquely</term><note>original: "schleyms," meaning slanted or at an angle.</note> and takes nothing away from the base of the cone. This oblique cut may be taken higher on one side and lower on the other, so that one side is closer to the base and the other is further away. 

The second cut, in drawing, is a <term>parallel line</term><note>original: "barlini," meaning a line parallel to the side of the cone.</note> with the side of the cone *a.b.*, or reversed, as one wishes; this the scholars call <term>Parabola</term><note>original: "Parabola"</note>. 

The third cut, in drawing, is a vertical parallel line with the line drawn upwards from the center of the cone's base into the cone's apex, *a*. This they call <term>Hyperbola</term><note>original: "Hiperbole"</note>. I do not know the German names for these three cuts, but we shall give them names here so that they may be recognized. 

I will call the Ellipse an <term>egg-line</term><note>original: "eyer lini"</note>, because it is nearly identical to an egg. Let the Parabola be named a <term>burning-line</term><note>original: "brenn lini"</note>, because if one makes a mirror from its shape, it will ignite [fires]. But the Hyperbola I will call a <term>fork-line</term><note>original: "gabellini"</note>. 

Now, as I wish to draw the egg-line (Ellipse), I must first draw the cone and indicate the cut within it, and likewise create the <term>ground plan</term><note>original: "grund"</note> beneath it; I do it thus: Let the apex of the cone be at the top, *a*, and the base at the bottom, *b. c. d. e.* Now I draw a vertical line down from *a*, and let the oblique cut through the cone be *f* at the top and *g* at the bottom. This cut *f. g.* I divide with 11 points into 12 equal parts, and I begin the numbering below *f*. Beneath this cone, I draw its ground plan, so that *a* becomes a center and *b. c. d. e.* its circular outline, as is produced by a vertical cone. 

When vertical lines fall from all these points down into the ground plan, these lines—such as *f, g* and the numbers between them, 1, 2, 3, etc.—intersect the circular sketch. I mark these also with their letters and digits. Once that is done, I take a <term>compass</term><note>original: "zirckel"</note> and place one foot on the vertical line *a* within the cone at the height of the oblique cut *f. g.* at point 1. At this same height, I place the other foot of the compass out to the line *a. d.* and keep this width with the compass. I carry this width to the ground plan drawn below and place one foot of the compass in the center *a*, and the other foot on the extended line 1, and draw a round arc out towards *d* and back to line 1. 

After that, I place the compass again with one foot on the vertical line *a* in the cone at the height of point 2 of the cut *f. g.*, and the other foot on the line *a. d.*, and carry that same width again into the ground plan. I place one foot of the compass in the center *a* and the other foot on the straight line 2, and draw from there in a circle towards *d* and back to the straight line 2. I continue this way until point 4. Then, at number 5, I turn the compass with one foot on the line *a. b.* and carry that down, drawing around in the ground plan from center *a* from the extended line 5 towards *d* and back to this line 5. I do this through all the numbers, transferring everything from the upper cone to the ground plan. 

Afterwards, I create the bare line of the Ellipse from this ground plan as follows: I draw the length of the cut *f. g.* vertically, just as it is divided with its eleven points into 12 equal fields, and draw eleven transverse parallel lines through all the points. Then I take the widths from the ground plan on the straight line 1, as far as the compass marks them, and carry them to the cut *f. g.*, placing them on line 1 and marking the width on both [sides]...

The **Parabola** is to be made in the same way as the ellipse. First, I draw the cone *a.b.c.d.e.* and within it the vertical line *a.*, and I cut the parabola from the top down through the foot [base] of the cone, so that this cut is a <term>parallel line</term><note>original: "barlini"</note> to the side of the cone *a.b.*, and let this cut be *f.* at the top and *g.h.* at the bottom. After that, I divide *f.g.h.* with eleven points into twelve equal fields, and draw <term>transverse lines</term><note>original: "zwerch linien," meaning horizontal or cross-lines.</note> through all the points in *f.g.h.* Regarding those that stand on the side toward *a.d.*, I draw those same transverse lines from the vertical line *a.* to the cone’s line or side *a.d.* But those that stand on the other side, I draw from the vertical line *a.* to the side line of the cone *a.b.* After that, I make the base of the cone beneath the cone, of which the center is *a.* and the <term>circumference</term><note>original: "zirckellini," literally 'circle-line.'</note> is *b.c.d.e.* After that, from all the points of the numbers and *f.g.h.*, I let straight lines fall down from the cone through the round base, and mark them therein with their numbers, in the same way...

Albrecht Dürer demonstrates the "projection" method, mapping points from a three-dimensional cone onto a two-dimensional base to find the exact curvature.</summary>

...as was shown before in the base plan of the **egg-line** <term>Ellipse</term><note>original: "eyer lini Elipsis"</note>. Then I place the **compass** <note>original: "zirckel"</note> with one foot in the center of the base plan at *.a.*, and the other foot on the straight line *1.*, and draw a curve toward *.d.* until I reach line *1.* again. I do this for all the numbered lines until I finally reach *.g.* and *.h.*. I draw a horizontal line and place vertically upon it the height of the parabola from within the cone *.f. g. h.*. After that, I take the widths *.g. h.* from the base plan and place them on the horizontal line so that the vertical line *.f.* stands in the middle, and I mark these two points with *.g.* and *.h.*. 

Next, I draw eleven lines through all the points of the vertical line *.f.* as far as I need them. I then transfer all the widths from the base plan—following the numbers of all the straight lines that were cut by the compass marks—to the vertical line *.f.* and mark them as points on both sides, so that the vertical line *.f.* always remains in the center. Then I draw the **burning line** <term>parabola</term> from point to point, just as I have illustrated here.

--- Page 37 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the geometric construction of a parabola to its practical application in optics, specifically the creation of "burning mirrors." It maintains the previous page's focus on the parabola as a "burning line" but moves from the cone section to the laws of reflection.</meta>

IF you wish to make an **igniting mirror** <term>parabolic mirror</term><note>original: "ankünd spiegel," likely a variant of *anzündspiegel*, a mirror designed to concentrate sunlight to start a fire.</note> from the **parabola** <note>original: "Parabel"</note> or **burning-line** <note>original: "brenlini"</note> mentioned here before, then make the **cone** <note>original: "kegel"</note> from which you wish to cut the parabola with its peak no higher than its base is wide—or rather, so that the cone forms an equilateral **triangle** <note>original: "rechter dryangel." In this context, it refers to a triangle where the sides and base are equal.</note>. If you then cut a parabola into it and take that same line and make a hollow mirror from it, then cut it off a little at the front; it will then burn strongly at the points that coincide when the **rays** <note>original: "radi," from the Latin *radii*.</note> of the sun are cast into it, which then reflect back out. To understand this, you must first note that every object that appears in a mirror reflects back out in the same way it falls in. Yet, what is inside is seen at the corresponding place of what is outside; therefore, left becomes right and vice versa. I will draw this below for better understanding.

So, I draw a horizontal line *.a.b.* By this, understand a plane mirror or a body of water into which you look. Now I place a light *.c.* at a height on one side, and place a little man opposite on the other side to look into the mirror or the water; let his eye be *.d.*. The light *.c.* is not found by the eye until the angles of the light's **rays** <note>original: "radi"</note> *.c.* and the sight-lines of the vision *.d.* become equal. This happens as follows: When you draw an upright line upwards from point *.e.*, where the **reflection** <note>original: "widerbrechung," literally "breaking-back." The author uses this term for both reflection and refraction.</note> occurs, and place one foot of a **compass** <note>original: "zirckel"</note> in this point *.e.* and travel around with the other foot above the line *.a.b.*, and find by measurement that the rays of the light *.c.* and the sight-line *.d.* are equally far from the upright line, then that is the correct point where the light is found. Then, when your sight passes downwards through the mirror and an upright line falls down from the light *.c.* above, it is found by the line passing through *.d.* how deep the light appears in the mirror or water. In the same way, according to its nature, the sun’s **rays** <note>original: "radi"</note> strike back into the mirror made from the line of the parabola and all fall out into a single point and burn strongly. And as for the cause of this, the **mathematicians** <note>original: "Mathematici"</note> have demonstrated it; whoever wishes may read about it. You see my aforementioned opinion drawn below.

<summary>The author explains how to construct a parabolic mirror and describes the fundamental law of reflection—that the angle of incidence equals the angle of reflection—using the example of a light and an observer.</summary>
<keywords>parabola, burning mirror, reflection, optics, geometry, rays, cone construction</keywords>

--- Page 38 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for drafting conic sections. While the previous page focused on the parabola and its practical use in optics (burning mirrors), this page moves to the construction of the hyperbola, which the author refers to as the "fork-line." The geometric method involves projecting points from a side view (elevation) of a cone down into a circular base plan.</meta>

Now I will further draw the **fork-line** <term>hyperbola</term><note>original: "gabel lini Hiperbole." Dürer often used descriptive German names for geometric curves; "fork-line" refers to the way the two branches of a hyperbola appear to diverge like a fork.</note>; this will follow the same logic as before. I draw again the **cone** <note>original: "kegel"</note> *.a.b.c.d.e.* After that, I draw in this cone a vertical **parallel line** <note>original: "barlini," a shortened form of "parallel-linie."</note> opposite the vertical axis *.a.*; let it be *.f.* at the top and *.g h* at the bottom, so that side *.d.* is cut off. This section of the hyperbola *.f g h* I divide with eleven points into twelve fields. And from all the points of *.f g h* and the numbers, I extend **horizontal parallel lines** <note>original: "barlini ober zwerch"</note> as far as I need them, and draw a vertical line *.f.* on the side through all these horizontal lines.

After that, I make the round **base plan** <note>original: "grund"</note> beneath the cone with its **center** <note>original: "Centrum"</note> at *.a.* and the circular outline *.b.c.d.e.* Then I let the section of the cone *.f g h* cut through this base and add the letters *.g f h* accordingly, as it is projected from the cone into the base plan.

Then I take a **compass** <note>original: "zirckel"</note>, as I have shown before, and take the width of the half-cone on each horizontal line of the line *.f g h*, and carry these down into the base plan. I place one foot of the compass in the **center** <note>original: "Centrum"</note> *.a.* and with the other foot draw all the circular arcs toward *.d.*, which are then cut by the line *.g f h*, and I add their numbers to them. After that, I take the widths from the base plan along all the straight lines which to both... <note>The sentence ends abruptly, continuing onto the next page.</note>

<summary>This page provides a step-by-step geometric construction of a hyperbola (referred to as a "fork-line") by intersecting a cone with a vertical plane and projecting the resulting points onto a two-dimensional base plan.</summary>

and at point 16, a small section roughly remains. ### A shell-line

For this line made above, one may prepare a tool so that it can be made easily, namely as follows: Make a square piece of wood as long as you need horizontally, starting from *a* at the back to *b*. Into this, cut a deep **groove** <note>original: "niet," referring here to a channel or slot.</note> at the top so that something can be guided back and forth within it. Divide the wood with points and numbers into as many parts as you wish, and begin the numbering at *a*. Then, in the middle of each side of this horizontal frame, place two upright, thin rulers, as long as the horizontal wood or frame is, and set them close together. Mark them with the same numbers as the horizontal wood or frame, and begin the numbering at the bottom. Next, make a fine **rod** <note>original: "lenslein," a small slat or lath.</note>, as long or short as you wish to have it, and at the very end of it, make a small **revolving wheel** <note>original: "vmlaufes redlein," likely a caster or roller.</note> that fits into the middle of the groove in the horizontal frame *a, b*, so that it can move back and forth. Then slide the rod out between the two thin rulers toward the part *b*, and place the wheel at the back or bottom in the middle of the horizontal wood toward *a* at the first point 1. Also lay the rod down between the wooden uprights at the bottom point 1. And when you then move the wheel along the bottom toward the upright ruler, as you move inward, always move the rod up between your thin rulers until you come all the way to the end with the wheel passing through the two upright rulers.

But another line, let it be called a <term>spider line</term> <note>original: "spinnen lini"; here describing a specific type of spiral curve</note>, because in the <term>drafting</term> <note>original: "auffreissen"; the process of geometric layout or construction</note> by which it is made, it appears almost like a spider's web. I create it through a twofold movement in this way: I draw an upright line, *a b*. To this, I attach another line whose end point is *c*. I let the line *a b* remain fixed at end *a*, but I move end *b* around <term>in a circular manner</term> <note>original: "zirckels weiß"</note>, just as I have marked the path of the rotation everywhere with the letter *b*. After that, the other line *c*, which is joined to it at end *b*, should also remain fixed at its rear end at point *b*; however, its forward end, *c*, should be moved around <term>in a circular manner</term>. Thus, as the first line is moved around and the other joined line is also moved around separately, the end point *c* traces a unique line. But so that this line may be guided with certainty, I place a <term>compass</term> <note>original: "zirckel"</note> with one foot at point *a* and draw a <term>circular line</term> <note>original: "zirckellini"</note> below *b*. I also <term>graduate</term> <note>original: "gradir"; to mark a scale with degrees or units</note> this into parts with numbers, so that the line *a b* may move with certainty from point to point. I do the same at point *b*, and as often as I move one degree with line *a b*, I also move one degree on the <term>circle</term> *b* with line *c*. In this way, the end point *c* marks the points between which the line should be drawn together, which I have marked everywhere with *c*, as is drafted in the following illustration.

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for constructing a geometric drawing instrument. The previous page introduced the concept of the "spider line" created by rotating interconnected arms; this page provides specific mechanical details on how to build the adjustable rods and discs and how to calculate their proportions using geometric squares.</meta>

rods, if one wishes, they should be made in such a way that they may be moved <term>degree by degree</term> <note>original: "von grad zu grad"; referring to incremental adjustments along a scale</note> to be pulled apart and lengthened, or pushed into one another and shortened. And every rod shall be made so that it may be turned or rotated from degree to degree toward whichever side one wishes. One may also make many or few of these discs and rods, according to how one intends to use this instrument. And the bottom-most rod shall be placed upright, with the lowest part fixed and not bent to any side. For all things must revolve around this rod, but in its rotation, it may be turned through all degrees in the <term>pivot</term> <note>original: "zapfen"; a pin or socket allowing rotation</note> as one wishes. But to make this understood, I will present it thus: I make four rods, which have four discs at the top acting as joints in the <term>center</term> <note>original: "Centrum"</note> where the rods are bent. And the discs should be marked with <term>numbers</term> <note>original: "ziffer"</note> along their circular circumference by degrees. And the last and smallest disc shall have, according to its measure, a long needle that goes out from its center and runs around, by which the path of the intended line is indicated. This needle should also be made so that it can be pushed out or in, so that it may be made longer or shorter from degree to degree. Such an instrument as described above may be changed in many ways, according to what its use requires; one may also make it large or small. But it is necessary to note that the rods and discs must be made larger or smaller according to the correct proportion, for the hindmost shall be the largest and the foremost the smallest, so that all this is well-arranged in form, strength, and weight.

The way to make them is thus: First, create the discs according to their size in such an order as you wish to have the discs. Draw a proper <term>square</term> <note>original: "firung" or "vierung"</note> *b.c.d.e.* and set a point *a* in the middle. Take a <term>compass</term> <note>original: "zirckel"</note>, set one foot in point *a*, and with the other foot draw a circular line that touches the four sides of the square; this is the size of the first disc. After that, draw two straight lines *a-c* and *a-d*, and attach another such half-part outside of *c-d*; let that corner be *f*. Thus the second square *a-c-f-d* is found. Then set a compass with one foot in the middle of the line *c-d* at a point *g*, and with the other foot draw a circular line that touches the sides of the smaller square *a-c-f-d*; this is the size of the second disc. After that, set a point *h* in the middle of the line *b-c* and draw a straight line *a-h*; thus a square *h-c-g-a* is formed. After that, divide *a-c* with a point *j* in the middle, and take a compass, set one foot in point *j*, and with the other foot draw a circular line that touches the sides of the square *h-c-g-a*; that is the size of the third disc. After that, draw a straight line *j-h* and attach another such half-part outside of *h-c*; let that corner be *k*. Thus a square *h-f-c-i* is formed. After that, divide the line *h-c* with a point *l* in the middle. In this point *l*, set one foot of the compass, and with the other foot draw a circular line that touches the sides of the square *h-f-c-i*; this is the size of the smallest disc. This stands in the correct order, for the first disc is twice as large as the second, the second is twice as large as the third, and the third is twice as large as the fourth.

Now each of the rods should be four times as long as the disc is high which one sets upon it at its center. From this, it follows that all the lengths of these four rods may be taken entirely from the <term>diagonal lines</term> <note>original: "ortstrichen"; lines connecting corners, or diameters</note> or diameters of the squares, which diminish from one another, as is sufficiently shown from the squares of the discs. Likewise, the widths of the rods must also be taken from squares; note it thus: Make the first and largest rod as wide as one-seventeenth part of its length. From this, cut off a square at the top with a cross-line. After that, draw two straight lines from the center point of this square to the two side corners of the square, and attach another such half-part; thus a square half as large as the first is formed. Then draw the width of the second rod from this newly formed square; thus it will also be one-seventeenth part wide relative to its length. After that, do the same in like manner with the third and fourth squares—or if you wish to make more of them—which are diminished from one another, and always draw the width of the rods from them. Thus each one gains a proper width according to its measure, so that one has exactly the same form as the other. Thus the rods slide, turn, and rotate, following the displacement of the squares, further and further upwards, and their ends trace a fine <term>spiral line</term> <note>original: "schneckenlini"; literally a "snail line," describing a logarithmic or Archimedean spiral</note>. And if one wishes to scribe something decorative into these large rods, one finds how to divide it equally through the aforementioned method; for through this, the <term>bosses</term> <note>original: "bossen"; ornamental projections or relief work</note> of large and small things become equal to one another. And whoever makes this instrument should watch very closely so that the movements, when one guides them around, do not interfere with one another. Also, fasten the rods at the center of the discs, one on the right side and the other on the left, to one another, and so on throughout; and the rods shall be turned at the very bottom in their degrees, and in the middle...

--- Page 46 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of geometry and its practical applications for artists and craftsmen, specifically focusing on how to determine proportional lengths of lines to create two-dimensional planes and three-dimensional solids.</meta>

First, I want to arrange several lines in their cutting so they are proportionally extended against one another. Understand it this way: join two straight lines, **a-b** and **b-c**, so that point **b** is a right angle. Then draw a straight diagonal line across from **a** to **c**. Afterward, divide **b-c** with 4 points into 5 equal fields, and from these points **1, 2, 3, 4**, draw straight vertical parallel lines upward until they meet the diagonal line **a-c**. Thus all four of these lines will be cut off, quite equally proportional to each other, from which one may draw planes or <term>solids</term> <note>original: "Corpora"; referring to three-dimensional geometric bodies</note>. This method is the simplest <note>Dürer uses "schlechtest" in its archaic sense of "plain" or "simplest," rather than "worst."</note>, yet useful in practice; therefore I have drawn it below.

### 43

*The illustration shows a right-angled triangle where the base is divided into equal parts. Vertical lines rise from these points to the hypotenuse, demonstrating how the height of each line increases at a constant, linear rate.*

In another way, yet following the previous intention, I will extend lines against one another by shifting the points. Namely as follows: Make a horizontal line <note>original: "zwerch lini"</note>, **a-b**, as long as you need it, and set four points upon it. Place the first point, **1**, near the end, **b**. Place the second point, **2**, as far from it as you wish. Place the third point, **3**, twice as far from the second as **1** is from **2**. Place the fourth point, **4**, twice as far as **2** is from **3**. One may set as many of these points as one wishes, and increase or decrease this progression in order. 

And when the points are set, then extend straight parallel lines upward from them as long as you need, and label the lines at the top **1-c, 2-d, 3-e, 4-f**. Then, from point **c**, draw a diagonal line to cut off all the vertical lines, whether long or short. In this way, they gain a correct proportion <note>original: "rechte maß"</note> to one another. If one wishes to make planes or solids from them, their width and thickness are found at the top, when one moves from their ends on the diagonal line **f-g** with a straight horizontal line to the longer vertical line. These horizontal lines also maintain their specific proportions to one another, just as I have drawn in what follows.

### 44

*The illustration depicts a horizontal baseline with points spaced at exponentially increasing intervals. Vertical lines rise to a diagonal "ortlini," and horizontal segments are drawn to create a series of rectangles that grow in size according to the geometric progression described.*

<summary>Dürer demonstrates two methods for creating proportional sets of lines: the first using equal spacing to create a linear progression, and the second using doubled spacing to create a geometric progression.</summary>
<keywords>proportional lines, right angle, diagonal line, geometric progression, planes, solid bodies, drafting techniques</keywords>

--- Page 47 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical demonstration of how to derive proportional measurements. While the previous page focused on linear progressions using a diagonal line, this page introduces more complex curves—specifically circular arcs—to determine the dimensions of geometric forms.</meta>

Now I wish to demonstrate another method, using a <term>circular arc</term> <note>original: "zirckeltrum"; an arc or segment of a circle</note>, to draw lines in relation to one another and to extend them in an orderly fashion. This is how I proceed: I draw a circular arc, **b-c**, from a <term>center point</term> <note>original: "Centrum"</note>, **a**, and draw an upright vertical line **a-b** and another horizontal line <note>original: "zwerch lini"</note> **a-c**, so that **a** forms a <term>right angle</term> <note>original: "rechter winckel"</note>. Afterward, I divide **a-c** with four points into five equal fields, and from these points I draw vertical parallel lines upward until they meet the circular arc **b-c**. Where they touch the arc, I label them with the numbers and letters **1 d, 2 e, 3 f, 4 g**. These four lines relate to one another in a specific way. 

Next, I rotate the circular arc along with its letters, so that **b-a** becomes horizontal and **a-c** becomes vertical. Then I draw vertical lines from the points on the circular arc—**d, e, f, g**—down to the horizontal line **a-b**. These lines also relate to one another in a specific way. One should also note how their widths and thicknesses result from these lengths, and how each shape compares to the others, as I have illustrated in the drawing below.

*A page from a geometric treatise featuring three diagrams. 1. The top left diagram shows a circular sector (quadrant) with vertical lines drawn from a horizontal base (labeled with points a, 4, 3, 2, 1) up to the arc (labeled g, f, e, d). The number 45 is written above the arc. 2. The top right diagram shows a similar arc where horizontal lines are drawn from a vertical axis to the arc, labeled with letters and numbers. 3. The bottom diagram consists of a series of vertical line segments of decreasing heights, labeled with numbers (4, 3, 2, 1) and letters (g, f, e, d) corresponding to the geometric proportions derived from the sectors above.*

In the following section, I will use a <term>concave arc</term> <note>original: "hol zirckeltrum"; literally a "hollow" or inward-curving arc</note> to compare lines in their decrease and increase, and to show what shape they create in a <term>plane</term> <note>original: "plano"</note> or a <term>solid</term> <note>original: "Corpus"</note> when the width and thickness corresponding to their length is sought. 

I proceed as follows: First, I draw an upright, right-angled elongated rectangle <note>original: "fierung"</note>; let it be **a-b** at the top and **c-d** at the bottom. I place one foot of a compass at point **a** and the other at point **b**, and draw a curve upward from there until it reaches the side **a-d**. Afterward, I divide the side **c-d** into five fields using four points, and from there I draw four straight lines upward until they reach the concave circular line. Where they touch it, I label them **1 e, 2 f, 3 g, 4 h**. 

Now you see how these lines relate to one another; and if they are cut off at the top by a horizontal line, you see what width or solid volume they attain in relation to the other, longer line. If I now lay this rectangle on its side, so that **d-a** is at the top and **b-c** at the bottom, and draw straight lines from the points of the concave arc—**e, f, g, h**—down to the horizontal line **b-c**, you see how they relate to one another in length. And if I draw horizontal lines from the points **h, g, f, e** to the longer line, one sees what kind of plane or solid results, which may be made round or square. This is shown in the drawing that follows.

<meta>This page continues the instructional sequence on geometric proportions. While the previous page established how to derive measurements using a diagonal line, this page transitions into using circular arcs—both concave and convex—to determine the heights and thicknesses of architectural or artistic elements.</meta>

Another method for extending lines relative to one another, much like the previous way: make or draw a <term>quadrant</term> <note>original: "vierteyl eins zirckels"; a quarter of a circle</note> **b-c**. Let the <term>center</term> <note>original: "Centrum"</note> be **a**. Divide this arc with 4 points into 5 equal fields. From these 4 points, draw straight lines directly downwards onto a <term>horizontal line</term> <note>original: "zwerch lini"</note>. In this way, you will find how the lines relate to one another. From this, you will also find their width and thickness for creating something, as I have illustrated in the drawing below.

---

But in summary: all <term>vertical lines</term> <note>original: "aufrecht linien"</note> that are placed in an orderly fashion at equal or unequal distances next to one another on a horizontal line can be "cut off" <note>meaning their heights can be determined</note> in three ways: with a concave or a convex <term>circular line</term> <note>original: "zirckellini"; an arc</note>, or with a <term>diagonal line</term> <note>original: "ortlini"</note>, whether long or short. Each method gives the form its own particular character. And although this was all demonstrated before, understand it now even more clearly. 

Draw a horizontal line **a-b** and place four vertical lines upon it, marked with the <term>numbers</term> <note>original: "zyfern"</note> **1, 2, 3, 4**. At the end point **a**, draw a vertical line upwards. Then take a <term>compass</term> <note>original: "zirckel"</note>; set one foot on this vertical line and the other foot below on the line at point **a**, and draw an arc out through all four lines. In this way, they will be cut off in the correct order. 

However, if you wish to cut the lines shorter in relation to one another, open the compass wider and set it higher on the vertical line **a** than you did before. Place the other foot back at the same position at the bottom of line **a**, and draw the arc through the other lines. Then they will be cut shorter than before. This is now done with the

<summary>This page describes how to use a compass and a quadrant to determine the proportional heights and thicknesses of a series of vertical lines. It introduces the concept of using arcs—rather than just straight diagonals—to create specific geometric relationships between parts of a design.</summary>
<keywords>quadrant, proportional scaling, vertical lines, horizontal line, compass, geometric construction, Albrecht Dürer, measurement methods</keywords>

--- Page 49 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for determining the heights of vertical lines using geometry. While the previous page began explaining how to "cut" vertical lines using a concave arc, this page explores convex arcs and straight diagonal lines, providing a theoretical foundation based on Euclidean geometry.</meta>

<page-num>48</page-num>

...cut off in a concave curve <note>original: "holen ryß"; referring to the arc from the previous page</note>. But with the upward-bent <term>convex arc</term> <note>original: "auffgebogen zirckel"</note>, cut the aforementioned lines thus: draw a vertical line upwards and downwards through the end point **b**. Place one foot of the <term>compass</term> <note>original: "zirckel"</note> below on line **b**, and set the other foot on the <term>horizontal line</term> <note>original: "zwerch lini"</note> toward **a** (before the vertical lines), and draw from there through the four lines you wish to cut off.

If, however, you wish for them to be longer relative to each other in the cut, move one foot of the compass further up the vertical line **b** toward the top and place the other foot back in the previous point on the horizontal line **a**. Draw again through the four lines, and they will be cut longer in proportion to each other.

The reason I shift the compass on the straight lines **a** or **b** is for the sake of proper geometric skill. For **Euclid** teaches in his third book of the *Elements*, in the tenth theorem of the eleventh proposition <note>Dürer likely refers to Proposition 11 of Book III in Euclid's *Elements*, which discusses the tangency of circles</note>, that when a large and a small circle touch each other internally, the two <term>centers</term> <note>original: "Center"</note> of both circles always lie on a single straight line. Furthermore, this straight line drawn through both centers always indicates the exact point where the circles touch one another.

Understand it in this way: Draw an arc **1, 2, 3** from the center **a**. In the field within this curve, place a point **b** wherever you like. Then take a compass and place one foot on point **b** and the other on the arc **1, 2, 3**, and draw a smaller arc as it occurs. The center of this arc is **b**. If you then draw a straight line from center **a** through center **b** all the way to the arc **1, 2, 3**, this straight line will always show where the two arcs touch. Use this principle in the aforementioned cutting of lines; although one might set the compass differently, this method is more skillful.

However, one can also cut off the four proposed lines with a **straight line**, so that they extend more or less relative to one another. Note it thus: Set the point from which you wish to cut with the line at the end of the horizontal line **a**, and then draw straight lines from it through the four verticals, higher or lower, by which you wish to lengthen the four lines much or little relative to each other, as I have illustrated in the drawings that follow.

-> [Geometric Diagrams] <-

<note>The diagrams illustrate (1) an arc intersecting vertical lines to determine their height, (2) diagonal lines from a single point 'a' creating a perspective-like scaling of the four verticals, and (3) the Euclidean proof of internal tangency between two circles with centers 'a' and 'b'.</note>

--- Page 50 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical discussion on the geometry of vertical lines. While the previous page focused on using arcs (concave and convex) to "cut" or determine the height of lines, this section introduces arithmetic and geometric progressions to determine line proportions, specifically for the use of craftsmen like stonemasons.</meta>

Straight lines that are cut off relative to one another are of three kinds. Two of these types are created through numbers: the first is through an <term>even number</term> where the base <note>original: "wurtzel" or root; here meaning the base of a mathematical progression</note> is 2; the second is through an <term>odd number</term> where the base is 3. After these, there are lines whose proportions to one another are of such a measure that they cannot be expressed through numbers <note>These are "irrational" proportions, which cannot be written as simple fractions, a concept Dürer likely drew from Euclidean geometry</note>. Understand these three things as follows: First, place four lines next to each other and use the division of even numbers. Let the first line be 2, the second 4, the third 8, and the fourth 16 <note>a geometric progression of 2ⁿ</note>. But for the other four lines of odd numbers, the first is 3, the second 9, the third 27, and the fourth 81 <note>a geometric progression of 3ⁿ</note>. In this way, one can increase or decrease even and odd numbers in both types of line progressions, and not always use a simple doubling, as anyone who knows a little arithmetic will easily understand.

One may also ascend through the lines using a single number. However, how one should divide lines that cannot be divided by numbers is shown in the figure immediately preceding this <note>referring to the geometric constructions on the previous page that don't rely on integers</note>. I have drawn the two types of even and odd numbers to be used for lines below; these may also be used by <term>stonemasons</term> <note>original: "Steinmetzen"; Dürer wrote this manual specifically to bridge the gap between theoretical geometry and the practical needs of craftsmen</note> in their <term>projections</term> <note>original: "außzügen"; architectural drafts or templates</note>. One can also extend these lines further down onto a <term>horizontal line</term> <note>original: "zwerch lini"</note>, though their proportions to each other will then change from what they were before.

->[Geometric diagram showing vertical lines of increasing height labeled 2, 4, 8, 16 on the left and 3, 9, 27, 81 on the right, with a line labeled 49 in the center.]<-

Furthermore, if you have two lines, one long and one short, and you wish to find a third, even shorter line that is in proportion to them—so that the relationship of the shorter to the longer is the same as the relationship of the newly found smallest line to the middle one <note>This describes finding a geometric progression or a mean proportional: a:b = b:c</note>—do it as follows: Join the two lines, the long and the short, together end-to-end along a horizontal length. Start at the beginning of the long line and mark the total lengths as **a**, **b**, and **c**. Then, take the length of the shorter line **b**-**c**, place it with point **b** onto point **a**, and angle it down with point **c** toward the horizontal line **a**-**b**-**c**...

<summary>This page explains how to determine line proportions using geometric progressions (bases of 2 and 3) and introduces a method for finding a third proportional line. Dürer emphasizes the practical application of these mathematical principles for stonemasons and architects.</summary>

<keywords>geometric progression, even and odd numbers, stonemasons, proportional lines, Albrecht Dürer, irrational numbers, architectural drafting</keywords>

--- Page 51 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the arithmetic progressions of lines discussed on the previous page into the practical geometric construction of proportional lines. Dürer is demonstrating how to find a "mean proportional"—a middle value in a geometric sequence—without using calculations, which was essential for artists and masons who lacked advanced mathematical training.</meta>

...and then draw a straight line from the inclined line starting at point **c** to point **b** on the horizontal line; this <term>diagonal line</term> <note>original: "ortlini," literally a 'corner-line' or hypotenuse</note> forms a triangle **a-b-c**. But let the aforementioned shorter line **b-c** extend straight out as far as you will need it. Then draw a matching <term>parallel line</term> <note>original: "barlini," a term Dürer used for parallel lines, likely derived from 'par-line'</note> against the diagonal line **b-c** starting from point **c** of the <term>horizontal line</term> <note>original: "zwerch lini"</note>. Where this diagonal line intersects the extended <term>base line</term> <note>original: "leient lini," meaning a line that lies flat or horizontal</note> **b-c**, place a **d**. In this way, the line **c-d** will be a proportional line compared to the two given lines **a-b-c**, and yet it is the smallest. It relates to the middle line exactly as the middle line relates to the larger one. For the two parallel lines **c-d** and **b-c** divide these lines proportionally. This is a most useful thing to know and is applicable to many things.

---

<note>A geometric construction diagram shows a triangle labeled with points a, b, and c. A diagonal line extends from point c, and a perpendicular line segment is drawn to illustrate the proportional relationship. Text labels within the diagram describe the lines as: "The smallest d," "the short b," and "The longest a." This illustrates a geometric progression where a:b = b:d.</note>

It is also possible to divide [a space] by a straight and curved line using curved lines in the following way: Draw an <term>arc</term> <note>original: "zirckeltrum," literally a 'piece of a circle'</term> **a-b**, and then draw a straight line connecting **a** and **b**. Divide this straight line with two points, **1** and **2**, into three equal parts. Place one foot of the <term>compass</term> <note>original: "zirckel"</note> at point **b** and with the other foot draw a curve above through **a**. Then, let the foot of the compass remain still at point **b**, but close the compass slightly and draw an arc from point **1** through the arc **a-b**. Do the same starting from point **2**. Thus **b** remains the <term>center</term> <note>original: "Centrum"</note> for all three arcs, as I have <term>drawn</term> <note>original: "aufgeryßen," a term used by craftsmen for technical drafting</note> below. This method of division is useful for many purposes.

<note>A geometric diagram illustrates the division of a curved line using a compass. A vertical line connects points 'a' and 'b' and is marked with points '1' and '2' to show equal thirds. Three concentric arcs are drawn from the fixed center point 'b' passing through 'a', '1', and '2' respectively, demonstrating how to maintain proportional spacing across a curve.</note>

--- Page 53 ---
<meta>This page transitions from Dürer's discussion of lines and proportional constructions to the second book of his manual, which focuses on two-dimensional planes and geometry. He establishes the transition from one-dimensional lines to two-dimensional surfaces, which will eventually lead to three-dimensional solids.</meta>

# Here follows the second little book concerning
## plane surfaces.

Since I have previously shown how certain lines should be drawn, I will now—as I mentioned at the beginning—proceed to <term>planes</term> <note>Dürer uses the term "planos."</note> or plane figures, and teach how to construct some of those many figures. However, so that one may understand what a <term>plane figure</term> <note>original: "ebne figur"</note> is: it is something that is defined and set apart by lines, yet does not yet enclose a <term>solid body</term> <note>original: "Corpus." In geometry, a "body" refers to a three-dimensional object.</note>. Such figures are drawn partly with straight lines, partly with curved ones, and some with straight and curved lines joined together. And in the same way that lines enclose a plane, so do planes enclose <term>solids</term> <note>original: "Corpora"</note>. Now, it is known through Euclid <note>The ancient Greek "Father of Geometry," whose work *Elements* was the standard textbook for the subject.</note> that two straight lines cannot enclose a plane; therefore, they do not form a figure, because they do not create a whole. If one draws them parallel, they remain open on both sides; if one draws them so that they converge at one end, they remain open at the other, as I have sketched below using two pairs of lines, **a-b** and **c-d**, as well as two others, **e-f** and **g-h**, which form a point.

<note>A diagram illustrates open geometric lines. On the left are two parallel horizontal lines labeled **a** and **b** for the top line, and **c** and **d** for the bottom line. To the right are two lines that converge at a point on the right, labeled **e** and **g** on the left ends and meeting at **f** and **h** on the right.</note>

But if two curved lines—one being **a** and the other **b**—are drawn with their hollow sides facing each other, they enclose a figure. They also enclose a figure if the outward curve is drawn against the inward curve; this figure takes the shape of the <term>new moon</term> <note>original: "newen Monscheins." In geometry, this crescent shape is called a lune.</note>. Likewise, if a curved line **d** is drawn over a straight line **c**, they also enclose a figure. Furthermore, a curved line that encloses a field <note>meaning a bounded area or plane</note> forms no corners, even if the drawing is elongated. Conversely, no plane surrounded by straight lines can be made without corners or angles. The same is true for entire <term>solids</term> <note>original: "Corporibus"</note>, as I have sketched below.

<note>A series of geometric figures is constructed from curved and straight lines. At the top left is a lens-shaped figure (often called a *vesica piscis*) formed by two intersecting arcs, labeled **a** and **b**. To its right is a semicircle resting on a horizontal diameter line, labeled **c** and **d**. Below these are two larger figures: a crescent moon shape (a lune) on the left and a perfect circle on the right.</note>

<summary>Dürer introduces the second book of his treatise, moving from the study of lines to the study of plane surfaces (two-dimensional geometry). He explains the fundamental rules for how lines (straight or curved) must be joined to enclose a space and form a figure.</summary>
<keywords>Albrecht Dürer, plane geometry, Euclid, geometric figures, lune, vesica piscis, curved lines, straight lines, solids, Corpora</keywords>

--- Page 54 ---
<meta>This page continues Albrecht Dürer’s geometric instructions from the second book of his manual. Having established that lines alone do not enclose a plane, he now moves to the fundamental components of plane figures: angles and corners, explaining their definitions and how to construct them using a compass and straightedge.</meta>

But before I begin to construct the figures, I wish first to speak a little concerning **angles**.
First, it should be noted that an **angle** and a **corner** share the same lines. But the difference between an <term>angle</term> <note>original: "winckel"</note> and a <term>corner</term> <note>original: "eck"</note> in practice is this: When you look at the sharpness from the outside, it is called a **corner**; but if you look into the depth from the inside, it is called an **angle**. I have sketched this below, placing an **a** at the exterior **corner** and a **b** in the interior **angle**.

---

<note>A geometrical diagram, labeled with the number '3', depicts a right angle formed by a vertical line meeting a horizontal line. The interior space of the angle is labeled "b angle" (winckel) and the exterior point of the vertex is labeled "a corner" (eck).</note>

One should also note that there are three kinds of angles and corners: a **right** angle, a **wide** angle, and a **narrow** angle. These are easily made in this way:
First, for the **right angle**, draw two straight lines roughly crosswise over one another; and at the point where they intersect, place an **a**. Then take a <term>compass</term> <note>original: "zirckel"</note>, set one foot in the **a**, and with the other, draw an arc through three points of the straight lines. Where they are intersected, place **b**, **c**, and **d**, and join **b-c** and **c-d** together with straight lines; thus **b-c-d** is a **right-angled square** <note>original: "winckelhacken." Dürer refers here to a "carpenter's square" or a perfect 90-degree tool.</note>. After that, extend the line **c-d** further out to the end **e**, and tilt the upright line with the end **b** toward the **d**; thus two kinds of angles are produced: a **narrow** <note>In modern geometry, an acute angle.</note> angle between **b** and **d**, and a **wide** <note>In modern geometry, an obtuse angle.</note> angle between **b** and **e**. For whatever is taken from one part is added to the other. I have sketched this below.

---

<note>Geometrical diagrams labeled with the number '4'. The left diagram shows the construction of a right angle using a compass arc centered at point 'a', intersecting a horizontal line at points 'c' and 'd', with a vertical line 'a-b'. The right diagram demonstrates the creation of an acute angle (labeled "narrow" or "enger" in the text) and an obtuse angle (labeled "wide" or "weytter") by tilting the vertical line 'b' towards 'd'. Points 'e', 'f', and 'g' mark the extended horizontal and tilted lines used for the construction.</note>

One should also know that when two straight lines are drawn equally crosswise through one another, they produce four **right angles**. However, if they are drawn through one another unequally, two kinds of angles are produced; those that stand opposite one another are always equal, whether they are wide or narrow, as is sketched below.

---

<note>Two diagrams of intersecting lines labeled with the number '5'. The left diagram shows two perpendicular lines forming four equal right angles, labeled "equiangular" (gleich wincklich). The right diagram shows two lines intersecting at an oblique angle, creating two pairs of vertical angles labeled "narrow" (eng) at the top and bottom, and "wide" (weit) on the left and right.</note>

These three kinds of angles may also be drawn with curved lines: **outward-curving** <note>convex</note>, as when two circles pass through one another; or **inward-curving** <note>concave</note> and hollow, as when two cones touch one another on the outside at a single point, they create two such angles. The result also varies with small and large <term>compass-strokes</term> <note>original: "zirckelryssen"</note> that are drawn together into an angle. Angles are also drawn with compass-strokes inside one another, which take a form like those seen above; these may be made with equal compass-strokes...

Further, it is known that no plane can be enclosed with fewer than three straight lines. For at the least, three enclose a **three-cornered figure** <note>original: "dreyecket figur"</note>. But **triangles** <note>original: "dreyangel"</note> made of straight lines are of three kinds: the first is of three equal sides; it has three equal corners or angles <note>An equilateral triangle.</note>. The second is of two equal and one unequal side; it has two equal and one unequal angle <note>An isosceles triangle.</note>. The third triangle has three unequal sides and three unequal angles <note>A scalene triangle.</note>. <meta>This page continues Dürer’s classification of geometric shapes. Having previously discussed triangles, he now moves to "fierungen" (four-sided figures or quadrilaterals). ...four equal sides and is drawn in the manner of a **rhombus**; it possesses two types of **angles**, and those that stand opposite one another are equal to each other. Such figures may be drawn in many ways, such as with curved lines bent outward or inward, as I have sketched below to be drawn in various ways, labeled in the order of **a, b, c** one after another.

<meta>This page continues from the previous discussion of various four-sided figures, moving now into the practical geometry of how to construct regular polygons—polygons with equal sides and angles—using a compass and straightedge within a circle.</meta>

<margin>9</margin>
Now I will show how one should make equal-sided 
figures on a plane, whether even or odd—such as three, four, five, six-
sided figures, and so on. First, make a six-sided 
figure <note>A hexagon.</note>, because the <term>compass</term> <note>original: "zirckel"</note> gives this of its own accord without being adjusted. Take a compass, 
place one foot in a <term>center point</term> <note>original: "Centrum"</note> **a**, and with the other foot 
draw a circular line, large or small, as you wish to have it. Then, if you 
place the compass with one foot on the circular line and step around the 
circle with both feet of the compass, there will be six points, which you should mark with 
numbers: **1, 2, 3,** etc. Thereafter, join the points **1** to **2**, and **2** to **3**, and **3** to **4**, 
etc., together with straight lines; thus a six-sided figure will be formed from it, 
and it cannot fail. For from the center **a** to the circular line is 
one-sixth of the circumference; likewise, one of the sides between the numbers is also 
one-sixth, as I have drawn here below.

<image-desc>A circular diagram showing the construction of a hexagon. The center is labeled 'a', and the circumference is marked with points 1 through 6. Radii connect the center to points 2 and 6. A complete hexagon is inscribed within the circle.</image-desc>

<margin>10</margin>
Now it follows next that one should enclose a 
triangle within a circle by means of the hexagon. This I do thus: I take the 
previously made circle with the six points and join 
the points **1** to **3**, and **3** to **5**, and **5** to **1** together with straight lines. 
Thus it results that the triangle touches the circular line 
and is equal on all sides, as is drawn here.

<image-desc>A circular diagram showing an equilateral triangle inscribed within a circle. The vertices of the triangle are at points 1, 3, and 5 of the previously established hexagonal points. The center is labeled 'a'.</image-desc>

<margin>11</margin>
Now, through the previous triangle and from its 
description, I will make a seven-sided figure <note>A heptagon.</note> by a common method 
that is used in practice for the sake of <term>efficiency</term> <note>original: "behendigkeyt," implying a quick practical approximation rather than a perfect Euclidean proof.</note>. 
I do it thus: I draw a straight line from the center **a** to the 
point **2**; this bisects the side of the triangle **1** to **3** in the middle. 
In that same point, I place a **b**. Thus the length from **1** to **b** goes seven 
times around, as is shown above in the figure and also 
drawn here below, with the corners joined together with straight lines.

<image-desc>A circular diagram showing the construction of a heptagon. A radius from center 'a' passes through point 2 to bisect the chord between points 1 and 3 at a point marked 'b'. The length from 1 to 'b' is used to mark seven points around the circle to form the heptagon.</image-desc>

<margin>12 13</margin>
Now it follows next that one should make a 14-sided figure 
out of the 7-sided figure. Do it thus: take the segment of the circle in the 7-sided figure 
between **1** and **2** and divide that into two equal parts; then step 
around the circular line with that length. This will give you 14 points; join 
them together with straight lines, and a 14-sided figure will be formed from it, 
as I have drawn here. By this same aforementioned way, one may 
divide it into 28 parts and double it further.

<image-desc>A circular diagram showing a 14-sided polygon (tetradecagon) inscribed in a circle. Points around the circumference are labeled with various numbers (1, 3, 4, 5, 6, 8, 9, 10, 11, 13, 14). Construction arcs are shown above the top of the circle near point 1. The center is labeled 'a'.</image-desc>

Now I will make a four-sided figure <note>A square.</note> in a circle, thus: I draw a 
circular line from a center **a**, and through the center **a** I draw a straight horizontal line; and where this cuts through the circular line on both 
sides, there I place **b** and **c**. After that, I draw an upright line out of the center **a**...

Now it is appropriate next to make an eight-sided figure; do it thus: Take the previously made drawing of the circle and keep within it the side **b.d.**, and divide the circular line between them into two parts; at this point place an **f**. Thereafter, draw **f.d.** straight together; this becomes one side of an eight-sided figure <note>An octagon.</note> around the circle. Now it is necessary to make a five-sided figure in a circle; do it thus: Draw from a **center point** <note>original: "Centrum"</note> **a** a circle, and draw a <term>horizontal line</term> <note>original: "zwerchlini"</note> through the center **a**, and where it cuts through the circular line on both sides, place **b** and **c**. Thereafter, draw through the center **a** an upright line at right angles, and where it likewise cuts the circular line, place a **d**. Thereafter, divide **a.c.** with a point **e** into two equal parts. <insert>Drawe a straight line **e.d.**</insert> and take a <term>compass</term> <note>original: "zirckel"</note>, place one foot in the point **e**, the other in the **d**, and draw from there down onto the horizontal line **b.c.**; where it cuts through, place an **f**, and draw **f.d.** straight together. This length **f.d.** is one side of a five-sided figure <note>A pentagon.</note> that serves around the circle; likewise, **f.a.** is one side of a ten-sided figure. <meta>This page continues the sequence of geometric constructions for regular polygons. Having covered the square, octagon, and hexadecagon on the previous page, the author now transitions to the construction of a pentagon and a fifteen-sided figure (pentadecagon).</meta>

### 15

But to make a **pentagon** <note>A five-sided polygon.</note> with an **unmoved compass** <note>A "fixed" compass construction, where the radius of the compass is not adjusted during the process.</note>, do it thus: Draw two circles through one another, so that the circumference of each goes through the other's center. Join the two centers **a** and **b** with a straight line; this will be the length of one side of the pentagon. Where the circular lines intersect one another, place a **c** at the top and a **d** at the bottom, and draw a straight line **c.d.** After that, take the unmoved compass and place one foot at point **d**, and with the other draw an arc through the two circles and their two centers **a** and **b**. Where the two circular lines are intersected, place **e** and **f**. But where the vertical line **c.d.** is intersected, place a **g**. Afterward, draw a straight line from **e** through **g** all the way out to the circle's line; there place an **h**. Then draw another straight line from **f** through **g** to the circle’s line; there place an **i**. Next, draw **i.a.** and **h.b.** straight together; these will be three sides of the pentagon. From there, let two sides of equal length from **i** and **h** meet at the top; this becomes a pentagon, as I have drawn below.

### 16

<image-desc>A detailed geometric diagram showing the construction of a pentagon inscribed in a circle. It utilizes three intersecting circles of equal size. Points are labeled with lowercase letters: 'a' and 'b' represent the centers of the two base circles; 'c' and 'd' are the top and bottom intersections of these circles; 'e' and 'f' are further intersection points on the arcs; 'g' is an intersection on the central vertical line; 'h' and 'i' are vertices on the primary circle.</image-desc>

From this pentagon, it is possible to make—with the help of the previously made **triangle** <note>original: "dreyangel"</note>—a **fifteen-sided figure** <note>A pentadecagon.</note>; do it thus: Draw a circle from the center **a**, and draw within it the side of a triangle with **b** at the top and **c** at the bottom. Then take the length of the side of a pentagon and place one end on point **b**, and place the other end on the circular line; there place a **d**. This leaves a portion remaining between **d** and **c**. Divide that same **arc** <note>original: "zirckeltrum"</note> into two equal parts with a point **e**. If you then join **e** and **c** together with a straight line, it becomes one side of a 15-sided figure that goes around the circle, as I have drawn below.

### 17

<image-desc>At the top of the page, centered around the number "17", are two geometric diagrams. The diagram on the left illustrates a circle with construction arcs (vesica piscis) and points labeled a, b, c, d, e, f, g. A **nine-sided figure** <note>An enneagon.</note> is to be found by means of a triangle, as follows: From a **center** <note>original: *Centrū*</note> **a**, draw a large circular line. Inside it, with an **unmoved compass** <note>A "fixed" compass construction, where the opening (radius) of the compass is not changed during the process.</note>, draw three **fish bladders** <note>original: *fischblosen*. This is the historical term for the *vesica piscis*, the almond-shaped intersection of two circles.</note>; let the upper end at the circular line be **b**, and the other ends on the sides be **c** and **d**. After that, draw an upright straight line **b.a.** in the upper fish bladder. Divide this line with two points, **1** and **2**, into three equal fields, so that **2** is the point closest to **a**. Pass a straight cross-line through point **2** at right angles to **b.a.** Where this line intersects the bladder lines on both sides, place **e** and **f**. After that, take a compass, place one foot in the center **a** and the other in point **e**, and draw a circular line all the way around through **f**. The length **e.f.** will then go nine times around in this circular drawing. I have drawn this out below.

<image-desc>A large geometric construction diagram for an enneagon, labeled with "18". The primary diagram on the left consists of a circle containing three large overlapping arcs (vesica piscis) forming a trefoil shape, with intersection points and centers labeled a, b, e, f, z, g. To its right is a smaller circle containing the resulting nine-sided polygon (enneagon), with its vertices numbered 1 through 9.</image-desc>

If I wish to quickly draw an **eleven-sided figure** <note>A hendecagon.</note> in a circle, I take a quarter of the circle’s **diameter** <note>The straight line passing from side to side through the center of a circle.</note> and extend it by an eighth part of its own length. I go around the circle with this length, and it fits approximately <note>original: *bei leüftig*</note>, so that it is found **mechanically** <note>original: *Mechanice*. This refers to a practical, "good enough" construction for craftsmen.</note> but not **demonstratively** <note>original: *demonstratiue*. This means the construction is not mathematically exact or provable by rigorous geometry.</note>. Furthermore, if I am to nimbly make a **thirteen-sided figure** <note>A tridecagon.</note>, I draw from a center **a** a circular line. After that, I draw a half...

### 19

In any circular arc <term>segment of a circle; original: *trum*</term> that comes before me, I divide it into 3 parts as follows: Let the circular arc be **a.b.**, joined with a straight line. As I have taught before, I divide the straight line **a.b.** with two points, **c.** and **d.**, into three equal fields. After that, I set a compass with one foot on point **a.** and with the other I draw an arc from point **c.** through the circular line; where it is intersected, I place an **e.** After that, I set the compass with one foot on point **b.** and with the other I draw from point **d.** through the circular line; where it is intersected, I place an **f.** After that, I draw two upright lines from **c.** and **d.** up to the circular line; there I place **g.** and **h.** Thus, the three lengths in the circular arc **a.e.g.h.** and **f.b.** will be equal to one another, and two narrow parts **e.g.** and **h.f.** remain over. After that, I take a compass, set it with one foot on point **a.** and the other on point **g.**, and from there I draw down to the straight line **a.b.**; there I place an **i.** After that, I set the compass with one foot on point **b.** and draw with the other from point **h.** onto the line **a.b.**; there I place a **k.** After that, I divide **c.i.** and **k.d.**, as I taught before, with two points into 3 parts, and set the compass with one foot on point **a.** and the other on the nearest point by the **i.**, and draw to the circular line; there I place an **l.** After that, I set the compass with one foot on the **b.** and the other on the nearest point by the **k.**, and draw from there to the circular line; there I place an **m.** Thus, the circular line **a.b.** is divided into 3 parts with the two points **l.** and **m.**, as I have sketched below. If anyone wishes to have it more precisely, let him seek it by formal proof <note>original: *demonstratiue*</note>.

<image-desc>A geometric construction diagram for trisecting a circular arc. It shows an arc above a horizontal line segment a-b, with various construction lines and points labeled a, e, g, i, j, l, c, d, k, δ, h, m, f, and b.</image-desc>

If one wishes to use circular lines in floors <term>pavement or screed; original: *estrichē*</term> or for a wall, one may join them together in two ways. First, through right-angled squares. Second, through diamond-shaped squares <note>original: *ranten fierung*. This refers to a grid of lozenges or rhombuses.</note>.

--- Page 62 ---
<meta>Continuing from the previous page, Dürer has finished the construction of a tridecagon (13-sided figure) and is now transitioning into more complex ornamental patterns created by intersecting circles, which were highly useful for stone masons and artists of the Renaissance.</meta>

First, make a proper square <term>original: *firung*; a four-sided figure with equal sides and angles</term> with equal sides and angles, and divide it with four straight lines—vertical and horizontal—into 9 small squares. Set a center point in each one. Take a compass, place one foot in these points one after another, and open the other foot just far enough so that it touches the four sides of its square. Draw a circle inside; thus, one circular line will touch four others. Also, there will always remain four-cornered, hollow, cut-out fields between four circular lines.

Secondly, if one places the circular lines in a diamond pattern <term>original: *rautens weyß*; like a rhombus</term> against each other, there will always remain hollow, cut-out three-cornered fields between three circular lines. Do it like this: draw a square **1.2.3.4.** four right triangles high, standing with sides and points upon each other, and three triangles wide, touching at their corners. Thus, the whole square contains 24 triangles—half and whole. Mark the triangles at the horizontal lines that they intersect, at their corners, with **a. b. c.** up to **r.** Thereafter, set the compass with one foot in the points of the letters and open the other foot to the width of half a side of the triangle. Draw a circular line from every letter point as it occurs; thus, there will be 7 whole circles and 10 halves, which makes twelve whole circles in total. And wherever many circles of this measure are placed together, six will always touch the seventh <note>This describes a standard hexagonal tiling or "flower of life" pattern, common in sacred geometry.</note>.

One may also draw circular lines through each other in many ways and make many things from them. Of these, I will now show one or three of nearly the same type, from which one may derive further designs. I draw from a center <term>original: *Centru*</term> **a.** a circular line, which I graduate with 12 points into equal parts. From each point, without moving the compass <term>original: *vnuerzuckte cirkel*; using a fixed compass width</term>, I draw a line that touches the center **a.**; thus, 12 circular arcs intersect at the thirteenth center **a.** Thereafter, I draw four other circular lines inside the large circular line from the center **a.**, which pass through the points where the other circular lines close, which have their marks **a. b. c. d. e.** &c.

Whoever wishes to draw a 6-pointed star within a circle, let him do it with a fixed compass as follows: Draw a circular line from a center **a.** and place one foot of the compass at the top of the circle at point **b.** With the other foot, draw a circular arc through the center **a.** from one end of the circle to the other; there, place **g.** and **c.** Thereafter, place one foot of the compass at point **g.** and with the other draw from point **b.** through the center **a.** to the circular line; there, place an **f.** Thereafter, place the compass with one foot in point **c.** and draw with the other from **b.** through **a.** to the circular line; there, place a **d.** Thereafter, place the compass with one foot in point **d.** and with the other draw from **c.** through **a.** to the circular line; there, place an **e.** Thereafter, place the compass with one foot in point **e.** and draw with the other from point **f.** through **a.** into **d.** Thereafter, place the compass with one foot in point **f.** and draw with the other foot from **g.** through **a.** into **c.**; so it is done. After that, you may adjust the compass <term>original: *verzucken*; change the width or radius</term> and draw small circular lines between the letters.

Also another method: draw a circular line from a center **a.** and divide it with four points **a. b. c. d.** into four equal parts, so that **b.** is at the top, **c.** at the bottom, and **d.** and **e.** are on both sides. Thereafter, divide **b. d.** with a point **o.**, and **b. e.** with a point **q.**, and **e. c.** with a point **p.**, and **c. d.** with a point **r.**—each into two equal parts. Thereafter, take a compass, place it with one foot in point **b.**, and with the other foot draw from **f.** through **a.** into **g.** Thereafter, place the compass with one foot in **c.** and draw with the other foot **f. a. i.** together. Thereafter, place the compass in **e.** and draw with the other foot **m. a. n.** together. Thereafter, place the compass with one foot in point **d.** and with the other draw **k. a. l.** together. Thereafter, place the compass in point **o.** and draw with the other foot through **a.** on both sides to the circular line, and in those same points between **b.** and **m.** place an **f.**, and between **d.** and **h.** place a **t.** Thereafter, place the compass in point **p.** and draw with the other foot through **a.** on both sides of the circular line; in those same points, place a **v.** between **l.** and **c.**, and an **r.** between **g.** and **e.** Thereafter, place the compass in point **r.** and draw with the other foot through **a.** on both sides into the circular line; in those same points place a **y.** between **f.** and **d.**, and a **z.** <note>original: *ʒ*; Dürer uses the archaic character for 'z'.</note> between **c.** and **n.** Thereafter, take the compass, place it in point **q.**, and draw with the other foot through **a.** on both sides to the circular line; in those same points place an **a. 1.** between **k.** and **b.**, and an **a. 2.** between **e.** and **i.** Thereafter, draw the two lines in all the pointed leaves <term>original: *spitzigen bletteren*; almond-shaped forms created by intersecting arcs, similar to the Gothic *vesica piscis*</term> that have been formed by the circular lines, as follows: place the compass with one foot in point **g.** and draw with the other the first line in the pointed leaf toward **f. a. 1.** Thereafter, place the compass in...

...point **10.** and draw with the other foot from the center **a.** on the aforementioned sheet the other arc <term>original: *ryß*; a line, drawing, or arc</term>; do the same through all six pointed petals <term>original: *bletter*; literally "leaves," describing the petal-like shapes in a geometric rose pattern</term>, and find the centers of these arcs in the points marked by the letters on the pointed petals.

There are also two kinds of stars to be drawn from a pentagon <term>a five-sided figure</term> and a heptagon <term>a seven-sided figure</term>. Proceed as follows: draw a circle around a pentagon and also around a heptagon. Thereafter, place one foot of the compass in one of the points located on the circle and open the other foot far enough that it touches the two nearest points on the sides. Draw an arc from one to the other and continue in this manner all the way around; do this for both the five-part and seven-part figures. Thus you will see how these arcs form two stars.

As I have drawn all of this below, you will be prompted by these examples to create many more designs. There is also much unusual tracery <term>original: *maſwerck*; the ornamental stone patterns found in Gothic windows</term> to be drawn through the segments <term>original: *drümer*; portions or fragments</term> of the circle.

--- Page 64 ---
<meta>The previous page concluded instructions for drawing star patterns inside pentagons and heptagons using a compass. This page transitions from circular geometry to the practical application of tiling "angular figures" (polygons) for decorative surfaces like floors.</meta>

Now I wish to arrange several angular figures together, illustrating how they might be incorporated into a floor <term>original: *estrich*; a finished pavement or plaster floor</term>. First, the triangles were shown earlier alongside the circular lines <note>Referring to the diagrams where triangles and hexagons are inscribed in circles</note>, but I will now draw <term>original: *aufreyssen*; to draft or scribe a geometric construction</term> them outside of the circle lines and demonstrate how to join them together in a different manner.

First, do this: I join 6 triangles with their corners at a center **a.** Next, I place similar triangles on the outside of each side of these six triangles. Then I connect the outer corners of the triangles with lines; these also become triangles. Afterward, I again place triangles on the sides, and so on and so forth.

Furthermore, one can arrange the triangles in such a way that no gap <term>original: *felt*; field or empty space</term> remains between them, even if the triangles are shifted—so that the corner of one triangle always meets the middle of the side of another. When one joins 6 triangles by their tips, a hexagon <term>original: *6.eck*</term> is formed. After that, one may attach triangles all around it.

<summary>Albrecht Dürer explains the transition from circular-based geometry to practical tiling patterns using triangles. He describes how to radiate triangles from a center point or shift them to create a seamless, gapless pavement design that forms hexagonal structures.</summary>
<keywords>Albrecht Dürer, geometry, tiling, tessellation, triangles, hexagon, pavement design, drafting techniques, decorative arts</keywords>

--- Page 65 ---
<meta>This page continues Dürer’s instructions on floor tiling (tessellation). The previous page concluded with the arrangement of equilateral triangles to form hexagonal patterns and staggered pavements. This page moves on to the geometric arrangement of squares and rhombi.</meta>

Likewise, if one joins right squares <term>original: *recht firung*; squares with four right angles</term> together, they remain consistent with one another, except that one may set them diagonally <note>literally "over the corner"</note> or crosswise, or one may shift <insert>stagger</insert> them just as stonemasons join their ashlar blocks <term>original: *quader*; squared building stones</term>, as is drafted below.

But for the lozenge squares <term>original: *rauten firung*; rhombi or diamond shapes</term>, which have two wide angles and two narrow ones opposite each other, arrange them together in two ways. Firstly, so that they are all divided by the St. Andrew’s cross <note>original: *Endres creütz*; an X-shaped cross, referring here to the grid pattern formed where the points of four rhombi meet</note>. Secondly, place two with their sides against each other and push the third in crosswise, so that it looks like a squared cube <note>Dürer is describing the "isometric illusion" where three rhombi joined at a point appear to be a three-dimensional cube</note>. In this manner, join them together further and further. It also produces a star if one joins 8 rhombi with their pointed corners together; one may also do this with six or five, as is drafted hereafter.

<summary>Albrecht Dürer describes practical methods for tiling floors using squares and rhombi. He explains how squares can be staggered like masonry and how rhombi can be arranged to create a three-dimensional cube illusion or star patterns.</summary>
<keywords>Albrecht Dürer, geometry, tiling, tessellation, square, rhombus, stonemasonry, ashlar, St. Andrew’s cross, optical illusion, star patterns, floor design</keywords>
