<meta>This page continues Dürer’s geometric exploration of floor tilings. While the previous page dealt with squares and rhombi (four-sided shapes), this page begins the study of polygons with five, six, seven, and eight sides, focusing specifically on the complex challenges of tiling with pentagons.</meta>

<page-type>text</page-type>
<sig>z 4</sig>

Furthermore, I want to assemble five-, six-, seven-, and eight-sided shapes <term>original: *eck*; literally "corners," referring here to polygons</term>, each separately, yet in different ways from one another.

For a pattern, first place three pentagons <term>original: *fünfeck*</term> with their sides along a horizontal line <term>original: *zwerch lini*</term>, so that they touch each other at their corners. Then, place three more pentagons opposite them below, joined by their sides to the upper ones, which also touch each other at their corners. In this way, elongated diamond shapes <term>original: *ablang rauten firung*; elongated rhombi</term> are formed between the pentagons. Do this once more and always push two such parts together, the corners of one into the angles of the other. This is the first method and the closest possible assembly of pentagons. <note>Dürer is demonstrating that regular pentagons do not tile perfectly; they leave gaps shaped like diamonds.</note>

Next, make the two assembled parts of the pentagons as before, and then join them at their corners so that each pentagon touches four other pentagons with one side and three corners. Thus, two types of diamond shapes will be formed between the pentagons: narrow ones where the sides meet closely, and wider ones between the corners. This can be extended as far as one desires.

For the fifth arrangement, assemble the pentagons as follows. First, make one pentagon and place an identical pentagon against each of its sides. Then, against each of those five pentagons, place two more pentagons, each against two of their sides; this creates five narrow diamond shapes between the pentagons. Then push pentagons into the angles all around that have been formed, so that their corners touch the narrow diamond shapes. Continue doing this for as long as you wish.

You may also assemble five "roses" <note>Dürer uses "roses" to describe a circular or radial arrangement of shapes around a central point</note> from the pentagons, which all touch one another, and then link them together in reverse as often as you like, filling the remaining field with whatever you wish. All of this is drafted <note>original: *aufgerissen*; sketched or drawn as a technical diagram</note> below.

<summary>Albrecht Dürer explores the geometric possibilities of tiling with pentagons, acknowledging that they do not fit together perfectly and instead create patterns of diamond-shaped gaps or "roses."</summary>
<keywords>Albrecht Dürer, geometry, pentagons, tiling, tessellation, rhombi, roses, pattern design, Underweysung der Messung</keywords>

Furthermore <note>original: *Item*; a common Latin transition word used in German texts of this period to mean "likewise" or "next item."</note>, I want to assemble the pentagons <term>original: *fünff eck*; literally "five-corners"</term> in three different ways. First, I fit them into one another so that all sides, corners, and angles touch each other, and no empty space <term>original: *lers von feldt*; literally "void of field"</term> remains in between. <note>Mathematically, regular pentagons cannot tile a flat plane perfectly by themselves; Dürer is illustrating a "tiling" where very thin diamond shapes (rhombi) are used as spacers to create a solid, gapless floor pattern, as seen in the first image description.</note>

<meta>This page continues Albrecht Dürer’s study of polygonal tilings from his "Instruction in Measurement." While the previous page focused on the pentagon, this page concludes the discussion on hexagons (six-sided shapes) and begins an exploration of heptagons (seven-sided shapes).</meta>

Secondly, I place them together so that they touch each other only at their corners; thus, triangles <term>original: *dreyangel*</term> remain between the six corners <note>original: *sechs ecken*; referring to hexagons. Dürer is describing a vertex-to-vertex arrangement where the gaps form equilateral triangles.</note>.

Thirdly, I place them together such that each one touches the others with two sides and two corners, as I have drawn in what follows.

<image-desc>Three woodcut diagrams illustrating hexagonal tilings. The top left shows a standard honeycomb pattern where hexagons share all sides. The top right shows hexagons meeting only at their vertices, creating triangular gaps between them. The middle diagram shows hexagons arranged so they share two sides and two vertices with neighbors, creating a different interlocking pattern.</image-desc>

I want to assemble the heptagons <term>original: *sibeneck*; literally "seven-corners"</term> in two ways. Firstly, so that they touch each other with their corners; thus, triangles and four-pointed stars <note>original: *vierecket stern*; these are likely diamond-shaped gaps or quadrilaterals resulting from the vertex-to-vertex arrangement.</note> remain between them. In these stars, one makes a quadrangle <term>original: *firung*; a square or rectangular form</term> that touches the corners, or draws a St. Andrew’s Cross <term>original: *Endres creütz*; an X-shaped cross</term> from one corner to the other.

Secondly, I place the sides and two corners upright upon one another, and when I place such rows next to each other, I let the corners touch each other next to the side; thus, quadrangles and the aforementioned stars remain between the heptagons, as I have drawn hereafter.

Likewise <note>original: *Item*</note>, if one places the heptagons side-to-side one after another, so that two corners project outward and one corner projects inward, they go around in a circle <term>original: *cirkel*</term> but do not close; the pentagons <term>original: *fünfeck*</term> do the same. <note>Dürer is noting a geometric fact: regular heptagons and pentagons cannot tile a plane or form a perfect closed ring without gaps or overlaps because their internal angles do not divide 360 degrees evenly.</note>

<meta>This page continues Albrecht Dürer’s geometric explorations from his 1525 manual, "Instruction in Measurement." Furthermore, I want to assemble the octagon <term>original: *acht eck*</term> in three different ways <term>original: *dreyerley weyß*</term>. Firstly, so that they touch each other with their corners and two sides; thus, triangles <term>original: *dreyangel*</term> remain between them.

Secondly, I place them together so that they touch each other with four sides and stand next to and on top of each other along a straight cross-line <term>original: *creüz lini*</term>; thus, quadrangles <term>original: *fierung*</term> appear between them, which stand cornerwise <note>original: *vber ort*; this describes squares rotated at a 45-degree angle, appearing as diamonds within the pattern.</note>.

Thirdly, I place them together so that they touch each other with four sides diagonally <note>original: *vber ort*</note>; thus, straight, upright, crosswise <term>original: *creützweiß*</term> quadrangles remain between them. I have drawn <term>original: *aufgerissen*</term> these ideas of mine below.

Such designs can be used for room tiling <term>original: *stuben tillen*</term> and pavements <term>original: *estrichen*</term>; one may also assemble all sorts of the aforementioned <term>original: *egemeltẽ*</term> figures together or skillfully intersperse them, and whatever fields remain between them <note>The text concludes mid-sentence, suggesting the thought continues on the next page regarding how to fill the remaining gaps in the patterns.</note>

...so that one divides and creates something skillful within them.
One may also assemble other irregular figures <term>original: *vngeregulirte figuren*</term> that have unequal sides, provided they are arranged in a good order; from this, many beautiful things result.
It is occasionally appropriate to shift the previously mentioned figures—such as the triangle <term>original: *dryangel*</term>, the square <term>original: *quadraten*</term>, and so on—into one another, letting them interpenetrate <note>original: *durch einander lest stechen*; literally "let them prick through each other." One may also place one figure around or inside another; this is done most skillfully when a figure with an even number of sides is paired with another even one, and an odd with an odd.
Furthermore, regarding figures with the fewest corners, one can fit the greatest number of them together within a circle <term>original: *cirkelryß*</term> so they intersect, because figures with many corners <term>original: *vilecketen*</term> tend to interfere with one another <note>Dürer is noting that simple shapes like triangles can be rotated and overlapped many times within a circle without the lines becoming a confused blur, whereas shapes with many sides (like decagons) quickly become too crowded.</note>.

<meta>This page continues Albrecht Dürer’s practical geometry manual. While the previous page discussed the visual patterns created by overlapping polygons, this page shifts toward the mathematical challenge of "squaring" shapes—specifically, how to construct a square with the exact same area as a triangle or a rectangle, and how to scale rectangles proportionally.</meta>

Furthermore, one may draw many different kinds of figures with unequal sides <term>original: *vngleychen seyten*</term> whose corners do not touch a circular line, from which many beautiful things can be made. 
One may also assemble many kinds of figures on a plane—such as triangles <term>original: *dryangel*</term>, squares <term>original: *quadrat*</term>, pentagons, hexagons, heptagons, or octagons—from which one may create many wonderful designs for floorboards and pavements <term>original: *tillen vñ estrichen*</term>, as mentioned before. One may also combine irregular and regular figures, which also produces beautiful and rare designs; this is where unusual patterns and paths come from. If I were to show all of this here, this little book would become far too long; therefore, let everyone think of it for themselves.

Now it is necessary to know a little about how to make figures equal to one another in their area <term>original: *inhaltung*</term>, so that one might make a triangle and then a square from it that contains as much area as the triangle, and the same with other regular solids.

Do it in this way: set a triangle **a.b.c.** and divide **a.c.** in the middle with a point **d.**, and draw a line **b.d.** so that the triangle is split equally in the middle. Then, if the half-part **a.b.c.d.** is turned over onto the other half-part **b.c.** and placed against the sides **a. b.**, an "overlaid quadrangle" <note>Dürer is describing how to rearrange a triangle into a rectangle of the same area.</note> is formed with equal angles **a.b.c.d.**, which contains as much area as the triangle **a.b.c.** 

Afterward, turn that "overlaid quadrangle" <note>the rectangle</note> into a proper square <term>original: *rechten firung*</term> that contains as much area as the triangle. How you shall make that square, note hereafter: take two sides—one long and one short—from the elongated quadrangle **a.b.c.d.** and join them end-to-end horizontally, marking their three points **e.f.g.** Then place a point **h.** in the middle of the line. Take a compass, set one foot in **h.** and with the other draw a circle <note>semicircle</note> from **c.** around to **g.** After that, draw a straight vertical line up from point **f.** to the circle line; where it touches it, place an **i.** If you then join four lines together at right angles, each as long as **i.f.**, a proper square will be made from them which contains as much area as the long quadrangle **a.c.d.b.**, and also contains just as much as the triangle **a.b.c.** One may also, for the sake of speed, compare a triangle and a square against each other in this way: make a square and divide one of the sides into two parts, and then make one side of the triangle one-third longer than the square, and then close the triangle—as all of this is drawn <term>original: *aufgerissen*</term> hereafter.

---

<image-desc>A geometrical diagram illustrating the conversion of a triangle into a square of equal area. At the top left, a triangle is labeled with letters a, b, c, and d. To its right, a construction involving a semicircle with center point 'h' and radius reaching points 'c' and 'g'. A vertical line segment 'f-i' is drawn from the baseline to the arc of the semicircle. ---

Likewise, if you wish to make a small or large version of an elongated quadrangle <note>a rectangle</note> so that they have the same shape <note>proportion</note> as one another, find it thus: Draw an elongated quadrangle, **a.b.** at the top and **c.d.** at the bottom, and draw a diagonal <term>original: *ortstrich*</term> from **c.** to **b.** and extend it as far out as you need it. Also extend the line **c.d.** further out past **d.** Now, if you want the quadrangle to be smaller, set a point **e.** on the horizontal line **c.d.**, moving it back from **d.** as far as you wish, and draw a vertical line up from **e.** to the diagonal <term>original: *Diameter*</term>; there, place an **f.** From there, go across with a level line to the side of the quadrangle **a.c.**; there, place a **g.** Thus, the quadrangle **g.f.c.e.** has the same proportions as the quadrangle **a.b.c.d.** If you now wish to make the quadrangle larger than the quadrangle **a.b.c.d.** is, do it in the same way outside the first established quadrangle, as you did before on the inside—as I have drawn <term>original: *aufgerissen*</term> below.

<meta>This page continues the geometric instructions of Albrecht Dürer. Having previously discussed how to transform triangles into rectangles of equal area, Dürer now moves to the "scaling" of these rectangles. He explains a geometric method for doubling or tripling the area of a rectangle while ensuring the new, larger rectangle remains "similar" (meaning it keeps the same proportional relationship between its height and width) to the original.</meta>

If you wish to make the previously shown rectangles <term>original: *fierungen*; four-sided figures</term>—which should always keep their proportions <term>original: *geſtalten*; literally "shapes" or "forms"</term>—so that they contain twice or three times as much area as they did before, proceed as follows, as was also partially mentioned earlier:

Make an oblong rectangle **a. b. c. d.**, and set it according to its length or width. Next, make two more of the same size as the first, and place one on each side. Mark the four corners of this now-lengthened rectangle as **e. f.** at the top and **e. f.** at the bottom. <note>Dürer is instructing the reader to place three identical rectangles side-by-side to create a wide base for his construction.</note> Then, divide the top line **e. f.** with a point **g.** exactly in the middle. Place one foot of a compass on point **g.** and, with the other, draw a circular arc <term>original: *cirkelryß*</term> from point **e.** upward and around to point **f.**

Afterward, extend the line **c. b.** upward, and where it intersects the circular arc, mark a point **h.** This line **b. h.** is the required length for the rectangle that is to contain twice as much area as the rectangle **a. b. c. d.** 

However, to find the correct width for this length so that the shape remains identical in proportion to the first rectangle, do as previously mentioned: Draw a diagonal line <term>original: *ortſtrich*; literally a "place-line" used to maintain geometric similarity</term> in the first rectangle from corner **d.** through corner **b.**, and then extend it further out as far as you need. 

Next, take the length **b. h.** and place one end of it at corner **d.** on the bottom horizontal line <term>original: *zwerchlini*</term> **e. f.** Where it extends past the length **d. c.**, mark a point **i.** From point **i.**, draw an upright vertical line <term>original: *aufrechten barlini*</term> upward to the diagonal line that passes through corner **b.** Where they intersect, mark a point **k.** Then, draw a horizontal line from corner **k.** across to the vertical line extended upward from **d. a.** Where these two lines meet to form a corner, mark an **l.** 

Thus, the rectangle **l. k. i. d.** contains twice as much area as the rectangle **a. b. c. d.**, and they have a proportional shape to one another.

If you then wish to triple the area <term>original: *dryfaltē*</term>, place another rectangle of the same size next to the previous three long rectangles, so that the four corners of this total length are **e. m.** and **e. m.** Then, place one foot of the compass on the top line **e. m.** at point **b.**, and with the other foot, draw a circular arc from point **e.** upward and around to point **m.** 

Then, draw an upright line from **f. f.** upward until it reaches the circular line; there, mark an **n.** This line **f. n.** is the length of the tripled rectangle. Next, take the length **f. n.** and place one end in corner **d.** on the line **e. f.**, and where it extends beyond line **i.**, mark an **o.** 

From **o.**, draw an upright line upward to the previously mentioned diagonal line <term>original: *ortlini*</term>, and where they intersect, mark a **p.** Then, draw a horizontal line from point **p.** across to the vertical line drawn upward from **d. l.**, and where they intersect, mark **q.** 

Thus, the rectangle **q. p. o. d.** contains three times as much area as the first rectangle **a. b. c. d.** and has the same proportion as the first, by virtue of the diagonal line. In this way, these things can be made larger or smaller as often as one wishes. From this, many beautiful and useful things may be discovered. 

The instructions described above are drawn out below.

...into one another. ->33<-

It would be necessary to know the **squaring of the circle** <note>original: *quadratura circuli*</note>—that is, the comparison of a circle and a square, so that one contains as much area as the other. However, this has not yet been demonstrated mechanically <note>original: *Mechanice*; here meaning a proof by practical geometric construction</note> by the learned. Nevertheless, an approximation can be made so that in practical work it is not off, or only very slightly so; this comparison may be made as follows. Draw a square and divide the diagonal <term>original: *ortstrich*; a line connecting opposite corners</term> into ten parts. Then draw a circle whose diameter <term>original: *Diameter*; the width of the circle through its center</term> shall be eight of those parts, as shown in the drawing of the quadrature <note>the process of constructing a square with the same area as a circle</note> that I have sketched below.

->34<-

If one makes a triangle of unequal sides <term>original: *dreyangel van ungleychen seyten*; a scalene triangle</term> that nevertheless has a right angle <term>original: *rechten winckel*</term>, then whatever figure one draws from those same sides (by multiplying them by themselves), the longest side—or the figure made from it—will always contain as much area as the other two sides combined <note>Dürer is describing the Pythagorean theorem ($a^2 + b^2 = c^2$), noting that it applies to any similar shapes drawn on the sides, not just squares</note>. Two types of figures for this are drawn below. First, the triangle **a. b. c.** where the sides are extended into triangles; second, **d. e. f.** where the sides are extended into squares.

<summary>Dürer demonstrates how to transform a hexagon into a square of equal area and offers a practical, though approximate, method for squaring the circle. He also illustrates the Pythagorean theorem, explaining that the area of shapes built on the legs of a right triangle equals the area of the shape built on the hypotenuse.</summary>
<keywords>hexagon, quadrature of the circle, squaring the circle, Pythagorean theorem, right triangle, geometric transformation, area calculation</keywords>

--- Page 76 ---
<meta>This page continues Dürer’s practical manual on geometry. Having previously discussed the "squaring of the circle" and the properties of triangles, Dürer now moves toward the construction and proportional division of complex curved shapes, specifically the *vesica piscis* (a lens shape formed by two intersecting circles).</meta>

35

If one encloses two concave curved lines <term>original: *cirkellini*; literally "circle-lines" or arcs</term> against each other so that the field becomes narrow and long, and if one wishes to divide and mark this off properly with horizontal lines <term>original: *zwerch linien*</term>, one must proceed as follows. Draw an upright straight line with point **a** at the top and **b** at the bottom. Then draw a horizontal line at right angles through the middle of it, and on this horizontal line, on the right side, place a point **c** as far back as you wish; likewise, place a point **d** on the left side at the same distance. Place one foot of the compass in point **c** and the other in point **a**, and from there draw an arc down to point **b**. Do the same from the other side, starting from point **d**. 

Once this is done, draw two circular lines with a compass: the first above the horizontal line and the second below it, in such a way that each touches the horizontal line at one point, and also touches the two concave side-lines on both sides. After that, draw two horizontal lines above and below these two circles. Then, draw two other smaller circles, both of which touch the two circles previously drawn (the upper and lower ones) as well as the two concave side-lines; then draw two more horizontal lines above and below them. 

Continue in this manner as far as you wish; the area will be divided proportionally <note>Dürer is describing a method of geometric progression where circles decrease in size as they approach the narrow ends of the shape</note>, just as I have sketched hereafter. From this construction, many things can be made <note>This shape, often called a *vesica piscis* or mandorla, was essential in medieval and Renaissance art for framing sacred figures and designing architectural elements like Gothic arches</note>.

36

->End of the Second Book.<-

<summary>Dürer describes the geometric construction of a lens shape (vesica piscis) and provides a method for dividing it into proportional segments using inscribed circles. This page marks the conclusion of the second book of his treatise on measurement.</summary>
<keywords>vesica piscis, proportional division, geometry, Albrecht Dürer, compass construction, mandorla, horizontal lines</keywords>

--- Page 77 ---
<meta>This page marks the transition from Dürer’s study of two-dimensional geometry—specifically the "vesica piscis" and proportional circles from the previous page—to the construction of three-dimensional solids. He begins his "Third Book" by explaining how flat shapes can be projected into the third dimension to create columns and prisms.</meta>

# The Third Book, concerning
## Solid Objects.

Now that a little has been shown previously concerning flat figures <term>original: *ebnen figuren*; two-dimensional shapes</term>, I will henceforth also deal in part with solid objects <term>original: *Corperlichen dingen*</term>; these I will first derive from the flat figures as follows.

First, I take a circular field [labeled] **b. c. d. e.**; let the center point be **a**, and I extend it straight upwards as high as I wish; thus, a round column <term>original: *seulen*; in this context, a cylinder</term> is formed from it.

Next, I take a triangular field, likewise a square <term>original: *fierung*</term>, a pentagon, and a hexagon, and also extend them straight upwards—as previously mentioned with the round field—as high as I wish. In this way, three-, four-, five-, and six-sided columns [prisms] are produced. And if I let the cornered squares in the base be shifted and intersect one another twice <note>Dürer is describing a method of creating a star-shaped base by overlapping two squares at different angles</note>, as was shown previously, and then extend this upwards, quite beautiful columns will result from it.

Furthermore, you may make these bases for the columns with as many corners as you wish; one can also make them twisted <term>original: *gewunden*; referring to a spiral or helical form</term> along their edges as they rise upwards, by turning the winding a certain amount to the side, or half as much, as I have sketched a little further on.

<summary>Dürer introduces the third book of his manual, explaining how to transform two-dimensional shapes like circles and polygons into three-dimensional columns or prisms. He also describes more complex forms, such as star-shaped or twisted columns, created by rotating or overlapping the base shapes.</summary>
<keywords>solid objects, geometry, prisms, cylinders, columns, extrusion, polygons, architectural design, Albrecht Dürer</keywords>

--- Page 78 ---
<meta>This page continues Dürer’s exploration of three-dimensional geometry. Having previously described how to extend flat shapes vertically to create prisms and cylinders (which he calls "columns"), he now explains how to taper these same shapes toward a single point to create cones and pyramids.</meta>

### Another method for making pointed solids.

I proceed upward from all the previously mentioned bases <term>original: *gründen*; the geometric foundations or base planes</term> as high as I wish toward a single point. In this way, cones <term>original: *kegel*</term> are formed, as well as three-, four-, five-, or six-sided shapes. These may be made straight <term>original: *schlecht*; simple or unadorned</term> or twisted <term>original: *gewunden*; referring to a spiral or helical construction</term>, and one may use as many corners as desired, just as was previously shown with the columns. The ancients called such things Pyramids <note>Dürer uses the term "Piramides" to encompass both pyramids with flat faces and cones with circular bases</note>. I have sketched these below.

<margin>Geometry, Cones, Pyramids, Pointed solids, Twisted, Sketched, Columns</margin>

<summary>Dürer explains the geometric construction of pyramids and cones by tapering various polygonal or circular bases to a single apex. He notes that these "pointed solids" can be either straight or spiraled, following the same principles as the prisms described previously.</summary>
<keywords>pyramids, cones, geometric solids, three-dimensional construction, Albrecht Dürer, Renaissance geometry, twisted forms</keywords>

--- Page 79 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of "pointed solids" (cones and pyramids). Having established the variety of bases they can be built upon, Dürer now explains how these shapes can be combined with his previously described "columns" (prisms and cylinders) to create architectural forms like towers, and details the three ways an apex can be aligned with its base.</meta>

If these cones <term>original: *kegel*; Dürer uses this term generally for both circular cones and polygonal pyramids</term> are placed in the proper proportion upon the previously described columns, they form the body of a tower; though one must afterwards add the ornamentation <note>Dürer suggests that geometric solids provide the underlying structural "skeleton" for architectural features like church spires or turrets, which the artist then embellishes</note>.

There are three ways to make these cones: the first is such that the apex <term>original: *spiß*; the point or vertex</term> is raised directly over the center of the base <term>original: *fues*; literally "foot"</term>.

The second is such that the apex stands directly above the edge of the base; in this way, the cone acquires a right angle <term>original: *rechten winckel*</term> at one point on its base <note>This describes a right-angled cone or pyramid where one side is perfectly vertical</note>.

The third is made so that the apex hangs out over the base, as is sketched <term>original: *aufgerissen*; a term for a technical or architectural drawing</term> below.

<sig>G ij</sig>

<summary>Dürer explains that cones and pyramids serve as the structural basis for towers and describes three types of alignment for the apex: centered, aligned with the edge, or leaning beyond the base.</summary>
<keywords>architecture, towers, apex, geometric construction, right angles, Albrecht Dürer, technical drawing</keywords>

--- Page 80 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the abstract geometry of cones and pyramids to their practical application in architecture, specifically focusing on the design of pillars and the complex vaulting systems typical of the Late Gothic style. Dürer connects the "ground plan" (drawing the base) to the "elevation" (building the structure upward).</meta>

Since I have already begun with columns <term>original: *seülen*</term>, I wish to write a little further about them. Columns may be made in many different ways, and they must be made stronger in proportion to the weight they are meant to carry. Some craftsmen give their columns bases <term>original: *füß*; literally "feet"</term> and capitals <term>original: *haubter*; literally "heads"</term>; however, some make only pillars <term>original: *pfeyler*; usually referring to sturdier, square, or polygonal piers</term> that rise directly from the ground. In the upper reaches, one divides their sections to meet the arches of the vault <term>original: *gewelb*</term>. Alternatively, one may let the pillar continue upward and have the specific decorations of the arches run into it, so that one molding <term>original: *stab*; a convex, rod-like molding</term> flows into a cove <term>original: *holkeln*; a concave, hollow molding</term>, and one opposing part intersects the other. If this is done correctly, it looks exotic and masterfully crafted <term>original: *wercklich*</term>, as skilled builders <term>original: *kunstreychen bauleut*</term> well know. In such pillars, many variations of hollows, moldings, chamfers <term>original: *fasen*</term>, edges, and angles can be used. However, these things must be properly drafted <term>original: *auf gerissen*; drawn as a two-dimensional ground plan</term> and then projected <term>original: *aufgezogen*; drawn in elevation or three dimensions</term>.

Four pillars may be set opposite one another, one of which is large, and each may have its own particular decoration in the ground plan. When these are projected upward and a vault is closed using the sections of these pillars, it looks quite wondrous. For those who have a greater love for closing vaults with rare, rich patterns <term>original: *seltzamen reychungen*</term> for the sake of elegance, I will draft one below; if anyone likes it, they may make use of it. I will also make and draft several ground plans for pillars.

Likewise, I will provide several sloped moldings <term>original: *oberschieffete gesims*</term> that are used at the bottom of the pillars, whether high or low above the pavement <term>original: *estrich*</term>. Regarding this, each person must pay attention to the correct proportions himself, for if everything were to be written out at length, this little book would become far too long. And though some ground plans are drawn with simple lines, they must nonetheless be maintained and set in their proper proportions at their base, as the drafted moldings will show hereafter. One may also make the pillars with all kinds of corners and add decoration into them as one wishes.

These lines illustrate the geometric method for drafting the complex molding profiles (holkeln, fasen, and ecke) described in the text. A small cross symbol "+" is positioned at the top center, just inside the upper boundary of the square frame.

<summary>Dürer discusses the design of architectural pillars and piers, explaining how complex moldings and vaulting ribs should transition seamlessly into one another. He emphasizes that these intricate Late Gothic forms must be precisely drafted in a ground plan before being projected into their vertical forms.</summary>
<keywords>pillars, architecture, vaulting, moldings, ground plan, technical drafting, Late Gothic, Albrecht Dürer, masonry</keywords>

--- Page 82 ---
If one wishes to speak of the entire edifice or its parts, I consider that it is not hidden from any renowned architect or craftsman how skillfully and masterfully the ancient Roman <term>Vitruvius</term> <note>Marcus Vitruvius Pollio was a 1st-century BC Roman architect whose treatise *De architectura* became the foundational text for Renaissance architecture.</note> wrote in his books concerning the stability, utility, and beauty of buildings; for this reason, he is to be followed before others, and his teachings are to be used.

However, as I now undertake to teach the making of a column or two for young apprentices to practice upon, I consider the German spirit; for usually all those who wish to build something new also desire to have a new fashion for it that has never been seen before. Therefore, I will create something different, from which each may take what pleases him and act according to his own will. I also wish to show from what the decoration of the <term>hobel</term> <note>Likely referring to the molding or planed surfaces</note> and the works may be made, and demonstrate the reasons where one should use them larger or smaller; these decorations pertain to straight, round, or curved things.

First, make a column that has a height of eight-and-a-half times its thickness measured at the bottom above the <term>fase</term> <note>A chamfer or base molding.</note>; but make the base molding one-eighth thicker than the column, and one-eighth of the column's thickness in width. Make the column one-eighth thinner at the top than at the bottom, yet make its moldings and rings project as far as the column is thick at the bottom above the lower molding, and make them as wide as they project. 

Now that the column is made and its round ground plan is drawn from a center point **a**, then create something decorative of a winding nature within it; for this, use the previously described <term>schraufen lini</term> <note>literally "screw-line," meaning a helix or spiral</note> from the first booklet <note>This refers to Dürer’s earlier work, *Underweysung der Messung* (Instruction in Measurement), published in 1525.</note>. First, a simple one with the paths, or a double one against each other; but on the column, at least eight of these paths may be used side by side. Place them in the plan view on the circular line next to each other at equal distances; draw them into the column from the bottom upwards. If they go double against each other, then sixteen lines are drawn from eight points. Such windings may be used through the entire column or allowed to end in the bottom third. These windings have many variations, and many kinds are yet to be invented. Such paths may be drawn closely together or stretched into a steep length. One may also make them narrow at the beginning at the bottom and stretch them longer the higher they go, via the 16th figure of the <term>dreyangel</term> <note>triangle</note> **a b c** of the first booklet. All such things you may use on bulging columns as well as on straight columns, whether they are uniform or small at the top and large at the bottom, provided that they are divided accordingly.

Now you shall use this aforementioned helical line for the column and begin in the ground plan: mark the round plan into as many parts as you wish and add the numbers to them, and draw a <term>zwerch lini</term> <note>transverse or horizontal line</note> through the center **a**. Begin counting the number of the aforementioned points 1, 2, 3, 4 at the end of the transverse line; from there you must bring the winding into the drawn-up column and divide it equally; do it thus: 

Move with straight lines from all points of the plan view upwards to the column, starting above the base molding, and place the points with their numbers on the column as they stand in the plan. Likewise, divide the top of the column where it is smallest into equal parts and count as you did at the bottom. Thereafter, draw straight lines on the column, connecting the upper and lower numbers one to another. Then divide the column lengthwise with fourteen transverse lines into fifteen equal fields, and begin counting the numbers from the bottom upwards: 1, 2, 3, etc. Thus the entire column will be latticed; from this alone many things can be made. I set it down here, however, so that the winding may be made around the column. 

Now begin at the very bottom of the column at the first point of the vertical line 1, and move curvedly along the column with an <term>ortlini</term> <note>a diagonal or oblique line</note> upwards into the corner where the vertical line 2 and the transverse line 1 meet. Thereafter, move conversely from the point of the vertical line 2 with a curved diagonal line along the column upwards into the corner that closes the vertical and the transverse at both parts 1. Do this through all the numbers between all the elongated squares through the entire column, or use it only in the winding on one side and not through each other, or use it only in the lowest third of the column and let straight lines run up in the upper part, or do not let them go further than the lower third of the column, yet let them stand out a little. 

In summary: you may use each of these things individually, or in part, or all together, and whether one uses the transverse lines or not, many strange things may be carved and hewn from these principles; whoever takes it in hand will surely find them. For this column, make a <term>captel</term> <note>capital</note> varied in six ways, just as it may always be changed in a hundred different ways. For this capital, make a square as thick as the column is at its top beneath its ring, and half as high; upon it lay a <term>gesirte blaten</term> <note>decorated plate or abacus</note> one-third as thick as the height of the capital. Make this plate entirely square and so wide that its sides touch the uppermost projecting molding on the capital; how far the capital shall project at the top follows hereafter. This plate may be made to intersect itself doubly, as is shown in the preceding figure, whereby the plate becomes eight-cornered and eight-angled. Likewise, whoever wishes to carve out the sides of the square plate with a compass, let him draw two cross-lines through the plate so that four right squares are within it.

<summary>Dürer discusses the application of geometric principles to architectural column design, moving beyond Vitruvian tradition to satisfy the German preference for novelty through the use of helical (screw-line) decorations and grids. He provides a step-by-step technical guide for projecting a two-dimensional ground plan onto a three-dimensional column to create complex spiral motifs.</summary>

<keywords>Vitruvius, column design, helical lines, architectural geometry, German Renaissance, spiral ornamentation, capital design, abacus, technical drawing, proportions</keywords>

--- Page 83 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical instruction on column design and geometric construction, following the introduction of Vitruvius and the basic proportions of a column from the previous page.</meta>

...where the lines cross through one another, place an **a**. From this point, draw the lines with their four positions of the diameter or center-line far outward; at those same four positions place **a, b, c, d, e**, and open a compass as wide as one side of the <term>square</term> <note>original: "fierung"</note> is long. Place one foot of the compass in each of the four letters, and with the other, draw an arc into the four sides of the square. Wherever these round marks strike outward, always cut two off at the front with a center-line, before or outside the corners of the square.

One may also create many kinds of designs in the thick <term>plates</term> <note>original: "blatten," likely referring to the abacus or plinth</note> using <term>chamfers</term> <note>original: "fasen," a flat, sloped edge</note>, <term>waves</term> <note>original: "wellen," an ogee or S-shaped molding</note>, <term>hollows</term> <note>original: "holkelen," a concave or cavetto molding</note>, and other lines, through which they are carved and something is cut into them. But whoever wishes to vary such things can always make an opposite of the previous one; mark it thus: First, carve out the plate and divide its thickness into two parts. Divide the upper part into two again; this creates two variations. From the topmost part, make a flat chamfer; in the other, make a hollow as far back as its part is high. Then, from the remaining lower half-part, make another chamfer that must be set back as far as it is high, or instead of the hollow, make a quarter-section of a wave. If you turn these both upside down, it becomes something else again. 

Another way: divide the thickness of the plate into two parts; from the uppermost, make a quarter-section of a wave, and from the lower, a chamfer; turn the bottom part upward, and it is different again. Another: cut off the top corner down to the halfway point with a quarter-section of a wave, and hollow out the bottom part entirely, as far back as it is high. Another: divide the thickness into two parts; from the uppermost, make a chamfer; from the bottom two, make a hollow that recedes as far back as it is high. Turn this over, and it is different again. Or divide the thickness into two parts; in the bottom part, make a hollow that steps back at the bottom as far as its height; divide the upper part in two—the middle remains a chamfer, and from the top, make a backward-sloping chamfer. Another: divide the thickness of the plate into 6 parts; from the uppermost, make a chamfer; below that, using two parts, make a hollow that recedes at the bottom as far as its height; and in the bottom two parts, also make a larger hollow in the same manner. If inverted, this is changed again.

Item: one may lead a serpentine line under a chamfer. You may also invert this. You may also make a small hollow at the top, a very sharp wave in the middle, and a large hollow at the bottom—ensuring that two small fillets remain above and below the wave. Such variations are nearly infinite. I do not set these things down because they *must* be made this way, but so that something may be learned from them, and so that everyone may be encouraged to find further and more unusual designs. For in these proportions, not just one thing alone is good, but many things are good for those who know how to make them. Therefore, one must search for them, just as the highly renowned <term>Vitruvius</term> and others have searched and found good things. Yet it was never intended that nothing else could be found that is also good—especially in those things that cannot be proven to be "the best" possible. 

Now I will describe the <term>capital</term> <note>original: "captel"</note> in the simplest way, using only the square for many things without any decoration; however, decorations must afterwards be added, especially if a work is large. One may decorate each <term>chamfer</term>, <term>hollow</term>, and <term>bead</term> individually and place something handsome upon it or carve into it. Do it thus: divide the height of the capital with five points into 6 equal fields. From the uppermost part, make a flat chamfer that projects outward by one-sixth of the capital’s thickness. Then place a point under the chamfer, set inward by half the height of the chamfer. From these points, draw a vertical line down through three fields; let this remain a large flat face. But from the lower two parts, make a hollow in the upper one that goes as deep as it is high; thus, a small projection remains above the lower chamfer, and so this capital gains a small neck. 

Secondly, do it thus: let the top chamfer remain in all its proportions as mentioned before. Since the capital is divided by height into 6 fields, place a point in the middle of the fourth field and draw a horizontal line through the capital. Above the bottom two parts, make a small fillet, projecting as far as it is high. Let the bottom two parts remain as a flat neck. But from the upper part, make an upward-curving arc that meets the top chamfer so that the chamfer projects twice as far as its own width. 

Thirdly: make the chamfer with the same projection as before, but now only half as thick. Do nothing further to the capital except draw a hollow curved line from the side of the capital up to the projection of the chamfer, so that it gains a flare at the top. 

Thereafter, in the other three capitals, make the chamfers just like the previous ones. When the capital is divided into six parts by height, as mentioned before, draw a horizontal line through the capital under the third part. Draw an arc between the chamfer and the line just drawn, but ensure it does not reduce the thickness of the capital in its hollow; from this, the overhang and sharpness are found, determining how far it goes on the horizontal line just drawn. Then draw a horizontal line through the capital from the middle of the fourth field; from this, make a small hollow whose depth touches the thickness of the capital; thus the curved line gives the measure for the other horizontal line, showing how far it should overlap.

Thereafter, divide the lowest six parts with two points into three equal fields. Cut off the bottom third with a horizontal line <note>original: "zwerch lini"</note>; from this, make a <term>chamfer</term> <note>original: "fasen," a flat, sloped molding</note> that projects outward as far as it is high. Then, draw a circular arc <note>original: "cirkeltrum"</note> between the existing small chamfers. Ensure the ends of the arc touch the sides of the <term>capital</term> <note>original: "captel"</note> at the top and bottom, matching its thickness, and that the rounding of the <term>waves</term> <note>original: "wellen," S-shaped or ogee moldings</note> does not project further than the nearest sharp edge of the <term>hollow</term> <note>original: "holkelen," a concave molding</note> directly above it. Fifthly: make the uppermost large hollow as before, and divide the lower part of the capital into three parts with two horizontal lines. From the upper two parts, make two small hollows, and let the bottom part remain a flat chamfer. Sixthly: divide the area from the upper chamfer down to the end of the capital into three equal fields. From the upper two parts, make one large hollow, and from the bottom third, make a small one, ensuring that neither takes away from the thickness of the capital’s depth. Once these capitals are made, they may be decorated in many ways according to one's own pleasure, which I will briefly demonstrate. Take the previously described plates—firstly, the octagonal one—and place it on the first capital. Under each corner, make a square support <note>original: "gefirtte drag," likely a bracket or console</note>, wider at the back than at the front. Let these project outward from the thickness of the capital by one quarter-measure, and make the supports as thick as the plate itself. Likewise, if you wish to decorate a capital, allow for this thickness at those same ends beforehand. On the second capital, place the square plate, and from the chamfer of the capital, create a twisted, rolled-up <term>scroll</term> <note>original: "zedel," here referring to a volute or decorative flourish</note> on two opposing sides, adding something decorative to it. On the third capital, place the recessed square plate and let the square project outward under the corners of the plate by one quarter-measure of the capital’s thickness; make it thicker than the plate by one quarter of the plate's thickness. Then, in the middle of the four hollows of the plate, create a round ornament as thick as the plate, and let its projection touch the sides of the square plates. Once these squares are carved, one may add beautiful things into them, such as artistic foliage <note>original: "lauberg"</note>, animal heads, birds, and all sorts of things according to the whim of the craftsman. Once the capitals are finished, they are placed upon the <term>columns</term> <note>original: "seulen"</note>, so that one gives shape to the other. Next, make the <term>base</term> <note>original: "füsslein" or "fueß"</note> for the column as follows, and set it beneath the column.

Make the base half as high as the column is thick at its bottom chamfer. Draw two vertical lines down from both sides of the chamfer to the end of the base. Then, divide the base into three equal fields with two horizontal lines. In the bottom third, make a square element like a stone; let it project as far as it is high. "Projecting" <note>original: "auß geladen"</note> means that an object is moved forward from its own body to which it is attached. Then, divide the middle third into three equal fields with horizontal lines. In the upper one, leave a flat chamfer; in the lower two fields, make a portion of a wave. Do it thus: place one foot of the compass on the upper horizontal line of the bottom square at the vertical side-line of the base, and place the other at the bottom of the upper chamfer line, and draw a circular arc down from the side of the base toward the end of the square. Then, divide the top third into three parts; from the bottom third, make a chamfer that overlaps the lower chamfer by half its height. Then, draw a wave in the two upper parts that overlaps the next lower chamfer as far as its arc reaches when its point is set within the width of the chamfer.

Now this column should be placed upon a <term>pedestal</term> <note>original: "bossament"</note>; do it thus. First, make an oblong square <note>original: "fierung"</note> with a length one-quarter that of the entire column (including its capital and base), and make it as wide as the lowest square on the column's base; this pedestal's ground plan should be square. Then, at the top of this oblong square, cut off a fourteenth part with a horizontal line and let it project outward by that same amount. Divide this section in the middle with a horizontal line; from the upper part make a chamfer, and from the lower part make a quarter of a wave that sits in the corner and meets the middle of the chamfer's overhang. Then, cut off a seventh part from the bottom of this pedestal with a horizontal line and let it project at the very bottom by that same amount. From this seventh part, cut off a fifth part at the top with a horizontal line; from this, make a chamfer projecting as far as it is high, and hollow out its corner at the top with a circular arc. Then, with a horizontal line, cut a half-part between the chamfer and the bottom of the pedestal; from this, make a chamfer at the bottom. Then, divide the projection of the chamfer between the vertical side of the pedestal and the end of the chamfer into three parts with vertical lines. <note>On the left margin, a tall, narrow vertical diagram depicts a pillar or scale. It is divided into 14 numbered sections vertically, labeled 1–14 on the left. Rather, so that the base <note>original: "vnder sas"</note> projects by one of its three parts; this <term>pedestal</term> <note>original: "possament"</note> may be decorated in many ways. However, as written above, I have drafted this below with its foundations so that you may know what the decoration is that one can make with the <term>plane</term> <note>original: "hobel"</note> or <term>lathe</term> <note>original: "dreiwerck"</note>. Mark well that there are six specific things from which one works such things: 

1. The first is a **flat fillet** <note>original: "blatte fassen"</note>.
2. The second is a **hollow** <note>original: "holkelen," a concave cove molding</note>.
3. The third is an **out-bowed wave** <note>original: "auspogne wellen," an ogee or S-curve</note>.
4. The fourth is an **edge** <note>original: "eck"</note>.
5. The fifth is an **angle** <note>original: "winckel"</note>.
6. The sixth is the **serpentine line** <note>original: "schlangen lini"</note>, which one may draw as one wishes. 

All these things may be used together, or in part; one may make them shallow or deep, flat or raised, wide or narrow, sharp or blunt, large or small relative to one another, broad or thin, as one wishes. But every craftsman should use discretion <note>original: "bescheidenheit"</note>, so that on large things he makes the decoration from larger elements, and on small things, smaller. I will show this hereafter with two moldings—one long and one short—next to each other; for every man’s reason soon grasps that a long thing should reasonably be thicker than a shorter one of its kind. Therefore, if a <term>builder</term> <note>original: "bauman"</note> wants to make <term>cornice-work</term> <note>original: "gesimsewerck"</note> or a **door frame** and the like, he should make the outer elements that run further out of larger things than the inner, shorter ones; whatever is made contrary to this looks poor. Understand it thus: make an upright molding <note>original: "fasen"</note> between two lines, cut it off at the top with a horizontal line <note>original: "zwerch lini"</note>, and mark its corners at the top with **a** and **b**. Then place a point **c** on the side, roughly at the middle of the molding, and draw two straight lines **a-c** and **b-c**. Then place the inner molding, which shall be shorter, with an upright line next to **a** and **b**.

<note>A central diagram shows a geometric construction. Two vertical lines labeled 'a' and 'b' are connected by lines to a point 'c' further to the right, forming a triangle abc. A shorter vertical line segment 'd e' is drawn within this triangle, showing how the width decreases proportionally as the height is reduced. To the right of this are six symbols representing architectural molding profiles: a flat bracket, a cove, a wave, a corner, a V-shape, and an S-curve.</note>

Set these as far from each other as you wish the molding to be wide. Where the line touches the straight line **c-a**, place a **d**. From there, draw a horizontal line to the other straight line **c-b**; where it touches, place an **e**. Drawing an upright line down from **e** will result in **d-e** being correctly proportioned <note>original: "proporcionirt"</note> against **a-b**. Just as it is done with this molding, so it is also done with the other aforementioned things, including the **columns**, **capitals**, **bases**, and **pedestals**, all of which are drafted hereafter.

<note>Technical architectural diagrams illustrate the construction of columns and pedestals. At the top, three drawings depict column capitals, including Ionic-style volutes, accompanied by scales and measurements. Below these is a profile view of a pedestal and base. To the right and left of the center are plan views: one shows a circular column shaft set within a star-like, twelve-pointed polygon, and another within a square border. Whoever wishes to give the aforementioned columns a **belly** <note>The "belly" or swelling of a column shaft is known in architecture as <term>entasis</term>, used to prevent the optical illusion of the column looking too thin in the middle.</note>, may do so in two ways.

First, divide the column lengthwise into three parts, and mark off the bottom third with a **horizontal line** <note>original: "zwerch lini"</note> at that same spot. Make the column as thick as its **base molding** <note>original: "fasen"</note> is at the bottom. Then, set three points: the upper one below the top molding of the column, the lower one above the bottom molding on the side of the column, and the third at the spot where the column is at its thickest. Next, draw a **circular arc** <note>original: "cirkelriß"; a line drawn with a compass</note> through these three points, just as the 24th figure of the first booklet teaches you; from this, the column gains a well-formed belly.

Alternatively, create the belly in this way: in the same manner, divide the column into three parts as before, and make the horizontal line that cuts off the [bottom] **third** <note>original: "dritteyl"</note> as wide as before. As for the width that extends beyond the straight side-line of the column, divide that into four parts on each side. Afterwards, divide the upper two-thirds into four equal parts using three horizontal lines; likewise, divide the bottom third into four parts with three horizontal lines. Then, allow the belly of the column to have its greatest [width] in the bottom third...

<summary>This page provides geometric instructions for creating 'entasis'—the aesthetic swelling of a column shaft—using two distinct methods involving proportional division and compass-drawn arcs.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for shaping a column, specifically detailing the final steps for carving the "belly" or entasis, followed by directions for fluting and creating spiral decorations.</meta>

...widths, and always reduce the small parts that are marked on the widest horizontal lines by one, following the horizontal lines one after another, both upwards and downwards. In this way, the <term>swell</term> <note>original: "bauch" (belly). In classical architecture, this is "entasis," the subtle convex curve of the column shaft.</note> is proportionally divided. 

If you then wish to <term>decorate</term> <note>original: "zirẽ" (zieren). Referring here to the architectural ornamentation of the shaft.</note> the columns, you may do so with straight, upright lines or with spirals. This is done based on the <term>ground plan</term> <note>original: "nider gedrucktem grund." The horizontal cross-section or base circumference of the column.</note>; therefore, first establish the thickness of the column’s base and divide it into 32 parts. From one part, always create a <term>flute</term> <note>original: "holkelen" (little hollow). A vertical groove carved into the shaft of the column.</note> with the depth of a half-circle; let the next part remain as a <term>facet</term> <note>original: "fasen." The flat space or fillet between the grooves.</note>. In the middle of this facet, create a <term>bead</term> <note>original: "steblein" (little rod). A small, raised convex molding.</note> half as large as the facet is wide, and well-raised. 

Now, if you wish to wind the column with a double spiral—spiraling against each other—or with a single spiral, you must lengthen the sections where the spirals meet as they go upward, but shorten them as they go downward. Do this using the triangle **a. b. c.** from the <term>compass-drawing</term> **e.** <note>original: "cirkelryß." A geometric construction or arc drafted precisely with a compass.</note> as described previously, and as I have <term>sketched</term> <note>original: "aufgerissen." A technical term for plotting or drafting a diagram.</note> in the following diagram.

--- Page 89 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for column design, moving from the shaft's tapering and decoration discussed on the previous page to the specific geometric construction of the capital and the proportions of a twisted memorial column.</meta>

For the previously mentioned bellied columns <note>German: *baucheten seulen*; these are columns with 'entasis,' a slight swelling in the shaft for visual harmony</note>, create a capital in this manner. First, make it as high and as thick as the column is wide at the top, just below the fillet <term>fillet: a small, flat, band-like molding</term>. Then, draw the decorated abacus <term>abacus: the flat slab forming the uppermost member of a capital</term> at the top; make it one-sixth as thick as the total height of the capital, and let it project outward by half of the capital’s thickness. 

Next, divide the space between the abacus and the bottom of the capital into six equal fields. Take one of these six parts and form it into the shape of a shallow bowl; let its upper edge project to the midpoint of the abacus’s overhang, and give it a fillet one-fourth as wide as the abacus is thick. Below this bowl, make another fillet that is one-third thinner than the upper one. This fillet should project outward by one-fifth of the abacus’s overhang. From this fillet, draw a hollow molding <term>hollow molding: a concave profile, often called a scotia</term> inward until it reaches the thickness of the capital. This will create a shape like a wide courtly goblet, and the bowl sitting upon it together form a pleasing appearance. 

Afterward, you may decorate the capital with flowers, animals, or other things according to your pleasure. If you wish to place ornaments under the four corners of the abacus, you may pull them down so they curve over the fillet of the goblet-shape; make these decorations as thick as the abacus and let them project two-fifths of the width of the abacus’s overhang. You may also decorate the bowl-shape itself, and between the overhanging foliage under the corners of the abacus, you may add further ornamentation. Furthermore, if the corners of the abacus seem to project too far, draw their curves deeper with the compass <term>compass: a geometric tool for drawing circles and arcs</term> and clip the corners as short as you desire, just as I have drawn them below.

<image-desc>
Two architectural technical drawings. The upper drawing shows a side elevation of a column capital with a grid scale numbered 1 to 6 vertically and 1 to 5 horizontally, demonstrating geometric proportions. Various profile lines indicate the projection of the abacus (blatten) and the necking. To the right of the capital is a symbol resembling a figure eight. The lower drawing is a plan view (from above) of the same capital, showing the square top with concave-curved sides meeting at pointed corners, with concentric circles representing the shaft and the capital's profile.
</image-desc>

Following this, I will teach how to make another round column, which shall be twisted and curved in a special way. This may be used for decoration or as a memorial to support a statue. Make it thus: whatever the thickness of the column is above its projecting fillets and hollows, make the shaft nine times as high up to its upper hollow-molding fillet. Draw it below the capital and make it one-sixth thinner at the top than at the bottom, joining the column’s profile with straight lines. The ring <note>German: *ring*; likely referring to the astragal or a decorative band</note> on the column should end with a projection as wide as the column is thick at the base, and its height should be equal to that projection. Divide this decorative height into three equal fields with two horizontal lines: the ring or bead remains in the top field, the fillet in the second, ending with its projection in the middle between the—

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for constructing architectural columns. Having previously discussed the proportions of the capital, the text now transitions into a complex geometrical guide for drafting a "wound" or spiral column (often called a Solomonic column). It explains how to project a two-dimensional ground plan into a three-dimensional helical form using a series of numbered points and compass measurements.</meta>

ring and the column’s thickness; and in the lower third, a **hollow molding** <term>hollow molding: a concave, recessed groove, often called a scotia</term> runs from the **fillet** <term>fillet: a small, flat, raised band or molding</term> to the column shaft. However, the fillet under the column exceeds the column’s thickness by one-seventh, and it should end beneath the column at one-sixth of its thickness. Divide this height into three equal fields using three points, and cut off the top third with a **horizontal line** <note>German: *zwerchlini*</note>; from this, create a hollow molding extending from the end of the column to the fillet, which will be twice as wide as the first hollow molding.

Now, before you create this capital for the **pedestal** <note>German: *posament*; referring to the decorative base or mounting of the column</note>, you must first deal further with the straight column. For first, you must lay out a **ground plan** <term>ground plan: the two-dimensional base drawing used to determine the proportions of a three-dimensional object</term> from which you must wind this column. Once the previously mentioned column is drawn out, draw an upright line straight through the middle of it; let it be **a** at the top and **b** at the bottom. This line **a-b** must be wound **like a snail’s shell** <note>German: *schneckens weyß*; meaning a spiral or helical shape</note> based on a ground plan made in this way: 

Set a center point **a**. From it, draw a circle as thick as the column. Then, draw a straight vertical line through this circle, passing through the center **a** from one side of the circle to the other. Divide the upper half of this straight line—between the edge of the circle and the center **a**—into two parts with a point **c**. 

Next, on the vertical line below center **a**, set a center point **d** and draw a circle from it that touches point **c** at the top and the large outer circle at the bottom. Then, divide the straight line between **a** and **c** into two parts with a point **e**, and draw a circle from it that touches both **c** and **a**. 

Once this is done, graduate these three circles with numbers from one to sixty. Begin counting on the inside, nearest to **a**: 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, etc. Count in the smallest circle from one to six; this number should end at point **c**. Then count outward onto the middle circle from 7, 8, 9, etc., up to eighteen; this represents half of the middle circle. 

Next, proceed outward with the number nineteen onto the large circle, and go all the way around so that you reach forty-two directly below the number eighteen (which is along the vertical line **c-e-a-d**). Then, with the number forty-three, go back into the middle circle until you reach fifty-four at point **c**. Finally, step into the small circle with fifty-five until you arrive at number sixty at point **a**. 

You may lead these numbers to whichever side you wish. Through these numbered points of the flattened ground plan, the **axis** <term>axis: the central line around which the column's form rotates</term> of the upright column must be wound. 

Once this ground plan is made, divide the upright column into sixty points and numbers along its length. But do this in a special way: draw the line beneath the column (which is above the hollow molding and fillet) straight out horizontally; make it twice as long as the column is thick. At its end, place a point **f**. From this point **f**, draw a straight **vertical reference line** <note>German: *ortlini*</note> up to just below the hollow molding at the top of the column. 

Then, place one foot of the compass on point **f** and the other near the column on the horizontal line at a point **h**. Draw an arc upward until it meets the vertical reference line; where they intersect, place a **g**. Then divide this circular arc into sixty equal parts and mark them with points. From point **f**, draw straight lines through every degree of the circular arc until they reach the column. 

Next, from these points that have been marked on the column, draw lines across horizontally and label them with the numbers from the flattened ground plan; these should begin at the bottom of the column and the circular arc **g-h**. From this, you will see how the divisions on the straight column stretch further apart the higher they go.

Afterward, draw the axis line **a-b** on the column's form once more, with all its horizontal lines and numbers. Take a compass and go to the round ground plan where the points for the axis are to be shifted and wound. Always place one foot of the compass on the straight line of the round ground plan that divides the circles into two parts, and however the numbered points are spaced, take that same width horizontally with the other foot of the compass. Transfer it to the column’s axis **a-b**; place one foot on the horizontal line that has the number you took from the round ground plan. 

In the same way, use the other foot of the compass to mark a point on this horizontal line to show where the shifted point of the wound axis should stand. Do this for all the numbers on both sides of the round ground plan and on the vertical axis **a-b** and the horizontal lines. Thus, the curved axis of the wound column will be marked with points on both sides of the straight one; because of this winding, the wound column becomes longer than the straight height.

Then take a compass and transfer the thickness of the column from the straight axis of the first column—from all the horizontal lines—onto the wound axis through all its horizontal lines, and draw a circle from them. Through this, you find the thickness of the column. For you must remember that from the round column, in all horizontal lines passing through their center points, a sphere might be cut out; remember also where...

<summary>This page provides highly technical geometric instructions for drawing a spiral (wound) column. It details how to create a ground plan using nested circles and a numbering system to project a 3D helix onto a 2D surface.</summary>
<keywords>spiral column, geometry, architectural drawing, ground plan, axis, helix, technical manual, drafting techniques</keywords>

--- Page 91 ---
<meta>This page continues the geometric explanation of the "wound" or spiral column. While the previous page established the ground plan and the central axis (line a-b) following a snail-shell spiral, this text explains how to give that axis three-dimensional volume by imagining a series of spheres along the path.</meta>

<sig>H ij</sig>

If you place a sphere and move it back and forth as you wish, it can always be cut directly across from you by a **flat plane** <term>flat plane: a perfectly level surface; translated from the Latin *plano*</term> right through its center. If a round column is then bent back and forth, one should always imagine such spheres within it, which may be divided by the previously mentioned cuts from as many center points as one chooses to take. Now, imagine and assume that every point along the wound path of the column is the center of a sphere. From there, draw a **circle** <note>original: *cirkelryß*</note> upon the same **horizontal line** <note>original: *zwerch lini*</note> where that point sits, as thick as the straight column would be at that location. Do this through all points along the path of the spiral column. 

In this way, the thickness of the column is determined throughout all its curves. Once all these circles are drawn, trace a line around the outside of these circular sketches; from this, the **contours** <note>German: *bossen*; refers to the outer profile or rough form of the stone</note> of the column will emerge. However, if you draw a straight horizontal line through the center or path-points of all those circles (which represent the cross-sections of the sphere), just as it was drawn before—which in the curved column signifies and is a circular line—and then join those points on both sides lengthwise through the entire spiral column across all the circular lines, you will see how the lines in the spiral narrow and pull together in certain places because of the twisting of the column. 

But while the horizontal lines in a straight column represent circular planes standing directly atop one another, these same lines in the winding of the curved column no longer remain **flat** <note>original: *blat*; meaning level or parallel</note> upon each other. Instead, they shift, sag, and turn this way and that—upward, downward, and to the side—becoming slanted, oblong, and rounded lines. I have sketched these out following the simplest possible method.

<summary>This page describes a geometric method for drafting a spiral column by imagining it as a series of spheres centered along a helical path. It explains how the twisting of the column causes the horizontal cross-sections to appear distorted, slanted, or compressed when viewed from the side.</summary>
<keywords>spiral column, geometry, sphere, horizontal line, perspective distortion, architectural drafting, Albrecht Dürer</keywords>

--- Page 92 ---
<meta>This page continues the geometric explanation of the "wound" or spiral column. While the previous page established the ground plan and the central axis, this text explains how to give that axis three-dimensional volume by imagining a series of spheres along the path.</meta>

->H ij<-

If you take a sphere and move it back and forth as you wish, it can always be sliced directly through its center by a **flat plane** <term>flat plane: a perfectly level surface; translated from the Latin *plano*</term>. If you then have a round column that is bent or curved, you should visualize a series of these spheres nested within it. These spheres can be divided by the cross-sections mentioned earlier, using as many center points as you choose to designate.

Now, imagine and assume that every point along the winding path of the column is the center of such a sphere. From each of those points, draw a **circle** <note>original: *cirkelryß*</note> upon the same **horizontal line** <note>original: *zwerch lini*</note> where that point is located. The circle should be as thick as the straight column would be at that specific point. You must do this for every point along the path of the spiral column.

In this way, the thickness of the column is determined throughout all its curves. Once all these circles have been drawn, trace a line connecting their outer edges; from this, the **contours** <note>German: *bossen*; refers to the outer profile or rough form of the stone</note> of the column will emerge.

However, if you draw a straight horizontal line through the centers of all those circles—which represent the cross-sections of the spheres—just as it was drawn before (which, in a curved column, signifies a circular line), and then connect those points on both sides along the entire length of the spiral column, you will see how the lines of the spiral narrow and pull together in certain places because of the twisting of the column.

### Geometric Construction of the Curved Column

This diagram illustrates the mathematical "spine" of the column. A vertical axis, marked **a** at the base and **b** (representing the number 60) at the top, is intersected by sixty horizontal parallel lines. A sinusoidal curve—the path of the spiral—weaves back and forth across this vertical axis. Numerical values on the right (ranging from 10 up to 60) track the height, while small numerals along the curve itself track the precise progression of the winding path as it rises from the bottom to the top.

<column-break/>

This second diagram demonstrates how to give the column its physical volume. A series of overlapping circles forms a vertical, serpentine shape. While the stack begins as a straight vertical column at the top (marked **b**), the centers of the circles shift as they descend toward the base (marked **a**), creating a winding "S" curve. A series of small dots traces the central axis through the centers of these circles, showing how these circular cross-sections define the outer edges and "thickness" of the curved column.

### Technical Vocabulary

- <term>Geometry: the mathematical study of shapes, lines, and points; translated from *Geometrie*</term>
- <term>Curve: a continuous bending line without angles; translated from *Kurve*</term>
- <term>Compasses: the tool used to draw circles, or the circular lines themselves; translated from *Zirkel*</term>
- <term>Scale: a rule or standard for measurement; translated from *Maßstab*</term>
- <term>Construction: the systematic drawing or building of a form; translated from *Konstruktion*</term>

<summary>This page contains two technical diagrams illustrating how to plot a spiral column's path on a coordinate grid and how to use overlapping circles to define its three-dimensional volume.</summary>
