--- Page 94 ---
<meta>This page continues the instructions for constructing a winding or serpentine column, moving from the geometry of the shaft to the specific proportions and design of its base and capital.</meta>

For this curved column, make a base like this. First, make an oblong rectangle <note>German: *fierung*</note> three times as long as it is high, and three times as high as the fillet <note>German: *fasen*, a flat architectural molding</note> at the bottom of the column is high. After that, create the divisions within it using horizontal lines, and label the lines with letters. Let the top long side of the rectangle be **a** and the bottom be **b**. Then, divide the space between **a** and **b** with two lines, **c** and **d**, into three fields. But divide the space between **a** and **c** in half with a line. Next, divide the space between **a** and **e** with 4 points into 5 fields; cut off the top one with a line, **f**. Then divide the space between **e** and **c** with 3 points into 4 fields; cut off the bottom one with a line, **g**. Then divide the space between **e** and **g** with three points into 4 fields, and cut off the top field with a line, **h**. Then divide the space between **d** and **b** with 5 points into 6 fields, and cut off the bottom two with a line, **i**, and the top one with a line, **k**.

Once the horizontal lines for the ornaments that belong there are made, then make the vertical lines where all things shall end. Do this on one side and you will have the other as well. Let the vertical side of the oblong rectangle be **l**, and the line that passes down from the column's fillet through the rectangle be **m**. Now divide the space between **l** and **m** with a line, **n**, into two parts; this line touches the wide fillet in the middle of the base between **c** and **d**. Then divide the space between **n** and **m** into two parts with a line, **o**; thus a round wave or ring <note>German: *wellen oder ring*, a torus molding</note> is drawn between **e** and **f**, which ends at line **o**. But between **o** and **m**, make two parts with a line, **p**; the small fillet between **a** and **f** ends there, and likewise the small fillet under the ring between **e** and **h** also ends there. Then divide the space between **n** and **o** into two parts with a line, **q**; the small fillet between **g** and **c** ends there, below the hollow molding <note>German: *holkelein*, a concave molding</note> between **h** and **g**, which ends at line **m**. Then extend the small fillet between **n**, **d**, and **k** as far past line **n** as it is thick; but the bottom fillet touches line **l**, and the hollow molding between the fillets touches line **n**. Or, if you have come out at the top with the small fillet, then make a flat hollow molding from that same corner down to the bottom fillet; thus the top fillet is removed, as I have drawn below—though not the one just mentioned.

*A technical architectural diagram illustrating the proportions for the base of a curved column. The first image shows a grid of horizontal and vertical lines labeled with letters (a, b, c, d, e, f, g, h, i, k, l, n, o, p, m) and numerical markings (1, 2, 3, 4, 5). Below it, a second drawing shows the resulting profile of the column base, featuring its steps and moldings (fillets and hollow moldings).*

Also, for the aforementioned curved column, draw a capital in two ways. First, make the capital's body within a square taken from the thickness of the top of the column, as mentioned before; and make the squared plate <note>German: *blatten*, the abacus</note> in the same width and thickness and in its place as before. However, the overhang <note>German: *oberschießung*</note> of this plate should be extended far out because of the column's curve, so that the capital looks stable and well-proportioned <note>German: *ratlich*</note>. Then make the top fillet under the plate with the same projection and width as described in the very last capital. Draw a horizontal line halfway between the fillet and the end of the capital, and cut off the bottom third of this upper half with a horizontal line. Then draw a diagonal line <note>German: *ort lini*</note> from the end of the upper fillet down to the end of the capital. Place a compass <note>German: *cirkel*</note> with one foot on the diagonal line and draw a circular line at the top between the greater widths, from the end of the fillet inward toward the capital, so that the semi-circle touches the nearest horizontal line below. Thus the rounded line cuts off the edge <note>German: *scherpf*</note> of the hollow molding according to how far it extends; from this comes a large...

<summary>This page provides detailed geometric instructions for drafting the base and capital of a winding column, using a grid of lines and specific proportional divisions for the moldings.</summary>
<keywords>column base, capital design, architectural moldings, fillet, hollow molding, torus, geometric construction, proportions, drafting techniques</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for constructing architectural elements, moving from the base of a column (discussed on the previous page) to the detailed geometry and ornamentation of the capital and the pedestal block above it.</meta>

<sig>H iiij</sig>

...hollow moldings. After that, draw a circle-line between the two narrower horizontal lines so that it touches both of them, as well as the sides of the capital and the edge-line. From this, a small hollow molding <term>holkelen: a concave or "cove" molding</term> will be formed; note how far its lower edge extends outward. Then make another small hollow molding from the extended edge to the fillet of the abacus <term>blat: here referring to the abacus or top plate of the capital</term> or the sides of the capital.

If you wish to decorate the capital, place the ornament under the corners of the abacus within its square <term>fierung: a square or rectangular frame used for layout</term> as thick as the large hollow molding. Extend it a little further than half the thickness of the capital. In the smaller hollow moldings, you may create a branch from which foliage pulls upward; in the hollow of the abacus, you may place a rose or another ornament.

For a second variation, alter the capital as follows: leave the abacus and the fillet beneath it as they were before, but cut off the next sixth part under the abacus with a horizontal line <term>zwerch lini: a horizontal line</term>. Divide this into two equal parts. From the upper half, create a deep hollow molding starting from the upper fillet and moving inward until it reaches the core thickness of the capital. Then divide the lower half with another horizontal line into two parts and make two fillets <term>fasen: flat, band-like moldings</term> from them. The lowest one should project half as far as the one directly under the abacus, but the other small fillet <term>fesslein: a thin molding or listel</term> resting on the lowest fillet should be set back as far as it is high.

Next, divide the third sixth-part in two with a horizontal line. In the upper part, use a serpentine line <term>schlangen lini: an S-shaped or "cyma" curve</term> for the ornament that begins under the lowest fillet, matching the width of the small fillet above it. Construct it so that the line curves outward at the top and inward at the bottom; this inward curve should end midway between its upper part and the main body of the capital. Then draw a convex line over the body of the capital, starting from the small angle just created and extending down to the capital again, but ensure it does not project further than the corner directly above it.

Then, construct the ornament under the four corners of the abacus within its square to be one and a half sixth-parts thick, and let it project by half the thickness of the capital. Also, create a square for the ornament within the hollow of the abacus, whether large or small. I demonstrate these through the use of squares for the sake of those who work in stone and wood <note>The author provides these geometric grids specifically to assist stonemasons and woodcarvers in transferring designs to physical materials</note>.

Furthermore, decorate the thickness of the abacus as follows: divide its edge into three parts. Let the top part remain a flat fillet. From the two lower parts, draw a serpentine line so that its curve projects at the bottom, and begin this serpentine line as far behind the fillet as the fillet itself is wide.

If you wish to layout the abacus using a compass <term>cirkel: a pair of compasses or dividers</term>, proceed thus: Draw two crossing lines through the square of the abacus so that it is divided into four smaller squares; extend these lines as far beyond the square as necessary. Then draw the ground plans <note>German: *grund*, the bird's-eye view or footprint of the architectural element</note> within their squares for both versions of the capital. With a compass, take the measure of the largest circle; place one foot of the compass on the crossing line at the previously mentioned circular mark, and place the other foot further out on the crossing line at a point **a**. With the first foot, draw curves on both sides through the sides of the square. Repeat this for the other sides of the square, using the points **a**, **b**, **c**, and **d**.

Afterward, also use the compass to draw the decoration of the abacus's thickness on the ground plan from these points **a**, **b**, **c**, and **d**, showing how deep they reach inward. Also, the pedestal block <term>schamel: a plinth block or "stool" placed atop the capital to support a statue</term> which you set upon the capital should be as thick as the capital itself, but only one-fourth as high. Decorate it at the top and bottom with small moldings. Furthermore, any statue <note>German: *bild*, referring to a sculpted figure</note> that you wish to place upon the columns should be twice as high as the capital and the pedestal block combined. I have provided drawings of these capitals and their ground plans below.

Now that the twisted column and its associated parts are finished, you should then create a pedestal beneath it that is perfectly square. First, draw an upright, oblong frame <term>fierung: a rectangular layout or "squaring" used as a guide</term> as wide as the base of the column and twice as high. Place a square stone beneath it that is one-quarter the height of the upright frame, and let it extend outward on each side of the main frame. 

From the bottom of this upright frame, cut off one-quarter with a horizontal line <term>zwerch lini: a horizontal construction line</term>. From that height, draw a diagonal edge-line <term>ort lini: a line defining the outer boundary or "tilt" of a profile</term> down to the lower stone, projecting outward by half a unit from the upright frame. 

From this, create a staircase <term>stiegen: a tiered base</term> with four steps <term>staffelen: the individual tiers or levels of a base</term>. Extend the steps outward at the top by the width of their diameter. On the top of each, create a fillet <term>fasen: a flat, band-like molding</term> one-quarter the width of the step's height, and beneath it, draw a hollow molding <term>holkelen: a concave or "cove" molding</term>.

After that, cut off the top eighth of the main frame with a horizontal line. Extend this top section outward as far as it is high, and draw a diagonal edge-line from the top of this projection down to the frame at the next horizontal line. Then, divide this top section in half with another horizontal line. 

In the upper half, cut off the top sixth with a horizontal line to create a fillet; its corner should extend beyond the diagonal edge-line. Next, in the lower part of that section, draw a curved arc <term>cirkeltrum: a segment of a circle</term> bowing out over the diagonal edge-line, so that it begins on the diagonal line and ends back on the diagonal line at the lower horizontal boundary. 

Then, divide the remaining lower half into two parts with a horizontal line. Divide the upper part into four. From the top fourth, create a fillet whose lower corner extends over the diagonal edge-line. Beneath it, draw a deep hollow molding that curves inward and back out until it touches the lower horizontal line. 

Finally, divide the remaining bottommost part into four units. From the top and bottom units, create two small fillets <term>feslein: a thin molding or listel</term> that project as far as they are high. From the middle two units, create a wave <term>wellen: an ogee or cyma molding, featuring a double curve</term> that extends outward more than a semi-circle beyond the...

The fillets <term>fasen: flat, band-like moldings</term> should be extended. Additionally, the uppermost large curved molding <term>cirkeltrum: a segment of a circle or curved profile</term> may be decorated with foliage <term>lauberg: leaf-like ornamentation</term>, and the other parts of the pedestal <term>possament: a decorative base or pedestal</term> may be adorned as one wishes. The four sides of the square frame <term>fierung: the rectangular or square layout</term> can be decorated with narrative scenes <note>original: "historien"</note> and inscriptions <term>epitafien: commemorative texts or epitaphs</term>. However, one must first place moldings <term>leysten: decorative strips or bands</term> on the four sides of the square; the fillets of these moldings should be one-twelfth the width of the pedestal. The offset where the moldings set in should be half that width. Furthermore, place four men on the four corners of the steps <term>stiegen: a tiered base</term> as a decoration, acting as sentinels. On the four corners of the pedestal above, place four small markers <term>mercklein: small decorative points or finials</term>. The scale of this column should be determined thus: when a man stands on the large square stone in front of the pedestal, his head should reach the middle of the pedestal. I have drawn this pedestal below; the ground plan is easy to draw from it, so it is omitted here.

The structure consists of a base foundation, followed by a set of three steps, and a central rectangular block topped with a flared cornice or molding. There are also smaller scale notations at the top left including the numbers 8, 4, 4, 2 and a letter E.]

However, if you wish, you may leave out the steps on the aforementioned pedestal and decorate it differently with molding-work <term>leistenwerck: decorative paneling or framing</term> than before. To do this: make this drawn stone two squares high. Make the lower cornice <term>gesims: a molded projection that crowns a wall or pedestal</term> half a square high and extend it outward on each side. From the bottom of this extension, draw a diagonal edge-line <note>original: "ortstrich," a line used to define the outer boundary of a profile</note> upwards to the end of the cornice at the stone; the decoration should be drawn along this. Divide this height into three parts. From the bottom third, make a square stone with a flat fillet. From the upper two thirds, make the other decoration. From the lower half of that section, make a fillet in the bottom third and a hollow molding <term>holkele: a concave or "cove" molding</term> in the upper two thirds. In the upper half mentioned before, make a fillet in its bottom third and a hollow molding above it, as was described for the lower part. In the uppermost part, make two narrow fillets and a bead <note>original: "stab," a rounded, rod-like molding</note> in the middle, as large as the two fillets combined.

Make the upper cornice one-third narrower than the lower one and let its thickness project far out. Divide this part into three sections: from the uppermost third, make two fillets <term>feſlein: small flat bands or moldings</term>, the upper one wider than the lower. But in the middle, draw a serpentine line <term>schlangen lini: an S-curve or cyma profile</term> that is as wide as the two fillets, so that the hollow molding <term>holkelen: a concave profile</term> is at the top and the bulge is at the bottom. However, from the lower two-thirds, make two hollow moldings, ensuring the lower one has a small fillet at the top. I have drawn this below.

[A technical architectural drawing showing the side profile of a pedestal or monument base. It features a rectangular foundation block, a stepped base with four levels, a central square plinth, and a molded cornice at the top. It often happens that when a field is won in battle, a memorial or column is erected at the place where the enemies were defeated as a remembrance of what kind of people they were who were overcome. If they were powerful people, one may make and erect such a column from the equipment <note>original: "zeug", referring here to captured military spoils such as weapons, armor, or artillery</note> that was captured from them.

First, lay a decorated stone foundation <term>geſird ſteinberg: an ornamental masonry base</term> upon a mound that can support everything placed upon it. One side should be twenty-eight feet <term>schuch: literally "shoes," a historical unit of measurement approximately equal to a foot</term> long and nine feet high above the ground. At each corner, place a cannonball <term>bůchſſen kugel</term> one and a half feet high. In the middle of this stonework, create four steps <term>staffelen</term> three feet high. Make the bottom step one and a quarter feet high, then divide the other three proportionally so that the top one becomes the smallest <note>original: "niderſt", meaning lowest in height or narrowest in the series</note>, and the two middle steps are divided evenly—just as was indicated previously at the end of the first book in the line diagrams. On the bottom step, make

One side is twenty-four feet <term>schuch: literally "shoe," a standard foot measurement</term> long, but make the uppermost step twenty-one feet long. Place a powder barrel <term>bulfer dunnen: a barrel used for gunpowder</term> on each corner, two and a half feet high and two feet wide at the belly. However, cut off the corners of the steps evenly with a diagonal line <term>ortstrich: a beveled or mitered edge in stonemasonry</term>. Then, in the center of the steps, set an upright oblong stone twelve feet high and eight feet wide. Let the cornice <term>gesims: a decorative molding at the top of a pedestal</term> project out at the top by three-quarters of a foot, and make it as thick as it is wide in its projection.

Then, from the uppermost quarter of the cornice, make a fillet <term>fasen: a small, flat architectural band</term>. Between this upper fillet and the end of the cornice in the bottom quarter, make another fillet as wide in projection as it is high; this one will be smaller than the upper one. Then draw a serpentine line <term>schlangen lini: an S-shaped curve, likely a cyma profile</term> between the two fillets, so that the hollow molding <term>holkelen: a concave profile</term> is at the top and the bulge is at the bottom. But the decoration at the bottom of the stone should project out two feet on each side, and make the lower flat fillet one foot high.

Next, draw a horizontal line <term>zwerchlini</term> across the upright stone one foot above the square flat fillet. From the bottom third, make a fillet that projects one foot beyond the stone, and from the upper two parts, make a hollow molding that ends in the next third near the stone, between the end of the fillet and the stone.

Then, set a mortar <term>werf mörser: a short, thick-walled cannon used for high-angle fire</term> with its carriage <term>laden: the wooden bed or mount for the artillery piece</term> in the middle of this stone, which should be eight feet wide at the base. Make the mortar along with its stand ten feet high. Make it five feet wide around the mouth <note>The "mouth" refers to the muzzle where the projectile is loaded.</note> for the sake of decoration and strength, but in the body, make it four and a quarter feet wide. The rings and ornaments should overlap. In the powder chamber <term>bulfersack: the narrowed rear part of the bore where the powder is placed</term>, make it three and a quarter feet wide.

Then, place in the middle of this mortar a large, strong cannon <term>buchsen: a heavy gun or field piece</term> twenty-one feet long, three feet wide at the rear but two feet wide at the front. The iron shot it fires should be one foot high. The mouth of the cannon should be strong, for the decoration at the front and rear should exceed the body of the cannon in proper order and strength, as skilled cannon-casters <term>buchsen giesser</term> well know how to do.

Then, stick into the top of the cannon a bell with its handle <note>original: "or", literally "ear," referring to the suspension loop of a bell</note>, three feet wide and two feet high. Lay two pavises <term>bafesen: large, rectangular shields used by infantry</term> crosswise upon it, and set four suits of armor <term>harnisch</term> with their backs to one another on the pavises, so that on the four corners their leg-armor <term>beingwand: greaves or leg protection</term> hangs over the pavises. Make all of this four feet high. But you may draw their plumes <term>federbusch: decorative bunches of feathers on a helmet</term> over their helmets <term>helmlet</term> and to the sides as high and wide as you wish. Make one pavise six feet long, as is sketched hereafter. But if these things are made on a large scale in actual work, each thing should be decorated individually.

## Elevation and Proportions of the Monument

For the elevation <term>Aufriss: a scaled architectural drawing of a vertical surface</term> of the entire monument <term>monument</term>, the foundation <term>fundament</term> must be laid twenty-five feet wide at the bottom and nine feet in height. Upon this foundation, set three wide steps. On the right side of these steps, place a small wooden barrel <note>likely a powder barrel, continuing the military theme</note> and a solid sphere or stone shot.

The pedestal <term>postament</term> sits upon these steps and should be twelve feet wide. In the center of this section, mark the number **8** <note>this likely refers to a specific proportion or a reference to a ledger of parts</note>. Above the pedestal, the shaft of the pillar <term>pfeiler</term> rises, tapering slightly as it ascends.

### The Vertical Scale

The proportions of the pillar <term>säule</term> and its base are determined by the following measurements <term>maße</term>, to be followed strictly from the bottom upward:

*   The base section: **4** and **3**
*   The main shaft: **13**
*   The upper decorative sections: **10**, **1**, **1 1/2**, and **3/4**

A secondary scale is marked to the left to ensure the heights of the major divisions are correct: **12**, **3**, **1**, and **1 1/2**, with the final crowning element measured at **3/4**.

### The Heraldic Crest

The monument is topped with an elaborate decorative crest. At the summit of the shaft, place a heraldic helmet <term>wappenhelm: a helmet used in armorial displays, typically shown above a shield</term>. From the top of this helmet, three large plumes of feathers shall rise, spreading outward to provide a grand finish to the structure.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of monumental architecture from page 15, moving from traditional military victory columns to satirical and thematic monuments designed for specific social classes or lifestyles, specifically the "Victory Column" for the Peasants' War and a memorial for a drunkard.</meta>

16.

**J**

Anyone who wishes to erect a victory monument <note>original: "victoria"; here referring to a commemorative column</note> because he has overcome the rebellious peasants <note>This refers to the German Peasants' War of 1524–1525, a massive failed uprising in the Holy Roman Empire</note> might make use of such a design as I will teach hereafter. First, set a squared stone ten feet <note>original: "schuch"; literally "shoes," a historical unit of measurement roughly equivalent to a foot</note> long on each side and four feet high. This should stand upon a squared slab twenty feet long on each side and one foot high. On a mound at the four corners, place bound cows, sheep, pigs, and all sorts of livestock. But on the upper squared stone, set four baskets at the four corners with cheese, butter, eggs, onions, and herbs, or whatever you happen to have. 

Thereafter, place another squared stone in the middle of this stone, seven feet long on each side and one foot high. In the center of this stone, set an oat chest <note>a storage bin for grain</note> four feet high; at the bottom, it should be six and a half feet long, but at the lock it should be six feet long, and at the very top of the lid, four feet long. Upon this, turn over a kettle three and a half feet wide, but only three feet at the base. In the middle of the kettle's base, set a cheese bowl half a foot high, two feet wide at the top, but no more than one and a half at the base. Cover this with a thick plate that overlaps it well. In the middle of the plate, set a butter churn three feet high, one and a half feet wide at the bottom, but only one foot wide at the top; however, the spouts from which one pours should project outward. In the center of this butter churn, set a well-crafted milk jug two and a half feet high, one foot wide at the belly, but half a foot at the top; however, make the foot wider at the bottom. 

And in the milk jug, stand up four scrapers <note>specifically dung scrapers or "dung-forks" used to clean stables</note> with which one scrapes the manure together; draw these upward five and a half feet. Around them, bind a sheaf five feet high, so that the scrapers project out by half a foot. Hang upon it the peasants' tools: hoes, shovels, pickaxes, pitchforks, threshing flails, and the like. Then, set a chicken basket at the very top of the scrapers, and turn a lard pot <note>original: "schmalkhafen"</note> over it, and set a sorrowful peasant upon it, who is pierced through with a sword. I have drawn this out in the illustration that follows.

Likewise, anyone who wished to erect a memorial for a drunkard at his burial might make use of a similar illustrated concept as follows. First, his grave, around which an epitaph <note>original: "epitafis"</note> should be made that praises debauchery with mockery. On the grave, stand a beer barrel upright, and cover the top with a game board <note>original: "bretspil"; likely a board for backgammon or checkers</note>. Upon that, turn two bowls over one another; within them shall be gluttony <note>original: "fresserey"</note>. Then, on the base of the upper bowl, set a wide, low-slung beer jug with two handles. Cover it with a plate and turn a tall beer glass upside down upon it. On the base of the glass, set a small basket with bread, cheese, and butter. In the same way, one might decorate a burial in many different ways according to how each person lived. I wanted to show such things for the sake of curiosity <note>original: "abenteuer wegen"; literally "for the sake of adventure" or "unusual things"</note> and have drawn them alongside the other columns.

16.

**J**

Anyone who wishes to erect a victory monument <note>original: "victoria"; here referring to a commemorative column</note> because he has overcome the rebellious peasants <note>This refers to the German Peasants' War of 1524–1525, a massive failed uprising in the Holy Roman Empire</note> might make use of such a design as I will teach hereafter. First, set a squared stone ten feet <note>original: "schuch"; literally "shoes," a historical unit of measurement roughly equivalent to a foot</note> long on each side and four feet high. This should stand upon a squared slab twenty feet long on each side and one foot high. On a mound at the four corners, place bound cows, sheep, pigs, and all sorts of livestock. But on the upper squared stone, set four baskets at the four corners with cheese, butter, eggs, onions, and herbs, or whatever you happen to have. 

Thereafter, place another squared stone in the middle of this stone, seven feet long on each side and one foot high. In the center of this stone, set an oat chest <note>a storage bin for grain</note> four feet high; at the bottom, it should be six and a half feet long, but at the lock it should be six feet long, and at the very top of the lid, four feet long. Upon this, turn over a kettle three and a half feet wide, but only three feet at the base. In the middle of the kettle's base, set a cheese bowl half a foot high, two feet wide at the top, but no more than one and a half at the base. Cover this with a thick plate that overlaps it well. In the middle of the plate, set a butter churn three feet high, one and a half feet wide at the bottom, but only one foot wide at the top; however, the spouts from...

<page-num>17</page-num>

->**IN THE YEAR OF OUR LORD 1525**<-
<note>original: "ANNO DOMINI 1 5 2 5"</note>

Having previously demonstrated a little regarding elongated bodies <note>refers to the geometric solids discussed in earlier chapters of the manual</note>, I now wish to teach how to construct a solid, round tower—presenting only the bare structure without any ornamentation. However, whoever wishes to build such a tower may decorate it according to their own taste. This tower should be erected in a city at the most suitable location and placed in the middle of a marketplace, so that one can see out over the entire city from it, and so that strangers in all the streets may know how to orient themselves <note>original: "zurichtē"</note> by this tower. This marketplace should be, at the very least, wide enough that one side of its square is five hundred feet <note>original: "schuch," literally "shoes"</note> long.

<sig>I ij</sig>

<vocab>tower, marketplace, square, feet, elongated bodies, orient/direct, city</vocab>

<summary>Albrecht Dürer describes the placement and purpose of a massive civic tower intended for the center of a large marketplace, emphasizing its role as a visual landmark for orientation.</summary>
<keywords>architecture, city planning, marketplace, landmark, orientation, tower design, measurements</keywords>

--- Page 104 ---
<meta>This page continues Dürer’s detailed technical instructions for the construction of a civic tower. While the previous page established the tower's location and purpose within a city marketplace, this page provides specific measurements for its base, walls, internal staircase, and roof structure.</meta>

In this place, set in the center a round set of steps ten feet <note>original: "schuch," literally "shoes"</note> high, consisting of eighteen steps. Make each step one foot wide so that the ascent <note>original: "drit," referring to the tread or pace</note> is gentle. Set the diameter or the straight-line measurement <note>original: "ortstrich"</note> through the bottom step, where the staircase is widest, at one hundred feet. Thus, the diameter of the topmost step will be six hundred and forty-one feet <note>The OCR likely misreads "sechs vnd achtzig" (eighty-six) as "sechs hundert vnd eyn vnd vierzig" (six hundred and forty-one); in a staircase narrowing by one foot each side over eighteen steps, the diameter would mathematically decrease from 100 to 64.</note>. These steps serve so that one may see from them what is happening throughout the marketplace and what is being offered for sale.

Next, place the tower in the middle of these steps and make it forty feet wide at the bottom, including the walls. Make the walls ten feet thick at the bottom, so that the tower remains twenty feet hollow on the inside across its diameter. Carry this same interior width straight up so that it stands perfectly vertical until the walkway that is to be built. At that point, make the wall five feet thick, so that the wall leans inward on the outside and at the top, and the tower becomes one-quarter narrower at the top than at the bottom. This is aesthetically pleasing and structurally sound.

Then, make the tower two hundred feet high from the base up to the roofing; thus, its height will be five times its bottom width. Inside the tower, along the wall, build a shallow spiral staircase <term>schnecken stiegen|literally "snail stairs," a common term for a winding or helical staircase</term> all the way up to the walkway. It should be of such a scale that, if necessary, one could even ride a horse up it. For this staircase, use the spiral line <note>original: "schneken lini," a geometric helix</note> shown in the first book in figure 17. 

Afterward, make a narrow gallery <note>original: "vmsgang"</note> around the tower, level with the top of the wall. Including the cornice <term>gesims|a decorative architectural molding</term> and all else, it should not project further out than eight feet, and let it extend three feet out from the wall. However, you may allow the upper cornice to project further. 

Then, place a masonry roof atop the tower walls and draw the outer form using the line from the first book in figure 30 <note>Dürer refers back to his earlier geometric proofs to define the specific curvature of the roof</note>. However, for the interior, draw the line with a compass so that the roof wall is thinner at the top than at the bottom. Make this roofing fifty feet high up to the bottom of the bell house. 

As for the little bell house <note>original: "glockenheuslein"</note>, make it five feet wide and ten feet high. The upper half should be open between the cornices, interspersed with columns. Make the roof on top of it ten feet high as well, and the shape of the roof is drawn together at the top with two outward-curving compass lines. 

Next, make the pole, the finial <note>original: "knopf," literally "button" or "knob"</note>, and the weather vane <note>original: "fannen," literally "flags"</note> ten feet high as well. The tower warden's <note>original: "thurner"</note> quarters should be within the roofing, so that he may give signals and regulate the hours <note>original: "die oz richt," referring to the striking of the clock or monitoring the time</note>. This tower is drawn in elevation <note>original: "aufgerissen," an architectural term for a vertical profile drawing</note> below.

--- Page 106 ---
<meta>This page continues from the previous illustration of the monumental tower, moving from the architectural design to the practical geometry required to measure such a structure in the field.</meta>

Now it happens that sometimes a person would like to know how high a tower is. To determine this, many instruments are used, such as the <term>Astrolabe</term> <note>original: 'Astrolabium'; an ancient instrument used to measure the altitude of stars or the heights of buildings</note> and its <term>quadrant</term>, the <term>Jacob’s staff</term> <note>original: 'baculus Jacob'; a tool used by sailors and surveyors to measure angles by sliding a cross-piece along a staff</note>, and the like. However, I want to show a simple <note>original: 'schlecht'; in this context meaning straightforward or basic rather than 'bad'</note> method here.

If you wish to determine the height of a tower, mark the top point **B** and the bottom point **C**, and let your eye be point **A**. Position yourself as far from the tower as you like, but at the same level as the base **C**. 

Then take a right-angled <term>square</term> <note>original: 'winckelmas'; a L-shaped tool for ensuring perfect 90-degree angles</note> and, on one end of it, attach a small <term>straight-edge</term> <note>original: 'richtscheytlein'; a sighting rule or alidade</note> that pivots on a small nail so that it is movable at the front. Place the square along the line **A-C** so that the vertical side faces the tower. Position your eye at the back where the movable straight-edge is pivoted, and aim the straight-edge at the top height **B**. 

Once you have the correct height, mark the spot on the square beneath the straight-edge and fix it in place. Then, staying in the same spot, lay your square and straight-edge down sideways toward your <term>horizon</term> <note>original: 'orizon'; the level ground or horizontal plane</note>. The height of the tower should then be laid out along the ground; mark this with a point **D**. In this way, two identical <term>triangles</term> are created: the upright triangle of the tower’s height, **A-B-C**, and the triangle laid flat, **A-C-D**. Thus, the height **B-C** and the distance on the ground **C-D** are of equal length. 

This method does not fail, but you must be diligent in your measuring. I have illustrated this below.

<summary>A practical guide to measuring the height of a tower using basic geometric principles and a handmade sighting tool, rather than complex astronomical instruments.</summary>
<keywords>geometry, surveying, measurement, tower height, Astrolabe, Jacob's staff, right-angle square, congruent triangles</keywords>

### [Diagram: The Geometry of Height Measurement]

A technical geometric diagram, likely from a treatise on surveying or perspective, demonstrating a method for measuring height via triangulation. 

On the left, a tall, cylindrical column is depicted resting on a base of three concentric steps. A vertical axis is drawn through the center of the column, extending from an ornamental finial at the top down to a point below the horizontal ground line. Specific points on this vertical axis are labeled with lowercase letters:
- **a**: Located at the top of the column's ornament.
- **c**: Located at the intersection of the column's base and the ground line.
- **d**: Located at the bottom terminus of the vertical line, below ground level.

On the right side of the diagram, a sighting instrument or measuring device is shown, consisting of a vertical post and a triangular frame. A horizontal ground line connects point **c** to the base of the instrument's vertical post. Two diagonal lines converge at a single vertex on the far right of the device (labeled with point **b**): one line descends from point **a** and the other rises from point **d**. Both diagonal lines pass through the cross-beams of the sighting device's triangular frame before meeting at the vertex.

--- Page 108 ---
<meta>This page transitions from general geometric surveying techniques to the specific application of geometry for constructing sundials, continuing the theme of practical mathematics for craftsmen.</meta>

It is also useful for stonemasons, painters, and carpenters to be able to set up a common sundial <note>original: "sonnē o:", an abbreviation for *Sonnenuhr*</note> on towers, houses, and walls. Therefore, I will demonstrate a small portion of this in the following pages, as much as is necessary for the common man, and teach how to make small clocks <note>original: "o:", abbreviation for *Uhren*</note> of twelve hours.

First, learn how to draw a vertical line from a point that stands above a straight line, as was also shown previously in the booklet on lines; but for better understanding, find it thus: Draw a line **a. b.**, and above it place a point **c.** Then place a compass with one foot on that point, and with the other draw an arc <note>original: *cirkeltrum*</note> across the straight line **a. b.**, and where it intersects, place **d.** and **e.** After that, place the compass with one foot on point **d.** and open the other as wide as you wish, and draw an arc through the line **a. b.** above and below. Do the same from point **e.** without moving the compass <note>keeping the same width</note>. Where these two curved lines intersect each other, place an **f.** at the top and a **g.** at the bottom. Then draw a straight line connecting **c. f.** and **g.**, and where it intersects **a. b.**, place an **h.** Thus, **c. h. g.** will be a vertical line at right angles. This is first necessary for sundials <note>original: *horalogien*</note> so that you know what a vertical line is, as it is drawn below.

<image-desc>A geometric diagram illustrating the construction of a perpendicular line. A horizontal line is labeled 'a' at the left end and 'b' at the right end. A point 'c' is placed above the center of the line. An arc centered at 'c' intersects the line 'ab' at two points labeled 'd' and 'e'. Two intersecting arcs, centered at points 'd' and 'e' respectively, cross each other at point 'f' (above the line, near 'c') and point 'g' (well below the line). A vertical line is drawn through points 'c', 'f', and 'g', intersecting the horizontal line 'ab' at a central point labeled 'h'.</image-desc>

Further, make a quarter of a circle <note>a quadrant</note> whose center is **a.** and the ends are **b.** and **c.** Divide this arc into ninety parts or degrees; first into three parts, then divide each third into three parts to make nine; then divide each of the nine parts into two to make eighteen; then divide each of the eighteen parts into five parts, so there are ninety parts in the arc of the circle. Stand this quadrant **c. a.** upright, and **a. b.** horizontally <note>original: *vberzwerch*</note>. The degrees should be counted upwards from **b.** toward **c.** 

Now, when you are in a city and wish to make a sundial, first seek—through an instrument or in tables made for this purpose—how high the pole <note>the North Star/celestial pole</note> is raised above the earth. For example, in Nuremberg its height is forty-nine degrees. So, count from **b.** upward to the end of the forty-ninth degree; there, place a **d.** and draw **a. d.** straight together. This line **a. d.** is called in Latin the *axis mundi*, which is the axis of the world <note>original: *axem mundi*</note>; that is the axle upon which the whole world turns. 

Then draw another vertical line, as you were taught before, from the point **d.** to the line **a. b.** at right angles; in that same point, place an **e.** This line is called in Latin the *muralis*, but in German the wall-line <note>original: *mauerlini*</note>, from which vertical sundials are made on walls. 

Then draw a straight line from point **e.** to the line **a. d.** at equal angles <note>perpendicularly</note> using the previous rule. Where this line intersects the axis or rod-line of the world, place an **f.** This line is called in Latin the *equinoctialis* <note>original: *equinotialē*</note>; that is the line that divides the axis of the world in the middle at equal angles. 

All these lines are now correctly set for Nuremberg, as is drawn hereafter, from which one can make sundials. This quadrant should be made large enough so that you can create the sundial from it, for you must use it constantly and work from it. The triangle <note>original: *dryangel*</note> **a. e. d.** may be made of wood or metal, into which one sets the style <note>the gnomon</note> or pointer of the hours. On a wall, **d.** should be fastened, and **a.** will stand free toward the pole under the earth, which in Latin is called *Antarcticus*. On a flat surface <note>a horizontal dial</note>, **a.** should be fastened, and **d.** pointed toward the *Arctic* pole <note>original: *polum articum*</note>. When **e. d.** lies in the *plano* or on the flat surface, then **d. a.** shows the height of the equinoctial.

### 21

<image-desc>
A geometric diagram showing a quadrant used for astronomical or gnomonic construction. The arc of the quadrant is graduated in five-degree increments from 5 to 90. A line labeled "Axis of the world" <note>original: "Axis mundi"</note> extends from the vertex "e" to the arc. Another line labeled "Equinoctial line" <note>original: "Equinotial lineale"</note> also extends from the vertex "e". The base of the quadrant is labeled "on the plane" <note>original: "in plano"</note>. Key points are marked with letters: "a" at the bottom left, "e" at the vertex, "b" at the bottom right, and "f" where the equinoctial line meets the arc. The word "zenith" <note>original: "zenit"</note> is written at the top of the vertical axis.
</image-desc>

Now begin to make the <term>horalogium</term> <note>a sundial or time-keeping device</note> and draw a straight line **g.h.** and draw another straight vertical line **k.l.** at right angles <note>original: "zü gleychen wincklen", literally "at equal angles"</note> right through the middle, and where they intersect each other, place an **m.** After that, take with a compass <note>the drafting tool</note> from the quadrant the length of the equinoctial line **e.f.** and place one foot in point **m.** and with the other draw a circle; this will be divided into four equal parts by the two previously drawn straight lines **g.h.** and **k.l.**, and where the circle line intersects the line **k.l.**, there place **n.** and **o.** Through these two points, draw two straight lines **p.q.** and **r.s.** that are parallel with the line **g.h.** After that, divide each quarter of the circle with points into 6 equal parts, so there will be twenty-four equal parts in the circle. After that, draw straight lines in the circle through the points that stand opposite each other through the center **m.** and all the way out to the two lines **p.q.** and **r.s.** and always search very diligently among the lines of the quadrant for where each particular sundial belongs to be made; but what was mentioned above is drawn out hereafter.

### 22

<image-desc>
A composite technical drawing for constructing a sundial. On the left is a smaller version of the previous quadrant diagram with labels including "zenith", "axis", "equinoctial", and "horizon". To the right is a larger rectangular projection. It features a central circle divided into twenty-four hour segments, numbered 1 through 12 twice. Horizontal lines at the top and bottom are labeled "sunset" <note>original: "vndergang"</note> and "sunrise" <note>original: "Aufgang"</note>. The diagram includes a network of intersecting lines for determining hour positions. Points are labeled with letters "p", "q", "g", "r", "l", "k", "m", "n", and "o". Additional hour numbers (4 to 8) are marked along the bottom horizontal line.
</image-desc>

Thus the above-mentioned serves for the equinoctial <note>the celestial equator</note> and midday. Place the <term>stil</term> <note>the gnomon or pointer of the sundial</note> in the middle at the center **m.**, upright against the equinoctial; take its length from the center **m.** to point **n.** or **o.** Make the styles for the <term>oriental</term> <note>east-facing</note> and <term>occidental</term> <note>west-facing</note> sundials also in this way; take their length from half the diameter of their equinoctial circle. After that, take with a compass from the previously made triangle the length **d.e.** and

<summary>These pages provide step-by-step geometric instructions for drafting the layout of a sundial, including how to divide a circle into twenty-four hours and determine the proper length of the gnomon (style).</summary>
<keywords>sundial construction, horalogium, equinoctial line, gnomon, style, geometry, quadrant, zenith, astronomical drafting</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for constructing sundials using geometric principles. It follows the establishment of basic coordinates to provide specific measurements for vertical and horizontal dial faces, specifically tailored for the latitude of Nuremberg.</meta>

Place one foot of the compass in point **n.** and the other on the line **l. f.** In that same spot, place a **v.** At this center, let the compass stand still with one foot, and with the other, draw a circle-line out from point **n.** After that, draw straight lines from this center **v.** into the points of line **p. q.** 

Next, take with a compass the length **a. e.** from the previously mentioned triangle and place one foot in point **o.** and the other on line **f. l.** In that same point, place an **r.** From there, draw straight lines to points **r. s.** After that, place one foot of the compass in point **r.** and draw a circle-line with the other through point **o.** Thus, the upper circle is made from the aforementioned length **d. e.** and is meant to be placed on an upright wall; but the lower circle, which is made from the length **a. e.**, is meant to be placed on the <term>horizon</term> <note>the flat, horizontal ground</note> or a level plane. As it is drawn below, from which the side <term>horologia</term> <note>time-keeping devices or sundials</note> are also to be taken.

<image-desc>A detailed geometric diagram for the construction of sundials, likely from Albrecht Dürer's "Underweysung der Messung". A central square is inscribed with diagonals, midpoints (a, b, d, e), and corners (l, m, n, f). Above the square is a semicircle divided into 12 hour segments, labeled "This belongs on the upright wall", flanked by "before noon" and "after noon". Below the square is another semicircle for a horizontal dial, labeled "This belongs on the horizon or plane", also flanked by "before noon" and "after noon". To the left of the square is the label "Towards sunset" with a lunar symbol, and to the right "Towards sunrise" with a solar symbol. Various construction points (p, v, q, r, s, o) and lines are marked to assist the reader in replicating the geometry.</image-desc>

For these <term>horologia</term>, one may make various multi-sided blocks <note>polyhedrons</note> upon which all sorts of sundials can be constructed. However, here I wish to demonstrate a common use by means of a <term>cubum</term> <note>a cube</note> which should be trimmed in several places; then, one shall place sundials upon the fields <note>the faces of the cube</note> that the sun may shine upon. 

Do it as follows: mark the cube on one side at its four corners with **f. l. m. n.** and place a point **c.** in the middle of it. From there, draw a small circle that touches the four sides of the square, and divide the circle with two straight lines into four equal parts at right angles, so that the horizontal line **a. c. b.** signifies the <term>horizon</term> <note>original: "orizon"</note>, and the vertical line at the top is the <term>zenith</term> <note>original: "zenit"</note> above the head, and at the bottom **e.** is the foot or base. 

Then divide each quarter of the circle into ninety parts as taught above, and count upwards from **b.** toward **d.** as many degrees as the <term>pole</term> <note>original: "polus"; referring to the celestial pole or the observer's latitude</note> stands high where you are located. But here in Nuremberg, as has been mentioned, the <term>elevation</term> <note>latitude</note> is forty-nine degrees. Where this degree ends, place a point **f.** 

Afterward, draw a straight line **f. c.** all the way to the circle line and place a **g.** there. This line indicates the <term>axis of the world</term> <note>original: "axem mundi"</note> around which the world turns. According to this line, the rods <note>gnomons</note> that cast the shadow on the walls should be directed. However, on the vertical side and on the level plane, one may direct the rods according to the <term>equinoctial</term> <note>the celestial equator</note> or zenith. The <term>styles</term> <note>the shadow-casting pins</note> of the four sundials in the circle of the equinoctial should each stand upright in its field at equal [right] angles. Then draw a straight cross-line through the center **c.** from one end of the circle to the other, so that the line makes four equal angles with the axis, and place at its ends **h. i.** This line indicates the <term>equinoctial</term> <note>the celestial equator</note> on both sides.

<summary>This page provides geometric instructions for drafting both vertical and horizontal sundials, specifying the necessary angles and points of orientation. It uses Nuremberg's latitude (49 degrees) as a primary example for setting the "axis of the world" to ensure accurate timekeeping.</summary>
<keywords>sundial construction, gnomonics, Nuremberg latitude, axis mundi, equinoctial line, geometry, Albrecht Dürer, horologia</keywords>

--- Page 111 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for constructing complex sundials. Having previously established the basic geometric framework, the author now explains how to orient the celestial axes—specifically calibrated for the latitude of Nuremberg—and how to modify a cube into an octagonal shape to accommodate multiple dial faces.</meta>

...through the points **h.** and **i.** Since this line of the <term>equinoctial</term> <note>original: "equinoctials"; the celestial equator</note> now stands crosswise at right angles over the line of the <term>axis of the world</term> <note>original: "ax mundi"; the imaginary line around which the heavens rotate</note>, as much as the axis rises with its end **f.**, so much does the cross-line of the equinoctial descend with its point **h.** Likewise, the opposite part of the pole (marked by **g.**) and the opposite part of the equinoctial (understood at point **i.**) move in the same manner. For whatever is added to the pole is taken away from the equinoctial. Thus, forty-nine degrees will remain in all four quadrants; this is the altitude of the pole at Nuremberg <note>Nuremberg is located at approximately 49 degrees North latitude</note>. The remaining forty-one degrees up to the <term>zenith</term> <note>the point directly overhead</note> is the distance from the equinoctial to our <term>horizon</term> <note>original: "orizon"</note>. 

By this method, the <term>cube</term> <note>original: "cubus"</note> on the square sides **k. l. m. n.** is divided into eight corners. You can correctly form the parallel lines if you draw the lines **f. g.** of the axis of the world and **h. i.** of the equinoctial all the way out on both sides through the cube. Then, following the first rule mentioned above, look for the cross-lines on all four sides **h. f. i. g.** as they are marked there. Along these lines, cut off the four sharp edges of the cube; from this, eight square fields will be created. Through the middle of all of them, the midday line <note>the meridian</note> shall be drawn. The two side faces will then have eight corners, though this squared body will be unequal. Place the side with the drawing toward the rising of the sun <note>East</note> and its lower part toward the setting sun <note>West</note>. Position the point **a.** toward midday <note>South</note> and **b.** toward midnight <note>North</note>. After that, arrange the <term>horologia</term> <note>original: "horalogia"; time-keeping instruments or sundial faces</note> on all the square fields where the sun reaches, as well as on the two side faces toward the sunrise and sunset, just as it is all illustrated below.

<summary>This page provides the geometric calculations to adjust a sundial for the latitude of Nuremberg (49°) and explains how to transform a cube into an eight-sided figure to host multiple sundial faces.</summary>
<keywords>Nuremberg, latitude, axis mundi, equinoctial, sundial construction, geometry, horologia, celestial coordinates</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for constructing sundials (horalogia) from the previous page, specifically focusing on dials oriented toward the celestial equator (equinoctial) and those placed on walls regardless of their orientation.</meta>

The following sundial <term>horalogium: an instrument for telling time, usually a sundial</term> should be made facing the equinoctial <term>equinoctial: the celestial equator, where day and night are of equal length</term> below the earth toward midnight <note>original: "mitternacht"; here used to mean the North</note>. Take the length of the style <term>stills: the gnomon or pin of a sundial that casts the shadow</term> from the middle center to the line of eight and four on the middle line.

[A technical diagram for a sundial component consisting of a horizontal rectangle. Above and inside the rectangle are labels 'a' and 'b' and the numbers 7, 8, 6, 4, and 5 marking specific points. A vertical line passes through the center point '6'.]

->a. b. is the length of the style.<-
->a. is the point where the style should stand upright.<-

Likewise, to make sundials on any wall, whatever its orientation may be, and to find the markings for all sides—whether it hangs in the zenith <term>zenit: the point directly overhead</term> tilting backward or forward—seek this through two graduated, shifted lines: aa. and bb. The first is for the horizontal and the second for the vertical line; by these, the rays <term>radij: the hour lines radiating from the center</term> are made longer or shorter, and the fields between them wider or narrower, as is drawn out below.

[A complex geometric projection used to construct a sundial on a wall. A central vertical axis is labeled 'zenith' at the top and 'base' at the bottom. Two circles and various intersecting diagonal and horizontal lines create a grid for hour markers. Numbers 7 through 12 and 1 through 8 are placed at the intersections. Points 'a' and 'b' are marked on the horizontal extremities. A mark '27' appears on the left side of the upper circle.]

And although there are many types of sundials in which one can see at every hour what the rising sign <note>the zodiac sign appearing on the eastern horizon</note> is, how the day increases and decreases, in which sign the sun is located, the planetary hours, and the like, I have nonetheless only wished to show the common hours. I have done this as briefly as possible, as the method of indicating these hours is not my primary purpose, but merely an incidental addition <note>original: "zufal"</note>.

--- Page 114 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the technical construction of sundials to the application of geometry in architectural lettering and perspective. While the previous page focused on "radij" (hour lines) and wall-mounted clocks, Dürer now applies these principles of projection to ensure legibility for text and sculpture placed high on buildings.</meta>

It often happens that one places writing on columns, towers, or high walls. Therefore, whoever wishes to write on a tower so that the top row of letters is as easy to read as the bottom row must make the upper ones larger than those at the bottom. This is achieved in the following way: position your eye as far from the tower and at whatever height you wish; let this be point **c**. Use the method of the triangle **a.b.c.** from the 16th figure of the first book <note>Dürer refers here to the first volume of this treatise, *Underweysung der Messung*, which establishes the basics of lines and shapes.</note>, and let **a.b.** represent the height of the tower or wall on which you wish to write. 

Now, divide the circular arc <term>cirkeldums: a segment of a circle or an arc</term> **b.e.** with points spaced equally for the rows of text you wish to write. Then, from the viewpoint at point **c**, draw straight lines through all the points on the arc **b.e.** until they reach the vertical height of the tower or wall **a.b.** Next, draw horizontal lines <term>parzlinien: horizontal or parallel construction lines</term> from these points across the face of the tower. You must place your writing between these lines. This will show you how much larger the upper letters must become compared to the lower ones. 

If you wish to divide a short line **f.g.** proportionally to the long line **a.b.**, draw all the lines straight to point **c** and intercept them with an upright parallel line **f.g.** facing point **c**. Thus, **f.g.** will be divided exactly like **a.b.**, to which it is parallel. This method is used in "fore-setting" or "hind-setting" <note>Early terminology for perspective foregrounding and backgrounding</note> to make things larger or smaller. In this way, all lines are to be divided according to others, whether in equal or unequal proportions, and even in parts that cannot be named <note>likely referring to irrational lengths or divisions that cannot be expressed as simple fractions</note>. 

This method of division applies not only to letters, but to all other things as well. It is especially useful when one wishes to decorate all the levels <term>gaden: the stories or tiers of a building</term> of a high tower with sculpture <note>original: "bildwercken"; referring to statues, reliefs, or carved ornaments</note>, so that the upper figures appear equal to the lower ones. This can be accomplished through this method, as illustrated below.

[A geometric diagram illustrating perspectival correction. A vertical wall **a-b** is on the left, with an observer at point **c** to the right. Lines radiate from **c**, passing through a small scale **f-g** and hitting the wall **a-b**. The wall is divided into three horizontal bands of text. Because of the angle of the viewer's eye, the top band is physically much taller than the bottom band so that they appear the same size from the ground.]

->THE WORD OF GOD REMAINS<-
<note>original: "DAS W GOTTES BLEIBT"; the 'W' is notably enlarged for the demonstration.</note>

->ETERNALLY THIS WORD IS CHRIST<-
<note>original: "EWIGLICH DIS WORT IST CRISTVS"</note>

->THE SALVATION OF ALL CHRISTIAN BELIEVERS<-
<note>original: "ALLER CRIST GLAVBIGEN HEIL"</note>

<summary>Albrecht Dürer explains a geometric method for "optical correction," showing how to scale letters and sculptures on high walls so they appear uniform to a viewer on the ground. This reflects the Renaissance concern with applying mathematical perspective to architecture and public monuments.</summary>
<keywords>perspective, architectural lettering, optical correction, Albrecht Dürer, geometry, proportional scaling, Renaissance art theory</keywords>

--- Page 115 ---
<meta>This page continues Dürer’s transition from geometric perspective to the practical application of geometry in typography. Having explained how to adjust the size of letters for height in the previous section, he now begins a detailed technical manual on the geometric construction of the Latin (Roman) alphabet, ensuring that builders and artists can create perfectly proportioned inscriptions.</meta>

<sig>K ij</sig>

Since builders, as well as painters and others, frequently have occasion to place inscriptions on high walls, it is necessary that they learn to form letters correctly. Therefore, I wish to demonstrate a little of this here: first, by providing a Latin <note>original: "Lateinisch." Dürer refers to the Roman Antiqua script.</note> alphabet, and afterwards a Textur <note>A Gothic or "Blackletter" script.</note> script, as these two types of writing are commonly used for such purposes.

For the first part, the Latin letters, make a perfect square <term>vierung: a square construction frame</term> for each individual letter to be contained within. When you draw the letter inside it, make its thicker stroke one-tenth as wide as the length of the square's side. Make the thinner stroke one-third as wide as the thicker one. Observe this rule for every letter throughout the entire alphabet.

First, make the **a** in this manner: mark the corners of its square with **a. b. c. d.**—do this for all letters—and divide this square with two crossing lines: the vertical **e. f.** and the horizontal **g. h.** After that, place two points, **i. k.**, at the bottom of the square near **c. d.**, each one-tenth of the way inward. Draw the thin stroke of the letter from **i** upward to the top of the square. Then draw the thick stroke back down, so that the outer edges of both strokes touch the points **i. k.** This leaves a triangle in the middle, while point **e** falls at the top center of the letter. Next, join the **a** with a cross-stroke below the horizontal line **g. h.**; make this stroke one-third the width of the larger (thick) stroke. Then, at the top of the thick stroke, let a small rounded arc <note>original: "cirkelckun"</note> extend backward out beyond the square, and finish the top of the letter with a wavy line <term>schlangenlini: literally a 'snake-line' or serpentine curve</term>, positioned so that the hollow faces the thin stroke. Curve the feet of the letter outward on both sides at the bottom so that they touch the corners of the square **c. d.** Use a compass for this, with a radius <term>halben Diameter: literally "half diameter"</term> equal to one-seventh of the square's side. On the inside of the feet, however, let the curve project two-thirds the width of the thick stroke; draw this on both sides with a circular arc whose diameter is equal to the width of the thick stroke.

Likewise, you may also cut this **a** off flat at the top of the square and curve the letter outward on both sides just as you did at the bottom, though the front part should be longer. In this case, however, the strokes must be moved a bit closer together at the top. You should use whichever of these three styles pleases you best. Note that just as this letter **a** is curved at the top and bottom, you should also curve the letters that are drawn diagonally <term>ortschelchs: slanted or transverse</term>, such as **v. r. y.**, though with slight variations, as you will hear later. Furthermore, you may make the **a** in yet another way: namely, sharp at the top, so the strokes lean closer together. Then, move the cross-bar a little lower and make it as wide as before; you may also cut the stroke off bluntly at the top or curve it outward in front. This letter is drafted <term>aufgerissen: technically drawn or illustrated</term> below.

[The technical illustration shows the geometric construction of the capital letter 'A' within a square frame, labeled with points a through k. Several variations of the letter 'A' are shown to the right and bottom, illustrating different serif styles—pointed, flat-topped, and decorative.]

<summary>Albrecht Dürer begins his guide to typography by explaining the geometric proportions of the Latin alphabet, specifically focusing on the construction of the letter 'A' using a square grid and precise ratios.</summary>
<keywords>typography, geometry, Latin alphabet, Roman letters, proportions, technical drawing, serifs, Albrecht Dürer</keywords>

--- Page 116 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for Albrecht Dürer's "Latin" (Roman Antiqua) alphabet. Having previously described the construction of the letter 'A' using a square grid, he now moves on to the letter 'B', offering two distinct methods: one based on perfect circles and another that mimics the natural swelling of a pen stroke.</meta>

Next, construct the **b** within its <term>vierung: a square construction frame</term> **a. b. c. d.** in this way: First, divide the square in two with a horizontal line **e. f.** After that, divide the sections **a. b.** and **e. f.** with another line **g. h.** also into two parts. Then, place the first upright, thick stroke of the letter as far into the square from the line **a. c.** as the stroke itself is wide. 

Next, draw an upright line **i. k.** behind the letter’s stroke toward the center, at a distance of one-tenth the length of the square's side. Where this intersects the line **g. h.**, mark it with an **l.** Then, draw the thin horizontal strokes from the upright stroke of the letter (from which the two rounded "bellies" <note>Dürer uses the term "beuch" (bellies) to refer to what modern typographers call the "bowls" of a letter.</note> are drawn) until they reach the upright line **i. k.**—one at the top under line **a. b.**, one above line **e. f.**, and one above the bottom line **c. d.**

After that, place a compass with one foot on point **l** and draw a semicircle with the other, inside the horizontal strokes, so that both ends of the curved line touch the upright line **i. k.** below **a. b.** and above **e. f.** 

Then, divide the letter's narrow horizontal stroke above **e. f.** at line **i. k.** with a point **m** in the middle. Next, on the line **g. h.**, mark the width of the letter's stroke with a point **n** behind the curved line. Then draw a short horizontal line from point **m** above **e. f.** toward **f**, as far as you need it. Next, draw a semicircle that touches the aforementioned line, point **n**, and the top horizontal line **a. b.** After that, draw an upright line through point **n**. All of these define the inner curves of the bowl, just as the others define the outer curves.

Next, draw the horizontal stroke above **c. d.** as long toward **d** as you need; mark that point **q.** Then divide **m. q.** with a horizontal line **o. p.** in two parts; where this intersects line **n**, mark it **r.** Then draw a semicircle touching the horizontal **e. f.**, point **r**, and point **q.** Next, mark the width of the letter's stroke with a point **s** behind **r** above the line **o. p.**, and draw a semicircle touching line **m**, point **s**, and line **c. d.** 

This leaves three "corners" in the letter <note>These are the sharp internal angles where the curves meet the straight strokes.</note>; however, in the bottom-most corner, draw a rounded hollow with a compass whose radius <note>original: "halben Diameter"</note> is two-thirds the width of the letter's thick stroke. Furthermore, make the outer flourishes <note>original: "ausschwenffung." These are the serifs or the graceful curves where the vertical stem meets the horizontal base.</note> at the top and bottom of the straight stroke using a compass with a radius equal to the width of the letter’s stroke.

### An Alternative Method

Or, you may make the **b** in this way: divide the side of the square **a. c.** into nine parts, and cut off the top four parts with a horizontal line **e. f.** Then make the upright stroke as described before, but place the upper bowl between **a. b.** and **e. f.**, and draw the lower bowl between **e. f.** and **c. d.** 

Next, divide **a. b.** into nine fields and mark off the four fields near **b** with a point **g.** Then divide **c. d.** into five fields, and mark off the one closest to **d** with a point **h.** Then draw a line **g. h.** This line must touch the outside of the upper and lower bowls. 

However, these bowls must be drawn in a special way, and the compass for the rounded strokes must be shifted along the guide lines <term>ortlini: a diagonal or positioning line used to shift the center point of a curve</term>. Create those two guide lines as follows: divide **a. e.** into four fields and mark the one closest to **e** with an **i.** Then mark the fifth part of **e. c.** above **c** with a point **k.** Then draw lines **i. b.** and **k. f.** straight together. Move the compass along these lines to draw the two bowls—the upper and lower—and they will become thicker at the top than the bottom, just as a pen <note>original: "feder" (quill)</note> naturally produces. Because of this, these bowls will not be perfectly circular; you must shift the compass along the guide line and assist the shape with a freehand stroke <term>handzug: a manual, freehand adjustment to a geometric shape</term>, as I have drawn below.

<summary>This page provides two technical methods for constructing the Roman letter 'B', detailing how to use a compass and grid to achieve either perfect geometric symmetry or a more natural, calligraphic appearance.</summary>
<keywords>Typography, Letter B, Geometry, Albrecht Dürer, Compass construction, Calligraphy, Proportions, Serifs</keywords>

--- Page 117 ---
<meta>This page continues Albrecht Dürer’s manual on the geometry of letters. Having just finished the construction of the letter 'B' on the previous page, Dürer now transitions to the letter 'C'. He provides two distinct methods for its construction: one based on uniform circles and another that mimics the natural variation of a calligrapher’s pen stroke.</meta>

<page-type>text</page-type>
<sig>K iij</sig>

# B B <note>While the running head continues from the previous section on the letter 'B', the text below begins the instructions for the letter 'C'.</note>

Afterward, construct the **c** in its <term>vierung: square construction frame</term> in this manner: draw a horizontal line **e. f.** through the middle of the square, and place a point **i** in the middle of line **e. f.** From there, draw a circle <note>Dürer uses the term "cirkelriß" to mean a line drawn with a compass.</note> that touches the four sides of the square **a. b. c. d.** Next, place the compass—without changing its width—on line **e. f.** behind point **i**, at a distance equal to the width of the letter's thickest stroke, at point **k**. Draw another circle there; this one passes over line **b. d.** and gives the front of the letter’s curve its proper thickness. 

Next, draw an upright line **g. h.** at a distance of one-tenth the side **b. d.** from the edge; this line cuts off the top and bottom of this letter **c**, as the ancients used to do. However, here I prefer to cut it in the middle between **g. h.** and **b. d.** Then, draw by hand the inner curves at the top and bottom where the two circles intersect, completing the thickness of the letter's thinner strokes. Also, at those same points, draw the curves for the top and bottom of the letter toward the sides of the square **a. b.** and **c. d.** But at the bottom, where the letter extends toward **g. h.**, draw the shape of the letter slightly more curved than the compass line, so that its end touches the compass line further out at the tip. Likewise, make the inner part of the upper section slightly more hollow than the circle line gives. Thus, the two compass circles provide almost the entire shape of the letter.

For the second method, construct the **c** like this: draw a <term>diagonal: the line connecting opposite corners of the square</term> <note>original: "Diameter.c.b."</note> **c. b.** within the square. Place one foot of the compass on point **i** and draw the outer circle as before with the other foot, but let it end at the top on the diagonal **c. b.**, and at the bottom, let the circle line extend a little further than before. 

Next, place one foot of the fixed compass above point **i** on the diagonal at a distance equal to the width of the letter's stroke, and draw the inner circle with the other foot. This causes the stroke to naturally follow the movement of a pen, becoming wider at the bottom than at the top. Afterward, draw the rest by hand, and let the clipped ends of the letter slant forward at the top and downward at the bottom, just as I have drawn below.
