<meta>This page transitions from the purely geometric construction of three-dimensional forms—specifically the doubling of the cube discussed on the previous page—to the practical application of these forms in art. It introduces the foundational principles of perspective and the behavior of light and shadow.</meta>

<page-type>text</page-type>

<image-desc>Four square diagrams are arranged in a vertical column. Each square contains an inscribed circle. In the center of each circle is a numeral written in 16th-century script: the first circle contains a vertical stroke with a dot (1), the second contains a cursive numeral 2, the third contains a cursive numeral 3 with a horizontal crossbar, and the fourth contains a looped numeral 4.</image-desc>

Just as I have previously shown various <term>solids</term> <note>original: "corpora," the Latin plural for bodies; here referring to three-dimensional geometric shapes.</note> and how to construct them, I now wish to teach how, when such <term>constructions</term> <note>original: "gemecht"</note> are viewed, they may be brought into a <term>painting</term> <note>original: "gemel," an archaic term for a pictorial representation or drawing.</note>. For this purpose, I will take the simplest solid, such as the <term>cube</term> <note>original: "würffel"</note>, and demonstrate thereby that one may treat all solids in this same manner. I shall also provide some understanding of <term>light</term> <note>original: "liecht"</note> and <term>shadow</term> <note>original: "schatten"</note>, and how to use one with the other.

For whatever is to be seen must be present before us and is perceived with the eye. To this also belongs a light, for darkness allows nothing to be seen; furthermore, there must be a medium between the eye and that which is seen, as follows hereafter.

Every light extends through straight lines as far as its rays travel. However, if an opaque object is placed before the light, the rays are blocked by it, and a shadow falls as far as the rays of light are withheld. This I will demonstrate in the <term>diagram</term> <note>original: "auffreysse," a technical drawing, plan, or draft.</note>.

<summary>The author moves from the construction of geometric solids to the principles of perspective, explaining that representing objects in a painting requires an understanding of the eye, a medium, and the way light rays are blocked by opaque objects to create shadows.</summary>
<keywords>perspective, geometric solids, cube, light and shadow, optics, artistic theory, Albrecht Dürer</keywords>

--- Page 167 ---
<meta>This page continues the instructional sequence on perspective and geometric construction. Having established the nature of light and solids on the previous page, the author now provides a step-by-step practical demonstration using a cube as the primary example.</meta>

Therefore, first make a flattened square base that is right-angled: **e. f. g. h.** Upon this plane, place a **cube**; in the plan view, this will appear as a square field, because the four top corners fall exactly upon the four bottom ones. For this reason, each corner is marked twice: the bottom four corners are **a. b. c. d.** but the top four are **1. 2. 3. 4.** Thus, **a1. b2. c3. d4.** come together.

Now this flattened base is finished.

Next, you must project this square field and the cube standing upon it upward, just as a <term>stonemason</term> <note>original: "steinmetz"; Dürer frequently draws on the practical, geometric methods used by builders and craftsmen of his time.</note> projects his ground plan in a drawing.

To do this, proceed as follows: above the square field **e. f. g. h.** make a <term>transverse line</term> <note>original: "zwerch par lini"; a horizontal line used here to represent the ground plane in an elevation or profile view.</note> as long as the square field is. Let the beginning be **e. h.** and the end **f. g.** This line represents the same thing as the lower plane **e. f. g. h.**, which is why its ends are marked twice.

Afterward, draw vertical lines upward from the flattened base of the cube **a1. b2. c3. d4.** through the transverse line **e. h. f. g.** as high as the cube is intended to be. In this way, you find the position of the cube as it stands upright upon the aforementioned transverse line. Thus, the bottom side of the cube, which stands upon the transverse lines **e. h.** and **f. g.**, has one end at **a. d.** and the other at **b. c.**; however, the top sides will be **1. 4.** and **2. 3.** Thus, the four corners of the projected cube—**1. 4.**, **2. 3.**, **b. c.**, and **a. d.**—are understandably positioned above the lower ground plan.

Next, the light must be placed. In the same way that I have set two views—one flattened plan and one projected elevation—I must also set a separate light for each view. For the elevation, I set the height or depth of the light; in the ground plan, I set the distance to the side or in the middle.

Proceed in this manner: set a light **o** as high and as far as you wish against the elevation view. And for the ground plan, set the other light **p** on whichever side you wish, provided that both light points stand at the same distance from the ground plan and the projected cube. Afterward, draw two straight rays from the upper point **o** of the light through the top corners of the projected cube **1. 4.** and **2. 3.** Where these rays fall upon the line **e. h.** and **f. g.**, place two points **i. k.**; this is how far the shadow reaches. 

Then, from point **p** of the lower light, draw the rays through the corners of the flattened base of the cube, which are marked **b2. c3.** and **d4.**, and let the rays extend forward. Where vertical lines are then drawn down from points **i. k.** through the rays of the lower light in the ground plan, those intersections define the length and width of the shadow. Mark the corners of the shadow **l. m. n.** Join these together with straight lines; likewise, join **b2. l. d4.** and **n.** together. By doing this, you have the complete outline of the shadow.

And note especially that these two lights are actually one light, just as the line **e. h.** and **f. g.** and the <term>plane</term> <note>original: "plano"; the flat surface on which the object sits.</note> beneath it **e. f. g. h.** are one thing, as previously mentioned. The flattened and projected cubes are also one. In short, both plans and both lights are as one single thing, but they are split apart for the sake of practical use. This is drawn out hereafter.

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for drawing a cube in perspective, moving from the geometric construction of shadows to the fundamental principles of optics and the "picture plane" (the transparent intersection between the eye and the object).</meta>

IF you now wish to bring this previously described cube, on its squared field with light and shadow, into a perspective drawing <note>original: "abgestolen gemel," literally a "stolen painting," referring to a drawing projected or "taken" from nature through perspective</note>, it is necessary that you know what belongs to it and by what means it is to be made.

Therefore, first, the **point of the eye** must be set.

Second, the thing that is to be seen, whether directly opposite or to one side. The third is the **light**, without which nothing is seen, as mentioned before.

The eye sees the things before it only through straight lines, and cannot see through any curved line; therefore, when two identical opaque things stand one behind the other and the eye is directly opposite them, only the front one can be seen and the one behind cannot. Therefore, if much is to be seen, those same things must be separated from one another so that the **rays of sight** <term>streym linien; the visual rays that extend from the eye to the object</term> may grasp them. There must also be a reasonable distance or length between the eye and that which is to be seen. For this reason, one should not place the object so close to the eye that it cannot be covered <note>i.e., that it overwhelms the field of vision</note> and blocks the sight, for many large things are seen from the small part of the sight at the correct distance. Also, the thing should not be placed too far away so that it is not lost to the sight; for when a thing stands so very far off, the rays of sight strike so close together at the eye that the eye can no longer see the small field between the rays. Here is a comparison drawn for you to follow.

<margin>
<image-desc>A vertical geometric diagram demonstrating the field of vision and perspective. At the bottom is a vertex point labeled 'a' representing the eye. Two lines radiate upwards from point 'a' to form a narrow triangular field. Three horizontal segments intersect these radiating lines at different distances from the eye. The furthest segment at the top is labeled 'b' on the left and 'c' on the right. The middle segment is labeled 'd' on the left and 'e' on the right. The segment closest to the eye is labeled 'f' on the left and 'g' on the right. A vertical scale or reference line runs along the right side of the diagram.</image-desc>
</margin>

But understand it thus: set a point **a**, which shall be your intended eye, and set a line **b c** very close before it, and let rays of sight go from point **a** to both ends **b** and **c**; thus you will find your eye entirely covered. Then take this line **b c** away and set another line **d e** at a reasonable distance further out, and draw rays of sight from **a** to both places **d** and **e**; the eye will see this well. Now let this line **d e** be taken away and let a line **f g** be placed very far off, and again draw rays of sight from **a** to the places **f** and **g**; then the rays of sight strike so close together back at the eye **a** that the eye can no longer well recognize the field between them. This is why when one sees a person from a great distance, the sight does not recognize him because of its weakness. Therefore, in such things, that which is to be clearly seen must stand at a recognizable distance. However, for seeing and making landscapes where one sees perhaps six or seven miles, that has its own special method.

Now note that between the distance of the eye and that which is seen, there shall be taken a flat, transparent **intersection** <note>original: "abschneydung," referring to the picture plane</note> of all the rays of sight that fall from the eye onto the things it sees. This flat, transparent field may be placed in the intersection close to the eye or far from it, or close to the thing it sees. If the plane is placed close to the sight, the painting that is to be made upon it will fall small; but if one moves the intersecting plane far from the eye and close to the thing one sees, the painting will fall larger upon it. Take this understanding of it: set two lines with both their ends together so that they form a pointed angle, which shall be **a**, and let the other two ends of the two lines be **b** and **c**. Between them, set two upright lines; let the one further from **a** be **d e**, and the one closer to **a** be **f g**. Where these two lines **d e** and **f g** are then cut off by the two forked lines **b** and **c**, from those same lines...

<sig>P</sig>

<summary>Dürer explains the optical principles of perspective, focusing on how the distance between the eye, the object, and the "picture plane" (intersection) determines the size and clarity of a drawing.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical explanation of optical principles, moving from the behavior of light and shadow to the geometry of the "cone of vision" and the practical steps for constructing a perspective drawing of a cube using plan and elevation views.</meta>

Draw straight rays <note>original: "streungen," meaning the spreading out of visual lines</note>; thus you will see that the further spread **d.e.** becomes larger and the closer spread **f.g.** becomes smaller, along with everything that falls within them. Just as I have sketched this here alongside.

<image-desc>A perspective diagram labeled with number 54. It shows a visual cone or pyramid originating from a point 'a' on the right (the eye). This cone projects onto a rectangle on the left defined by points 'c' and 'b'. Within the cone, two vertical lines are shown: a larger one labeled 'd' (top) and 'e' (bottom) further from the eye, and a smaller one labeled 'f' (top) and 'g' (bottom) closer to the eye.</image-desc>

Whatever is enclosed within the sight between the two **divergent lines** <term>gabel linien; literally "fork lines," referring to the lines forming the angle of the visual cone</term> **a. b. c.** and touches them—whether it be near or far, upright, diagonal, or curved—it all appears to the eye **a** as being of one size. As I have sketched this hereafter.

<image-desc>A perspective diagram labeled with number 55. It shows a visual cone originating from point 'a' on the right, extending to points 'c' (top) and 'b' (bottom) on the left. Inside this cone, three objects of different lengths and orientations (one vertical, one angled, one curved) are positioned such that they all touch the boundaries of the cone, illustrating how they would appear to be the same size from point 'a'.</image-desc>

Now I will set to work and take up the previously described **cube**, as it stands on its prepared plane, together with the light and the indicated shadow that it casts onto the **plan** <term>nider gedruckten grund; literally the "pressed-down ground," referring to the horizontal floor plan</term> and the **elevation** <term>auf gezognen grund; literally the "pulled-up ground," referring to the vertical side view</term>, as was sketched before. To show how the cube appears to the sight when viewed, I will demonstrate it in a drawing, and to do so, I will represent the sight as points and divide them, just as the single object before was divided into two—into a plan and an elevation. 

To mark the sight, I will draw eyes upon the established points. These points of the sight I place to one side in the drawing, as far from the two "grounds" <note>the plan and elevation views</note> as I wish, and draw a vertical line through them that is parallel to the side of the plan. Upon this line, I set a point of sight for the elevation, either high or low, according to how I wish to view the object; from this, I measure the height, lowness, and depth or distance. The other point of sight I set beneath the upper eye, also on the aforementioned vertical line, on whichever side of the plan I wish, or perhaps in the middle; from this, I measure all widths on both sides, far and near. 

Then I draw **rays of sight** <term>streim linien; projection lines extending from the viewer's eye to points on the object</term> from the point of the upper sight to all things that can be seen, which are marked with letters <note>original: "pustabne," an archaic spelling of "Buchstaben"</note> or numbers. The first ray of sight, which is set to the elevation, I draw to the point of light **o**. The second I draw down to the end of the circle of light. After that, I draw two lines above into the four corners of the cube; in front, two corners **2** and **3** fall together, and in the back, two corners **a** and **d** fall together. Then I draw two rays of sight into the two points **f** and **i**.

<summary>This page explains the geometry of the visual cone, showing how objects of different sizes can appear identical if they subtend the same angle, and begins the technical process of using plan and elevation points to project a cube into perspective.</summary>
<keywords>visual cone, rays of sight, perspective construction, plan and elevation, cube, optical geometry, vanishing point</keywords>

--- Page 171 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for constructing a perspective drawing, specifically focusing on how to draw "ray-lines" from the eye to a cube and its shadow, and how to intersect these rays with a picture plane.</meta>

But draw two lines upon the two locations of the transverse line where the cube stands; these signify a **square** <term>fierung; a four-sided figure or quadrangle</term>. For this reason, its two ends are labeled at the front as **g. f.** and at the back as **e. h.** In this way, the upright ground plan is correctly connected to the point of sight with **ray-lines** <term>streym linien; visual rays extending from the eye to the object</term>, just as it should be. 

Afterward, from the lower eye—which is positioned for the **flattened ground plan** <note>original: "nider gedruckten grund"; this refers to the plan view or bird’s-eye view, as if the 3D scene were pressed flat onto the paper</note>—draw ray-lines to all points of the flattened ground plan on its own plane.

First, proceed from the point of sight with ray-lines to the four corners of the plane **f. g. h. e.** Next, go to the four corners of the cube’s base, which then represent eight corners <note>representing both the top and bottom corners of the 3D cube projected onto the 2D surface</note>; because of this, they are labeled in front with **b2** and **c3**, and at the back with **d4** and **a1**. Then, draw three more ray-lines to the three corners **l. m. n.** of the cube's shadow. Now all the ray-lines for both ground plans have been drawn, as many as you need. 

Now, since the eye produces what it sees through these ray-lines, this must be transformed into a painting. This is accomplished through the previously mentioned **surfaces** <term>superficien; from the Latin "superficies," referring here to the picture plane on which the image is projected</term>. This is a transparent **plane** <term>planus; a flat surface</term> or level field that intersects all the ray-lines. In your drafting, you must represent this plane as a single line. 

Therefore, make this upright parallel line close to the ground plans, positioned between the eye and the objects, so that the things appear large, as mentioned before. Upon this line, the two points of sight must stand at right angles to the front painting. One could also, if necessary, lean this line backward or forward, but this line cuts off <note>or "intersects"</note> all the ray-lines. Afterward, draw two transverse lines from the points of sight at equal angles into the upright line of the surface, and at the points where they touch, draw two eyes as well. These four eyes now represent a single sight <note>original: "ein gesicht"; Dürer is describing a method of orthographic projection where multiple "points of sight" or viewpoints are used to help calculate the perspective correctly</note>, but this splitting brings ease to the work.

### Perspective Projection and Shadow Construction

<note>This page is primarily a technical diagram illustrating the principles of linear perspective. Due to significant ink bleed-through from the reverse side, some construction lines may appear faint or overlapping.</note>

->**Light Source**<-
<note>original: "Liecht"</note>

In the top diagram, the **Upright Cube** <note>original: "Cubus aufrecht"</note> is positioned vertically. Projection lines radiate from the light source, marked with a circle, through a specific point **o**. These rays target the corners of the square labeled **1, 2, 3,** and **4**. On the horizontal line below—the horizon—various points of construction are marked (**β, e, d, a, c, b, e, g, h, i, k**) to establish the depth and scale of the cube within the **perspective** <term>Perspektive; the art of representing three-dimensional objects on a two-dimensional surface</term>.

<column-break/>

->**The Cube on the Ground Plan**<-
<note>original: "Cubus im grund"</note>

The lower diagram demonstrates the projection of a **shadow** <note>original: "schatten"</note> from a cube resting on the base plane. 

1.  A second **Light Source** <note>original: "Liecht"</note> at the upper left directs a primary ray **p** toward the object.
2.  The cube's volume is defined by corners marked with numbers **1–4** and the Greek letters **α, β, γ, δ**.
3.  The resulting **shadow-outline** <term>Schattenriss; the silhouette cast by an object onto a surface</term> is mapped onto the ground at points **l, m,** and **n**.

By following this **geometry** <term>Geometrie</term>, the artist can precisely calculate how the shadow stretches or compresses based on the height and angle of the light, ensuring the **ground plan** <term>grund; the floor or base surface of the drawing</term> is perfectly aligned with the viewer's eye.

### The Geometry of Converging Lines

<note>This page is a technical illustration focusing on the core principles of linear perspective. It uses radiating lines to demonstrate how three-dimensional space is organized on a flat surface.</note>

->**Linear Perspective**<-
<term>Perspektive; the mathematical art of representing three-dimensional objects and spatial depth on a two-dimensional surface</term>

The diagram centers on the relationship between a vertical axis and the viewer's field of vision. On the right side of the page, a long vertical line serves as a primary guide. Two distinct **vanishing points** <term>Fluchtpunkt; a point in a perspective drawing to which parallel lines appear to converge</term> are marked upon this line, acting as the mathematical anchors for the composition.

<column-break/>

->**Radiating Projections**<-

From each of these focal points, a series of straight **lines** <note>original: "Linien"</note> radiate outward toward the left margin, demonstrating the laws of **geometry** <term>Geometrie</term>:

*   The upper set contains approximately ten lines, spaced to show a wide angle of projection.
*   The lower set is much denser, featuring about twenty lines at various angles to show more precise increments of spatial recession.

By using these radiating lines, the artist can determine the exact "height and angle" (as referenced on the previous page) required to draw objects that appear to recede naturally into the distance. This system ensures that every part of a drawing—whether a cube, a building, or a shadow—remains proportional to the viewer's eye.

<summary>This page illustrates the geometric foundation of perspective through a diagram of vanishing points and radiating lines. It provides the visual proof for how three-dimensional depth is calculated on a vertical axis.</summary>

<keywords>perspective, vanishing point, linear geometry, radiating lines, spatial projection, horizon line</keywords>

--- Page 174 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical explanation of geometric perspective, moving from the theoretical layout of lines to the practical application of plotting points from two-dimensional plans onto a three-dimensional perspective grid.</meta>

Now that you see the previously described method before your eyes and understand it, take another piece of paper and draw two cross-lines at right angles. In the center where they intersect, set the point of the eye. This single point now takes the place of the previous four eyes. All heights, lows, depths, and widths from both sides must be brought and placed at this point of the eye, as indicated by the previous radiating lines.

For this reason, take two <term>compasses</term><note>original: "cirkel"</note> for your measuring to avoid making errors. Use one for the elevation <note>original: "aufrechten grund," meaning the vertical side view</note> and the other for the plan view <note>original: "nider gedruckten [grund]," meaning the flat top-down view</note>.

Now, take the compass you wish to use for the elevation and place one foot on the previously made line of the <term>surface</term><note>original: "superficien," referring to the picture plane or the surface upon which the drawing is projected</note> at the eye belonging to the elevation. Place the other foot on the aforementioned line at the radiating line that is drawn from the further eye to the light point **o**, and keep this height.

Afterward, take the other compass and place it on the surface or <term>transparent line</term><note>original: "durchsichtige lini," another term for the picture plane</note> at the other eye belonging to the plan view. Place the other foot again on the transparent line at the radiating line drawn from the further lower eye into the light point **p**. Then, bring these two points together with the two compasses to your subsequent cross-lines. However high point **o** stands above the eye, the second compass will tell you how far point **p** stands to the side. These two points meet in a single point, which you should then mark with the two letters **o. p.** wherever you place it.

Do the same with all radiating lines that pass through the transparent line. Take all their heights and depths from the upper eye with the first compass, as mentioned before. Do the same with the other compass on the transparent line at the lower eye; take all the widths from the intersecting radiating lines according to how far they stand to the side of the eye. Then carry them all to the eye of your cross-lines. In this way, the two points taken from the transparent line—from both the eyes of the elevation and the plan view—will always fall into a single point, determining how high, low, or wide they should stand on a side. Always mark where they fall with their letters or numbers. And wherever I speak of the "transparent line," always understand it to mean the surface drawn nearest to the ground plans.

Afterward, join the plotted points together with straight lines, and you will see what results. From these things, you will learn where every corner of an object should stand, even those that cannot be seen by the eye; these are indicated here with <term>blind lines</term><note>original: "plintryssen," referring to dotted or dashed lines used to show hidden edges</note>. I have properly drawn this afterward upon its cross-line, but opposite that, I have drawn only the final result and indicated the shadows slightly with <term>hatching</term><note>original: "schraffirung"</note> to guide you. This is the correct basis of <term>perspective representation</term><note>original: "abstelens," the art of foreshortening or projecting 3D objects onto a 2D surface</note> as it pertains to painting.

<summary>This page explains the practical process of using compasses to transfer measurements from plan and elevation views onto a central coordinate system to create a perspective drawing. It introduces the use of "blind lines" for hidden edges and hatching for shadows.</summary>
<keywords>perspective, geometry, Albrecht Dürer, coordinate plotting, plan and elevation, picture plane, hatching, hidden edges</keywords>

--- Page 175 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for constructing geometric perspective, specifically focusing on a more direct method for foreshortening a square plane from a plan view into a pictorial space, following the previous discussion on using multiple "eyes" or viewpoints to determine depth.</meta>

Hereafter, I want to bring the previously described object, <term>foreshortened</term><note>original: "abgestollen," referring to the perspective compression of space</note>, into the drawing <note>original: "gemel"</note> using a different and more direct method. Proceed in this manner:

I lay a horizontal line <note>original: "vber zwerch ein lini"</note> across, of the same length as the previous **e, f, g, h** of the front elevation <note>original: "aufgezognen grundes"</note>. This line takes the place of a square plane. Then, place a "near eye" <note>original: "nahet aug," the primary viewpoint for the observer</note> on the side above the line, just as the eye point stood on the cross-lines in the previously described method.

Once that is done, I draw two straight lines from this eye to each end of the horizontal line **e, f, g, h**. These form two corners at the bottom; thus, three sides of the <term>square</term><note>original: "fierung"</note> are established, which I intend to foreshorten. Now, you must know how to construct the back side and determine how high it rises; it is done like this:

Place another eye on the side at the same distance used in the previously described plan, but at the same height as the "near eye." From this eye, draw two straight lines to both ends of the given line. Afterward, draw a vertical line <note>original: "aufrechte lini"</note> **aa-bb** that touches the front corner. At the point where this vertical line intersects the long <term>diagonal line</term><note>original: "streym lini," literally a "stripe line" or ray</note> drawn from the farther eye into the sharp angle, mark the point **cc**. 

From this point **cc**, draw a horizontal parallel line through the two diagonal lines that were drawn from the "near eye" to the two lower points of the square's horizontal line. Where these diagonal lines are intersected, they form two corners. In this way, the square plane is correctly <term>foreshortened</term><note>original: "abgestolen"</note>, just as the previous one was. Therefore, label its four corners with the four <term>letters</term><note>original: "pustabẽ" (Buchstaben)</note> **e, f, g, h**, as was done in the first example. I have <term>sketched</term><note>original: "aufgerissen"</note> this below before I proceed further.

The author explains the geometric construction of the four corners of a square on a receding ground plane.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for constructing geometric perspective. ->59<-

<image-desc>A perspective diagram showing the construction of a square plane (planus) on a ground surface. A horizon line is drawn with a central vanishing point depicted as an eye. Lines of sight (rays) converge from the eye to points on the ground line. The plane is labeled with letters including e, f, g, and h. The number 59 is centered above the diagram.</image-desc>

So now that this squared <term>plane</term><note>original Latin: "planus"</note> has been successfully <term>foreshortened</term><note>original: "abgestolen," a term used to describe the optical compression of space in perspective</note>, then place the **cube** upon it in its proper place, in the same way as it stood previously on its foreshortened plane. The way to do this is as follows:

Take the length of one side of the cube from the front <term>plan view</term><note>original: "nider gedruckten grund," literally the "pressed-down ground"</note> and mark it with two points **.x.y.** on the lower **horizontal line .f.g.** of your current foreshortened square. Place them at the same distance from corner **.f.** as they appear in the front plan view from the side of the squared plane **.e.f.** After that, draw two <term>ray lines</term><note>original: "streym lini," lines representing the path of light/sight to the eye</note> from the **eye** to the two points **.x.y.** Between these two lines, the cube must be placed with its bottom square surface toward the back of this plane. 

But to find how far back it goes, seek it thus: if you draw a **diagonal line .e.g.** in the front plan view, this diagonal will also be a diagonal in the plan view of the cube, for this line passes through its two corners **.1.a.** and **.3.c.** Now, since this is so, draw also a diagonal line **.e.g.** in your current foreshortened field. Where this intersects the two ray lines **.x.y.**, place an **.a.** on line **.x.** and a **.c.** on line **.y.** 

Afterward, draw two horizontal lines from the points **.a.c.** Where the horizontal line from **.a.** intersects the ray line **.y.**, place a **.d.** And where the horizontal line from **.c.** intersects the ray line **.x.**, place a **.b.** Thus, the squared plane at the bottom of the cube stands with its four corners correctly upon the squared, foreshortened plane **.e.f.g.h.**, just as in the other example before.

Now, from the four corners **.a.b.c.d.**, draw four vertical lines upward. Draw the front two as high as the horizontal line **.b.c.** is long. At that height, draw a horizontal line from one vertical line to the other, and mark the two corners above **.b.** with the number **.2.** and above **.c.** with the number **.3.** 

Afterward, draw two ray lines from the **eye** to the two corners **.2.3.** Where they then cut the vertical lines rising from **.a.d.**, place the number **.1.** above **.a.** and the number **.4.** above **.d.** Thus, the cube is correctly made, just as in the previous foreshortened ground. I have then drawn this out below. Before I go further.

<meta>The previous page was blank; this page begins the practical application of perspective to shadows.</meta>

NOW that the cube has been foreshortened <note>original: *abgestolen*, Dürer’s term for drawing an object in perspective</note> and constructed upon its foreshortened, squared field, you must then position the light and create the shadow of the cube upon its ground plane <note>original: *planum*</note>. To find this, proceed as follows.

Place the light source above the eye at whatever height you wish, on whichever side you prefer. Let this light source be point **o**; I will place it in the same location as previously described. Once this is positioned, draw a vertical line straight down from point **o**. Upon this line, you must place a point **p**, which represents the point directly beneath the light <note>This point **p** is the orthogonal projection of the light source onto the floor</note>. If I wish to place the light further away, I set point **o** higher on the vertical line. However, if I want the light to be closer, I move point **p** further down. But here, I will set it at approximately the same distance as it stood before.

Once these two points for the light, **o** and **p**, are established, draw straight <term>lines|streym lini; a ray or ruled line</term> from the light source **o** through the three upper corners of the cube—**2**, **3**, and **4**—and let these rays <note>original: *radios*</note> extend outward. Next, draw straight lines from point **p** through the three lower corners of the cube—**b**, **c**, and **d**. Where these three lines intersect the three upper rays from light source **o**, mark three points: **l**, **m**, and **n**. Then, connect with straight lines **b** to **l**, **l** to **m**, **m** to **n**, and **n** to **d**. Thus, the shadow of the cube is correctly constructed in its perspective, as described previously. To see this clearly, I have drafted everything here with its associated parts; in it, you will find the same principles as before.

I have also separately drafted and hatched <note>original: *geschaffirt*, referring to the technique of shading with parallel lines</note> only those parts which should remain for the final picture, so that it may be better understood.

--- Page 180 ---
<meta>The previous page concluded the geometrical construction of shadows for a cube; this page transitions into practical tools and mechanical methods for capturing perspective from life.</meta>

And in the same way that I have brought the cube into a foreshortened <note>original: *abgestolen*; Dürer’s specific term for an object rendered in perspective</note> drawing, so too can one bring all <term>bodies|corpora; solid three-dimensional shapes</term> that can be planned in ground-view and elevation into a drawing through such methods.

Next, I will teach how one can measure any object that one sees—provided it is not too far away—using three threads, and thereby bring it into a drawing.

### Another method I will teach: how to trace what one sees

If you wish to trace <note>original: *durchzeychnen*; literally "to draw through," referring to the act of tracing an image onto a transparent pane</note> what you see before you, prepare a clever device for it as follows.

Mount a clean, flat glass in a square frame. Then make a board as wide as the frame, but longer. Hinge the glass frame with two hinges and nail both parts to the other side of the joints on the board, so that the frame and the board can be opened and closed like a <term>game board|pret spil; likely referring to a folding board for backgammon or chess</term>. This allows the glass to be laid down against the board for safe-keeping whenever you wish.

Next, attach two small blocks <note>original: *klöblein*</note> in the middle of the board on both sides, fitted with iron rods that can pivot. Make these rods long enough to reach the glass frame when you lift it from the board at right angles. Flatten the ends of the rods and drill a hole through them, then place a small hook behind them that can rotate. Attach two small blocks to the glass frame at the points where the flat ends of the rods reach; when you place the rods against them and engage the hooks, the frame will stand firmly.

Afterward, make a squared, elongated piece of wood longer than the board is wide. Carve out a section from one side as wide as the board itself, so that the square wood extends past the board on both sides. Clamp this wood across the board within the cutout, so that it can be slid back and forth toward or away from the glass. Take this square wood lying on the board and hollow it out from the top along its length in a square shape, but not quite to the ends. Instead, drill two round holes through the two ends. Through these, push a long screw. However, this screw should not be threaded into the round holes themselves, but only in the space between them.

Now, make another piece of wood half as high as the frame. Fit this into the square groove of the cross-piece with a <term>tenon|zepf; a projecting piece of wood made to fit into a mortise</term>, so that the base of the upright wood rests upon the flat surface of the cross-piece. This allows the upright wood to be slid back and forth <term>transversely|zwerchs</term> at a <term>right angle|winckelrecht</term>, and

<summary>Dürer begins describing a mechanical drawing apparatus consisting of a hinged glass pane and a sliding sighting mechanism used to trace objects from life in perfect perspective.</summary>
<keywords>perspective apparatus, glass frame, tracing, technical drawing, Albrecht Dürer, measurement tools</keywords>

--- Page 181 ---
<meta>The previous page detailed the assembly of the frame and glass for a tracing device; this page explains the adjustable sight-piece and the procedure for drawing a portrait from life.</meta>

...drill a round hole into the bottom of the tenoned <note>original: *zepft*; wood prepared with a projection to fit into a joint</note> upright post and make a small screw-nut into which the previously mentioned long screw will fit. Then, insert the screw with its forward end into the round hole on one side of the crossbeam and thread it through the nut in the upright post until it comes out through the other hole. In this way, you can use this screw to move the upright post precisely back and forth to whichever side you desire.

Next, drill a round hole lengthwise through the center of the upright post and cut away the wood slightly on one side. On one side of this open slot, carve many small notches. Then, make a <term>lathed rod|gedreten stab; a wooden cylinder shaped on a lathe</term> that fits exactly into the drilled hole, and attach a small tooth <note>original: *zenlein*; a small peg or protrusion</note> at its base. Push the round rod into the aforementioned hole so that the tooth runs down into the open slot of the upright post. Every time you raise this round rod by one degree and wish to hold it steady, slide its tooth into a notch. In this manner, you can adjust this rod high or low.

At the top of this rod, attach a small, thin, well-crafted plate and drill a hole through it. This is so that you may look through it with one eye more steadily through the glass in the frame. Whatever you then see through the hole, trace onto the glass with a brush using <term>glazier's lead|glaser lot; a pigment or soft lead used for marking on glass</term>. Afterward, transfer that same drawing onto the surface upon which you wish to paint.

This method is good for all those who wish to <term>portray a likeness|ab Conterfeten; to create a portrait from life</term> and are not confident in their skill. If you wish to capture someone in this manner, have them lean their head against a support so they remain still and unmoved until you have made all the <term>essential lines|notstrich; the primary structural strokes of a drawing</term>. Once that is done, you may proceed with colors, but you must ensure you have a steady, unchanging light.

Furthermore, if you nail two cross-slats to the bottom of the aforementioned board where the glass is mounted, and drill two holes in each, and then insert the lathed rods into them—which have an iron pin at the bottom—and fit everything correctly together, you can conveniently use this apparatus as a table. All of this can be disassembled so that it is easy to carry and work with. I have sketched this below.

---

<image-desc>A faint, schematic line drawing of a perspective apparatus. It depicts a wooden base with two horizontal supports. An upright post is positioned in the center, through which a vertical rod is inserted. At the top of this rod is a small viewing plate with a peephole. --- Page 182 ---
<meta>The previous page concluded the description of a perspective device using a glass plate and a sliding eyepiece to trace portraits. This page introduces an alternative mechanical method using strings and a hinged panel to map three-dimensional objects, specifically a lute, onto a flat surface.</meta>

## Another Method.

By using three strings, you may bring any object that you can reach into a painting, to be recorded on a panel; do it this way. 

If you are in a hall, drive a large needle with a wide eye made for this purpose into a wall, and let this represent the <note>original: *aug*; the eye or the fixed viewpoint of the observer</note> eye. Through this, pull a strong thread and hang a lead weight on the end. Then, set a table or panel as far from the eye of the needle where the thread is as you wish; upon it, stand an upright frame crosswise against the eye of the needle—high or low on whichever side you prefer. This frame should have a <term>little door|türlein; a hinged wooden panel that acts as the drawing surface</term> that can be opened and closed; this little door shall be your panel upon which you wish to paint. 

Then, nail two strings, which are as long as the upright frame is high and wide, one at the top center of the frame and the other on one side, also in the center of the frame, and let them hang. Next, make a long iron pin that has a needle's eye at its very tip; thread the long string that was pulled through the needle's eye on the wall into it, and pass the needle and the long string through the frame and give it into someone else's hand, while you attend to the other two strings hanging on the frame. 

Now use it thus: lay a lute, or whatever else pleases you, as far from the frame as you wish, and let it remain unmoved as long as you need it. Have your <note>original: *gesellen*; assistant or apprentice</note> assistant extend the needle with the string out to the most necessary points of the lute. Every time he holds it still and pulls the long string taut, always cross the two strings on the frame straight over the long string, and stick them to the frame in both places with some wax. Then, tell your assistant to let his long string go slack. 

Afterward, swing the little door shut and mark that same point where the strings cross one another onto the panel. Then, open the little door again and do...

<summary>Albrecht Dürer describes a mechanical method for drawing in perspective using a system of weighted strings and a hinged "door" panel. This technique allows an artist to precisely map the coordinates of a 3D object, such as a lute, onto a 2D surface by finding the intersection of visual rays within a frame.</summary>
<keywords>Albrecht Dürer, perspective, drawing instrument, lute, geometry, projection, picture plane, Renaissance art technology</keywords>

<meta>This page concludes Dürer’s "Instruction in Measurement" (Underweysung der Messung), finishing the explanation of a mechanical perspective device and providing the author's closing remarks and copyright warning. It follows the description of using a string-and-frame apparatus to map a lute onto a flat surface.</meta>

...with another point, continuing in this way until you have marked the entire lute upon the panel. Then, join all the points that were produced upon the panel from the lute with lines; thus you will see what results from it. In this manner, you may also draw other objects. This method I have illustrated below.

*A famous woodcut illustration by Albrecht Dürer depicting a method for drawing a lute in perspective. Two men are working at a long table in a room with a window on the left. On the left side of the table, a man uses a stylus to point at a specific spot on a lute. A string is attached to the stylus and passes through a large wooden frame situated in the middle of the table, ending at a fixed point on the wall on the far right. The frame has a hinged door with a sheet of paper. The man on the right, seated on a stool, marks a point on the paper where the string passes through the opening. The background wall features Dürer's "AD" monogram and the year 1525.*

And with this, most gracious dear Lord <note>original: "günstiger lieber Herr"; Dürer likely addresses his patron and friend, the Nuremberg humanist Willibald Pirckheimer</note>: I wish to bring my writing to an end. If God grants me grace, I intend in time to bring to print the books I have written on <term>human proportion|menschlicher proporcion; the mathematical study of the relative sizes of parts of the human body</term> and other related matters. I also wish to warn everyone: should anyone dare to reprint this published booklet against my will, I shall reprint it myself and release it with more and larger additions than have been made now. Let every man take note of this.

--- Page 184 ---
<meta>This page serves as the "Errata" or correction list for the first edition of Albrecht Dürer's "Instruction in Measurement" (1525). It follows Dürer's closing remarks where he promised his next major work on human proportion. Here, he addresses the technical realities of the printing press—specifically how errors creep in when movable type is handled or when the author is in a rush.</meta>

Although I have applied all possible diligence to ensure that these booklets might be published correctly and well-edited, nevertheless, some errors in words and meaning have occurred through the pulling out and resetting of <term>type|pustaben; individual lead letters used in a printing press</term>, and also due to haste. I wish to indicate some of the most necessary of these here; any intelligent person will know how to correct the others themselves.

In B, on the fifth leaf, on the tenth line: read *sliced through* for *cut through*. <note>original: "durchschneyden / durchschnitten"</note>
In E, on the second leaf, on the second side, on the eighth line: read *equals* for *right*. <note>original: "rechten / gleychen"</note>
In E, on the fourth leaf, in the fifteenth figure: in the first circle, points *d* and *e* should be joined together with a line.
On the aforementioned leaf, the letters indicated by the text should also be added to the sixteenth figure.
In F, on the third leaf: the twenty-sixth figure is misplaced; the back part should stand at the front.
In F, on the fourth leaf, on the tenth line: for the last word, read *figures* for *bodies*. <note>original: "corporen / figuren"; "corporen" refers to three-dimensional geometric solids</note>
In J, on the second leaf, on the fourth line: leave out the words *one hundred and forty-one* and read *and sixty* instead.
In J, on the last leaf, on the thirteenth line: for the last word, read *other part* for *subdivide*. <note>original: "vntterteylen / ander teyl"</note>
In K, the second line: leave it unread, as it is wrongly placed and redundant.
In N, on the fifth leaf, under the 43rd figure, in the second line, it says *various*; instead, read *manifold*. <note>original: "manicherley / vilerley"</note>
In the aforementioned figure, the digit is also placed back-to-front.
In P ii, on the fifteenth line: at the beginning of the line, read *the distant measurement* for the two words *front-mentioned*. <note>original: "fornen gemels / den ferren gemess"</note>
In P, on the last leaf, on the second side, on the fifteenth line: for the two words *cut at*, read *cuts*. <note>original: "schneyd an / schneydet"</note>

-> <term>Imperial privilege|Keyserliche freyheit; a 16th-century form of copyright protection granted by the Holy Roman Emperor</term> shall be incorporated into the next booklet on <term>Proportion|Proporcion</term> which <-
-> I intend to print. <-

<meta>This page continues the legal and ecclesiastical arguments from the previous section, shifting from general archival metadata to specific citations of Roman law and a letter from Pope Gregory regarding a judicial dispute involving a bishop.</meta>

### Part XII
### Folio

...just as the aforementioned bishop is known to have requested; the bishop held those of an outside council <note>original: "alieni consilii" - refers to a legal body outside his own jurisdiction</note> as entirely suspect. Because a judgment not delivered by one’s own judge <note>original: "non a suo iudice" - a fundamental principle that a person must be tried by their rightful superior</note> holds no legal validity, as the text of this reading shows in Book 7, Title 47, Constitution 4: The Emperors Gratian, Valentinian, and Theodosius, Augusti, to Potitus the Vicar: In private cases of this kind, no litigants should be bound by a sentence not delivered by their own judge. Given on the 11th day before the Kalends of October at Rome, Ausinus and Olibrius being consuls. Furthermore, regarding what he says about being accused by his own servants—who ought by no means to have been heard—this constitution makes it clear, saying thus in Title 1, Book 9, Constitution 20: The Emperors Arcadius and Honorius, Augusti, to Antichianus: If anyone from the household or from the servants of any house emerges as an informer and accuser of any crime, let him be struck with the avenging sword at the very start of the charges and the beginning of the excuse, before the presentation of witnesses and before the examination of the judges, as he is about to destroy the reputation, life, and fortunes of the one to whose household or lordship he belonged. For it is necessary that the fatal voice be silenced before it is heard. However, we make an exception for the crime of treason <term>maiestatis crimen</term> <note>original: "maiestatis aut crimen excipimus" - under Roman law, the only time a slave's testimony against a master was permitted was in cases of treason against the Emperor</note>. Given on the 5th day before the Ides of November, Atticus and Caesarius being consuls. If it was said that he was accused of something pertaining to the crime of treason, even then he was not to be believed by God if his life or reputation was not as it is read in Book 48, Title: "According to the Julian Law": Modestinus, in Book 12 of the Pandects <note>The Pandects, or Digest, was a massive compilation of legal opinions by Roman jurists</note>, shortly after: "I believe, however, that judges should not hold this in reverence on the occasion of the treason of the prince, but in truth. For both the person must be examined—whether he could have done it—and what he did, and whether he thought it." And what does the same bishop say? Because in his absence some very worthless witnesses were produced; if this is true, it must be known by law to be of no importance. The New Constitution <term>constitutio nouella</term> <note>The "Novellae" were the new laws issued by Emperor Justinian after his initial Code</note> which speaks of witnesses in Chapter 16: "We have also frequently recognized this: that people entering before the defenders of places <term>defensores</term> or before the most distinguished judges of the provinces, or even as is customary here before the most distinguished Master of the Census, and seeking as if anything could be done against the laws, or otherwise sustaining injustice or being damaged, wish to produce witnesses. And lest it be later objected to them that the acts were completed by only one party, it is necessary that the judge or defender be advised to come and hear the witnesses when the person is present in that same city. But if he is willing to come but treats it with contempt, let the testimony against him be useless from one side. We ordain that testimony of this kind shall only be valid as if it had been made by one party if he had indeed been present. For he rejected the facts and refused to come and hear what was being deposed, since it was certainly in public and not..."
